# How The Process Differs By What You Are Making

You do not shoot an animation. The pipeline for one has no principal photography in it at all — no shooting day, no call sheet, no locations, no weather — and records its actors first rather than last.

This is what actually changes, kind by kind, and it changes far more than the names.

## What is in here

12 kinds of production. For each: **the unit of work** — the atom progress is actually counted in, which differs more than anything else — then what is **absent** from the live-action pipeline, what is **reordered**, the processes that exist only here, how the crew differs, the shape of the money, and what goes wrong.

The unit of work is the load-bearing one. A live-action feature counts shooting days. A CG feature counts approved seconds. A documentary counts field days against cut weeks. An engine that assumes one of them cannot hold the others.

### The kinds

1. **CG animation**
2. **2D and hand-drawn animation**
3. **Stop-motion and puppet animation (feature and series): armature puppets, replacement/3D-printed faces, clay, miniature sets, frame-by-frame photography**
4. **Documentary (all modes: observational/verite, participatory, essay, archival/compilation, natural history and wildlife, true crime and investigative, biographical)**
5. **Unscripted and reality television**
6. **Multi-camera sitcom shot before a live studio audience**
7. **Live and broadcast television**
8. **Commercials and branded content (advertising film: TV/cinema spots, digital and social films, brand content, product/tabletop work)**
9. **VFX-led tentpole and virtual production**
10. **Micro-budget and independent narrative feature (roughly $0–$300k "ultra low", $300k–$2M "low budget"; non-union or SAG-AFTRA-only, no completion bond, no distributor attached at the start)**
11. **Returning scripted television series as a machine**
12. **Music video, short film and short form**

---

## 1. CG animation

*feature and episodic series (3D computer-generated, Maya/Houdini/Nuke/USD-era studio pipeline)*

### The unit of work

The atom is the SHOT; the currency is the APPROVED SECOND. Nothing is measured in pages, setups or shooting days, because nothing is shot. A 100-minute feature is 144,000 frames at 24fps and, typically, 1,200-2,000 shots (est.); an 11-minute series episode is 150-250 shots. Every shot carries a stage (board / rough layout / final layout / blocking / animation final / CFX / FX / lit / comped / final) and a round number, and progress is read off the story reel as "percent of runtime at or beyond stage X" — the reel is the film from month three and the only honest completion metric.

Throughput is quota-driven, per department, per artist-week, and the quotas are the plan's load-bearing numbers. Feature character animation runs 3-5 seconds per animator per week (a hard, well-attested industry figure); a mid-budget 12-minute CG kids' series budgets ~6 seconds of animation per animator per DAY, ~13 seconds of layout per artist per day and ~4 comped shots per artist per day (CG-Wire's 52x12min model). That 5-10x spread between feature and series is the whole economic difference between the two forms. Lighting runs 1-4 days per shot on a feature and many shots per day on a series; CFX 0.5-3 days per shot; hero FX shots 1-6 weeks.

Secondary units the engine must also carry: ROUNDS (a shot approved in 3 rounds is cheap, in 12 rounds is a disaster; rounds-per-approval is the real measure of a director's cost), RETAKES (open notes burning down), FINALS PER WEEK in the last 9 months (the burn-down that predicts delivery), CORE-HOURS on the farm, and TERABYTES of cache.

A good day, feature: an animator gets one 4-second shot approved at round 4 and blocks the next; a lighting artist finals two shots; CFX clears eight sims with two kickbacks; the farm returns 96% of submitted frames before the 9am dailies; editorial cuts 90 seconds of newly-lit footage into the reel and the sequence still plays. A good day, series: 4-6 minutes of layout leaves the building, one episode's animation closes, the vendor delivers a block with under 15% retakes, and nothing in the pipeline stalled behind a missing asset.

A bad day has the same shape inverted: the farm crashed at 04:00 and there are no frames to review, so 60 artists have nothing to show and a day of the schedule — crew size x one day — is simply gone.

### What is absent

- PRINCIPAL PHOTOGRAPHY. There is no shooting day, no call sheet, no company move, no shooting ratio, no coverage, no slate, no take numbers on a set. The single most load-bearing structure of the live-action pipeline does not exist. Work does not happen on days, it happens on shots, in parallel, for years.
- LOCATIONS. No scouting, no permits, no location fees, no basecamp, no unit moves, no neighbourhood sound holds, no location manager. Any place that exists must instead be BUILT, which is a modelling cost, not a booking cost.
- WEATHER AND DAYLIGHT. There is no golden hour, no weather cover, no losing the light, no daylight-hours constraint on the day. Time of day is a lighting parameter set months after the performance.
- PHYSICAL CAMERA DEPARTMENT. No lens package, no camera test, no focus pull, no mag loads, no sensor, no DIT, no camera rental. Focal length, sensor size, DOF and shake are numeric attributes edited after the acting is finished.
- GRIP AND ELECTRIC. No gaffer, no key grip, no dolly track, no condors, no gennies, no rigging days, no pre-light. Lighting is a file, and can be redone at 2am by one person.
- PRODUCTION SOUND. No mixer, no boom, no lavs, no wild track, no room tone, no sound holds. There is no production track at all: every single sound in the finished film is created from nothing.
- ADR AS A NOISE FIX. Dialogue is already clean studio audio. The only re-record is for CHANGED LINES, which is a story problem, not an acoustic one.
- WARDROBE, HAIR AND MAKEUP AS PHYSICAL CRAFTS. No fittings, no continuity stills, no makeup trailer, no time-in-the-chair before call. Their equivalents (groom, costume/cloth setup) are asset builds done once, then simulated per shot.
- SET CONSTRUCTION AND STRIKE. Nothing is built out of material, nothing is struck, nothing costs money to store or to stand idle. A set built once can be redressed infinitely and reused across 52 episodes at zero marginal build cost.
- STUNTS, PICTURE CARS, ANIMALS, ARMOURERS, PRACTICAL SFX. No physical risk, no safety meeting, no animal handler, no insurance-driven restriction on what can be staged. Anything can be staged; the constraint is artist-weeks and render hours, not physics or liability.
- COVERAGE AND SELECTION. Nothing is shot from multiple angles so it can be chosen later. There is exactly one camera per shot and it is authored. Editorial cannot save a scene with a better angle, because no other angle was rendered.
- EDITORIAL ASSEMBLY FROM RUSHES. There is no bin of takes to assemble. Editorial builds the cut before any imagery exists and then swaps versions into it.
- THE RESHOOT AS AN EVENT. There is no discrete additional-photography block requiring cast and crew recalled. Every shot is infinitely re-doable at all times, which sounds like freedom and is actually the central discipline problem of the form.
- CAST DAY-OUT-OF-DAYS AND SCHEDULE-AROUND-TALENT. Actors never appear together, never need to be in the same room, never need continuity of look, and can record 20 months apart. Their availability constrains a booth day, not a $400k shoot day.
- MEAL PENALTIES, TURNAROUND AND FRENCH HOURS as scheduling forces on the shoot. Crew is salaried by the week under Animation Guild / equivalent terms, not by the shooting day.
- THE LAB, THE NEGATIVE, TELECINE, FILM-OUT FOR CAPTURE. The image is born digital and never leaves the digital domain.
- COMPLETION BOND LOGIC BUILT AROUND WEATHER AND ACTOR ILLNESS. The catastrophic risks here are story failure, render capacity and rig breakage, none of which a bond company knows how to police.

### What is reordered

- VOICE IS RECORDED FIRST, NOT LAST. In live action the performance is captured on set and post fixes it; here the actor is at the head of the chain. Scratch dialogue by story artists is cut into the reel within weeks of boarding; final cast records 18-30 months before release, alone in a booth, to picture that does not exist. Animation is then PERFORMED TO THAT FIXED TRACK — the animator is the actor's puppeteer, matching phoneme timing, breath and comic rhythm already committed. Consequence: a dialogue change is not a pickup, it is the invalidation of every shot animated to the old take.
- THE EDIT PRECEDES THE IMAGERY. Editorial is staffed on day one, not at wrap. The story reel exists for the entire life of the production and every stage replaces panels with progressively finished shots inside the SAME cut. The film is edited for four years and shot never.
- THE SCRIPT IS REWRITTEN AFTER 'SHOOTING' BEGINS. Sequences are reboarded, whole acts thrown out and rebuilt after assets have been modelled and rigged for them, sometimes after animation has started. In live action that is a catastrophe; here it is the normal method (Pixar-style Brain Trust screenings every 8-12 weeks).
- CAMERA COMES AFTER PERFORMANCE INTENT BUT REMAINS EDITABLE AFTER PERFORMANCE. Rough layout sets the camera on proxy characters; final layout re-frames and re-lenses AFTER the acting is approved. The equivalent of moving the camera after the actors have gone home.
- LIGHTING IS NEAR THE END, AND IS RE-DOABLE. The DP-lighting equivalent lights the film after every performance is finished and locked, sequence by sequence, and can relight a whole act overnight. In live action the light is baked into the negative at the moment of performance.
- DESIGN AND CONSTRUCTION RUN THROUGH 'PRODUCTION'. Sets and characters are built just-in-time, sequence by sequence, against launch dates — not completed before a shoot starts. Asset build overlaps shot production for 12+ months.
- SPOTTING AND SCORE ARE CONTINUOUS, NOT POST. Temp music is in the reel from month three; the composer writes against reels for the better part of a year. SONGS ARE LOCKED UPSTREAM OF ANIMATION, because characters must be animated to the final vocal.
- SOUND DESIGN IS APPLIED TO UNFINISHED PICTURE REPEATEDLY, from the first reel screening onward, rather than once after picture lock.
- PICTURE LOCK IS PARTIAL AND ROLLING. Sequences lock individually over 6-9 months; there is no single lock date for the film until very late, and a shot's length can change while three departments are working on it.
- CASTING CAN FOLLOW BOARDING. The character is often proved on the reel with a scratch voice before an actor is hired, and is sometimes redesigned toward the actor who is eventually cast.
- MARKETING PULLS SHOTS TO FINAL OUT OF ORDER. A teaser 12-18 months out forces specific shots through the entire pipeline — assets, rig, sim, lighting, comp, grade — while the rest of the film is still storyboards.
- POST-PRODUCTION AS A PHASE DOES NOT EXIST; IT IS ABSORBED INTO PRODUCTION. Compositing, colour and 'VFX' are production departments running concurrently with animation. What remains at the end is only mix, grade, deliverables and QC.

### How the crew differs

- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — STORY DEPARTMENT: Head of Story and Story Artists, who rewrite the film by drawing it and pitch their sequences aloud to the director. There is no live-action equivalent; they are simultaneously writers, directors and cinematographers of a version of the film that gets thrown away.
- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — EDITOR FROM DAY ONE: the Editor and Assistant Editors are hired before any imagery exists and cut a film made of drawings for four years. In live action the editor waits for rushes.
- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — THE SPLIT DP: many CG features carry two directors of photography, one for CAMERA (layout, framing, lensing, movement) and one for LIGHTING (light, shadow, exposure, atmosphere). Live action fuses these into one person because they happen in the same instant; here they are eighteen months apart.
- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — TECHNICAL DIRECTORS as a whole class: Character TD (rigger), Facial Rigger, Shading TD, Lighting TD, FX TD, Crowd TD, Pipeline TD, Render TD, Supervising Technical Director. Half the crew are engineers who are also artists; nothing in live action maps to them.
- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — ASSET-SIDE ARTISTS: Character Modeler, Set Modeler, Texture/Surfacing Artist, Look Development Artist, Groom Artist, Matte Painter. They build the world once and are gone before it is filmed.
- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — SIMULATION ARTISTS: Character Effects (CFX) Artist for cloth and hair, FX Artist for water, fire and destruction. Their work is a physics experiment that runs overnight and may fail.
- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — RENDER WRANGLER and SYSTEMS/IT at scale: people whose entire job is to keep a supercomputer producing frames before the morning review. A feature studio's IT department is a production department, not overhead.
- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — VISUAL DEVELOPMENT ARTIST and COLOUR SCRIPT ARTIST: the film is painted before it is designed and designed before it is built.
- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — ANIMATOR AS PERFORMER: the animator is the acting talent. Twenty to eighty of them each perform the same character, and holding one performance across dozens of hands is the Animation Supervisor's / Directing Animator's whole job. Live action has no analogue for 'the star is played by forty people'.
- PRESENT AND UNIQUE — DEDICATED PER-DEPARTMENT PRODUCTION COORDINATORS AND MANAGERS: an animation production office runs 8-15 parallel department pipelines simultaneously rather than one shooting unit.
- ABSENT — CAMERA CREW: 1st AC/focus puller, 2nd AC/clapper loader, camera operator, Steadicam operator, DIT, digital loader.
- ABSENT — GRIP AND ELECTRIC: gaffer, best boy electric, key grip, best boy grip, dolly grip, rigging gaffer, generator operator. The single largest crew block on a live-action set has no counterpart at all.
- ABSENT — SOUND ON SET: production sound mixer, boom operator, utility sound. Nothing is recorded in a real space.
- ABSENT — THE AD DEPARTMENT: 1st AD, 2nd AD, 2nd 2nd AD, set PAs. The schedule is run by Production Managers and Coordinators against a dependency graph, not by a person calling the day.
- ABSENT — SCRIPT SUPERVISOR: continuity is enforced by version control and the reel, not by a person with a stopwatch and notes.
- ABSENT — LOCATIONS AND TRANSPORT: location manager, scouts, unit managers, transport captain, drivers, picture-car coordinator.
- ABSENT — PHYSICAL CRAFT DEPARTMENTS: costume supervisor, cutter/fitter, set costumer, key hair, key makeup, prosthetics, set decorator, construction coordinator, greensman, propmaster on set, standby painter.
- ABSENT — RISK DEPARTMENTS: stunt coordinator, stunt performers, armorer, special effects (practical) supervisor, safety officer, animal wrangler, medic, marine coordinator.
- ABSENT — BACKGROUND CASTING AND EXTRAS WRANGLING: replaced entirely by a Crowd TD and an agent library.
- ABSENT — CATERING AND CRAFT SERVICE AT SHOOT SCALE, and the whole per-diem/travel/hotel apparatus. The crew comes to the same building for four years.
- MOVED — CASTING DIRECTOR exists but casts voices only, and is often as much a marketing hire as a performance one.
- MOVED — PRODUCTION DESIGNER exists but designs a world that will be built as data by fifty other people, and stays involved for the full production rather than finishing before the shoot.

### What goes wrong

- STORY REBOOT AFTER PRODUCTION HAS STARTED. A screening fails, an act is thrown out and reboarded, and the assets, layouts and finished animation built for it are simply deleted. Months of finished work with no salvage value. This is the form's defining catastrophe and it is a normal event, not a rare one.
- RIG REPUBLISH INVALIDATES IN-PROGRESS ANIMATION. A fix to a hero character's rig or facial shapes lands while forty animators have that character in flight; their work either has to be transferred, re-keyed or lost, and every shot already approved may need re-running through CFX.
- ASSET RE-APPROVAL CASCADE. A character redesign after modelling forces re-model, re-texture, re-lookdev, re-groom, re-rig, re-tailor and re-sim on every shot already completed. One design note can cost more than a whole sequence.
- RENDER TIMES BLOW PAST THE ESTIMATE. A shader, a light or a groom change multiplies minutes per frame; suddenly the show needs more core-hours than exist between now and delivery. Money cannot fix this quickly — farm capacity is ordered months ahead and cloud burst is a punishing multiple.
- FARM QUEUE STARVATION. FX simulations eat the farm overnight and lighting has no frames for the morning review, so sixty artists have nothing to show and a crew-day evaporates. Priority wars between departments are a daily political fact.
- FRAMES THAT FAIL AT 90%. Out-of-memory kills, licence server outages, a bad node poisoning a chunk, a job that renders 1,400 of 1,500 frames and must be requeued into a window that has closed.
- STORAGE AND IO EXHAUSTION. A sequence of multi-terabyte FX caches fills the filer; renders and sims fail mid-run; purged caches must be re-simulated from scratch at full cost because sims are not cheap to reproduce.
- NON-DETERMINISTIC SIMULATION. A cloth or hair cache that was perfect at version 3 explodes after a two-frame animation tweak, and re-running it does not reproduce the earlier result. There is no equivalent of 'just print the take we liked.'
- INTERPENETRATION AND POKE-THROUGH. Hands through cloth, hair through shoulders, feet through floor, props through hands — invisible in blocking, catastrophic at finals, and only discoverable once simulation runs.
- EVERYTHING IS VISIBLE AND NOTHING IS ACCIDENTAL. A live-action background is simply there; here every pixel is authored, so an unfinished background is UNFINISHED, and there is no lucky texture, no happy accident, no soft focus that costs nothing.
- SOFTWARE AND RENDERER VERSION DRIFT. A DCC or renderer upgrade mid-show shifts the look; freeze it and you carry bugs for four years, upgrade it and you may owe a full re-render of everything already final.
- COLOUR PIPELINE AND MISSING AOVS. An OCIO/ACES config change, a wrong colour space on a texture set, or a pass comp needs that lighting did not output — all of which resolve to re-rendering frames that were already finished.
- SAMPLING NOISE, FIREFLIES AND DENOISER ARTIFACTS that are invisible on a desk monitor and glaring on a 60-foot calibrated screen at the finals review, with the farm already fully committed.
- FLICKER AND TEMPORAL INSTABILITY across a sequence — in fur, in volumes, in denoising — that only exists in motion and therefore only appears after the whole sequence has been rendered.
- LOOK DRIFT ACROSS ARTISTS. A character lit by nine different lighters across four sequences slowly stops being the same character; a hero's fur reads warm in act one and grey in act three. Continuity here is a management problem, not a wardrobe department.
- PERFORMANCE DRIFT ACROSS ANIMATORS. Forty people playing one character, each with their own instincts; without a strong animation supervisor the character's acting subtly changes personality between sequences.
- QUOTA MIS-SET AT THE PLAN. If the assumed seconds-per-week is wrong, the error compounds silently for six months and cannot be recovered by overtime, because trained artists are the constraint and hiring takes a quarter.
- CREW RAMP FAILURE. Standing up 150-300 artists on a fixed date, in a tax-credit jurisdiction, with work permits and lead times, against every other show hiring the same people in the same city, in the same quarter.
- ROUND INFLATION. Because everything is infinitely re-doable, notes never stop. A shot in animation round 11 is not a better shot, it is a management failure, and the whole schedule dies of a thousand small improvements.
- VOICE TALENT LOSS OR CHANGE mid-production: an actor dies, becomes unavailable, or becomes unusable, after two years of animation performed to their reads. Every affected shot needs re-recorded lines and re-animated faces.
- LINE CHANGES AFTER ANIMATION. A single rewritten sentence invalidates lip sync, body rhythm and the shot's length, and cascades into layout, CFX, lighting, comp and the cut.
- LOCALISATION MISMATCH. Mouths animated to English make 30 dubbed versions look wrong; re-animating faces per language is unaffordable, so quality is traded away in the studio's largest overseas markets.
- VENDOR DIVERGENCE IN EPISODIC. A partner studio delivers off-model characters or mismatched lighting on a whole block; retaking it costs more than the outsourcing saved and the broadcast date does not move.
- PIPELINE DEADLOCK. Layout cannot start without sets, sets cannot finish without design approval, design is waiting on a story note, and the sequence launch date is contractual. The dependency graph, not the calendar, kills the show.
- THE LENGTH THAT WILL NOT COME DOWN. Runtime is decided in the reel, but every second cut late is a second of finished, finaled, fully paid-for animation thrown in the bin — the most expensive editing decision in any medium.
- LATE FORMAT DEMANDS. Stereo, IMAX, HDR or a higher resolution deliverable requested after lighting is finished, each of which can mean re-rendering the film with the farm already saturated.
- THE REEL IS THE TRUTH AND THE TRUTH IS PUBLIC. If the story does not play in boards, no amount of beautiful rendering saves it — and the failure is discovered in a screening room in front of the people who can cancel the film, up to eight times before release.

### The shape of the money

Labour is essentially the entire budget, and the budget is therefore just CREW SIZE x WEEKS. There is no physical production spend of any kind — no locations, sets, camera or lighting rental, stock, travel, transport, catering-at-scale or stage rent — so 70-85% of a CG feature's below-the-line is artist and engineer salaries (est.). This is the single deepest structural difference: you cannot buy speed with money the way live action can (more units, more crew, night shoots), because the constraint is trained artists who do not exist in the market and take months to hire and ramp.

Rough proportional shape of a $150-200M studio CG feature (estimates, and studio accounting differs wildly — Pixar's quoted budgets include company overhead that other studios exclude, which is why its numbers look high):

- Story/development, boarding, editorial and screenings: 10-20% of schedule and a meaningful cost share, because it runs for years at low headcount. Live action would call almost all of this "free."

- Asset build (modelling, texturing, lookdev, groom, rigging): 15-25%.

- Shot production (layout, animation, CFX, FX, lighting, comp): 45-60% — animation and lighting are the two biggest single departments.

- Compute: render farm capex/opex, storage, and software licences (Maya, Houdini, Nuke, Katana, RenderMan/Arnold, Deadline, ShotGrid) — roughly 5-12% of a feature (est.), higher on effects-heavy or high-fidelity shows, and often carried as studio overhead rather than a picture cost.

- Voice cast: usually LOW SINGLE DIGIT PERCENT, even with stars. A lead records 8-25 four-hour sessions across three years; SAG-AFTRA animation session scale is roughly $1,000-1,300 per session (est.), with stars on negotiated flat fees. Compare live action, where a single lead's quote can be 10-20% of the whole negative cost. Talent is cheap here and artists are expensive — the exact inverse of live action.

- Music, sound and mix: 3-6%.

- Post as a separate phase barely exists; comp, colour and effects are production line items.

The number that governs everything is COST PER FINISHED MINUTE, and its range across this one form is enormous:

- Studio CG feature: ~$150-200M for ~100 minutes = roughly $1.5-2M per finished minute.

- Premium streaming CG series: roughly $1.5-4M per 22-25 minute episode (est.) = ~$70-180k per minute.

- Mid-budget European CG kids' series: a published model for 52 x 12 minutes comes to EUR 4.12M total, about EUR 79k per episode, i.e. roughly EUR 6,600 per finished minute — with EUR 2.15M of that in the production stage (layout, animation, rendering, comp), EUR 449k in conception/scripts, EUR 325k in board and animatic, and EUR 444k in post.

That is a 200-300x spread per minute for the same craft, and the entire feature/series distinction lives in it: features buy rounds (a shot may go 8-15 rounds), series buy throughput (a shot goes 1-3 rounds and reuses everything).

Other money facts particular to this form:

- Fixed burn. Once 300 people are on payroll, every week of schedule slip has an exact, predictable, unavoidable price. There is no way to "wrap early."

- Tax credits dictate GEOGRAPHY of the crew (Canada, UK, France, Ireland, Spain, Australia), so the budget is often assembled around where the labour rebate is, not where the director is.

- Series economics are amortisation economics: the launch asset library is a capital cost recovered over 13-52 episodes, so season two is dramatically cheaper per minute than season one, and a script demanding a new set is a budget event.

- Marketing spend for a wide-release animated feature typically equals or exceeds the negative cost (P&A $100-150M+ for a major title, est.), and animated features earn a larger share of revenue internationally and from consumer products than comparable live action, which is why merchandisable design is a budget input, not an afterthought.

- Compute is the one line that behaves like a physical constraint: you cannot hire more of it on Friday for Monday, on-prem capacity is ordered 6-12 months ahead, and cloud burst costs a large multiple of amortised on-prem per core-hour.

### Processes that exist only here

#### Development & Greenlight Package

Pitches, treatments, optioned IP and comp titles assembled into a package: logline, beat outline, key art, budget band, target runtime and a release slot two to five years out.

- **Who.** Head of Development / Development Executive
- **Decides.** Which projects enter development and at what budget band; original vs optioned IP; who directs and who produces; which release quarter to claim; when to kill a project.
- **Engine.** Slate model: per-project development burn, an audience/genre vector, a studio release calendar with contested slots, a director/writer availability market, and option contracts with expiry and cost. Most projects must die here.
- **Takes.** 6-24 months per project; a studio kills 3-10 for every greenlight

#### Animation Screenplay Draft

A script written in the knowledge that it will be dismantled: dialogue and structure that the story department will reboard. Rewrites continue for years; writers are frequently replaced or supplemented by the board room.

- **Who.** Screenwriter (animation) / Story Consultant
- **Decides.** Structure and act breaks, character voice, ratio of dialogue to visual comedy, how many writers, whether to run script-first or board-first.
- **Engine.** Script object of beats/scenes carrying tone, dialogue density, asset and set implications, and a boardability score. Edits must propagate into board demand, asset demand and estimated shot count.
- **Takes.** 12-20 weeks per draft; 5-15 drafts across a feature

#### Visual Development & Colour Script

Paintings, shape-language studies, colour keys and a colour script that maps emotional temperature across the entire runtime before a single thing is built. It is the lighting brief written years early.

- **Who.** Visual Development Artist / Production Designer / Art Director
- **Decides.** Art direction and stylisation level (photoreal to graphic), palette per sequence, which sequences get the money shots, how far the design may depart from realism.
- **Engine.** A style vector per show (shape language, palette, render style) that constrains every downstream asset and applies a cost multiplier; the colour script stored as per-sequence lighting intent that lighting is later scored against.
- **Takes.** 6-18 months, overlapping story; colour script revised with every reel version

#### Character Design & Model Pack

Turnarounds, expression sheets, proportion and phoneme charts, colour callouts. The pack is the binding contract between design and modelling, and in series between the studio and its vendors.

- **Who.** Character Designer / Character Art Director
- **Decides.** Silhouette and appeal, complexity (hair, cloth, props, accessories), cast size, how much topology and rig logic is shared between characters, hero vs background tiers.
- **Engine.** Character asset carrying complexity attributes that drive model, groom, rig, CFX and per-frame render cost; an approval state; and a reuse graph feeding crowds and later episodes.
- **Takes.** 4-12 weeks per hero character, iterated for months

#### Storyboarding

Sequences drawn as panels with staging, camera and acting intent, then pitched aloud to the director. Boards, not the script, are the film's first real version and the primary rewriting instrument.

- **Who.** Story Artist / Head of Story
- **Decides.** Which artist boards which sequence, panel count and density, staging and camera intent, how far to depart from the script, what to pitch and what to hide.
- **Engine.** Sequence objects with panel counts, artist skill and tone-fit, and revision history; a board version drives reel content, estimated shot count and asset demand. Artists have comedy/drama/action affinities that change outcomes.
- **Takes.** 4-8 weeks per sequence per artist; sequences are reboarded 3-10+ times

#### Scratch Voice Record

Temporary dialogue read by story artists and in-house staff, recorded cheaply and cut into the reel so timing, comedy and clarity can be judged years before the cast exists.

- **Who.** Story Artist / Editorial / Voice Director
- **Decides.** Whether to cut with scratch or wait for cast; who reads; whether a scratch performance the director now prefers should be preserved or the star's take used instead.
- **Engine.** Audio takes with a performance quality separate from final-cast quality; the reel's read of a joke depends on the take used, so the scratch-to-final swap can measurably improve or destroy a scene.
- **Takes.** Hours per session, continuously through boarding

#### Story Reel / Animatic Assembly

Boards cut to scratch dialogue, temp music and temp sound with real timing. From month three this IS the film; every later stage replaces panels with shots inside the same cut, and the reel never stops existing.

- **Who.** Editor / First Assistant Editor
- **Decides.** Cut timing and rhythm, which panels survive, where to trim, what the current runtime is, which sequences go into the next screening and in what state.
- **Engine.** The reel as a durable timeline: every shot has length, current stage, current version and a source department. Runtime, cost-to-complete and percent-complete are all read off it. This object is the spine of the whole simulation.
- **Takes.** Continuous for 3-5 years; a new full reel every 8-12 weeks

#### Screening & Notes (Brain Trust / Sweatbox)

The whole reel screened for peers and executives; notes come back as sequences to reboard. Failure here can trigger a story reboot years in, after assets are built and animation has begun.

- **Who.** Director, Creative Leads, Studio Executives
- **Decides.** What to accept, defend or cut; whether to reboot an act; whether to replace the writer or the director; whether to move the release date; what to tell the studio.
- **Engine.** A screening event that scores each sequence against audience and critic models, emits weighted notes with cost estimates, and can invalidate downstream work — orphaning assets and throwing away finished shots. Director credibility is a depleting resource.
- **Takes.** One day, every 8-12 weeks; 8-10 across a feature

#### Voice Casting

Auditions, offers and deals for the final cast, often after boards have proved the character. Star casting is a marketing decision as much as a performance one, and the actor may reshape the character.

- **Who.** Casting Director (Voice) / Director / Studio
- **Decides.** Star vs character actor vs in-house talent; number of booth days; fee structure (session scale vs flat fee vs backend); whether to redesign the character toward the actor.
- **Engine.** A voice talent market with attributes (range, comic timing, warmth, recognisability), quotes, and availability windows across a multi-year schedule; per-session cost (SAG-AFTRA animation session rates roughly $1,000-1,300 per four hours, est.) plus star flat deals orders of magnitude above.
- **Takes.** 4-16 weeks; first record typically 18-30 months before release

#### Final Voice Record Session

The actor records alone in a booth against reel playback: many takes per line, alts, wild lines, efforts and breaths. Sessions repeat as the script changes. This audio becomes the timing spine of all animation.

- **Who.** Voice Director / Recording Engineer / Dialogue Editor
- **Decides.** Which take is the selected performance, how many alts to bank, whether to record ensemble scenes together, whether to re-record after a rewrite or animate to the old read.
- **Engine.** Per-line take pools with performance scores; the selected take fixes phoneme timing that animation must match. Changing a line must flag every shot already animated to the previous take as needing re-animation.
- **Takes.** 4-8 hour sessions; 8-25 session days per lead across the show

#### Production Plan & Quota Setting

Converts runtime into artist-weeks: shot count x per-department quota / crew size = schedule and cost. Feature animation quota is 3-5 seconds per animator per week; series quotas are 5-10x higher.

- **Who.** Head of Production / Line Producer / Production Executive
- **Decides.** Quota per department, crew ramp curve and peak headcount, sequence order, in-house vs vendor split, contingency percentage, which sequences are hero and get extra rounds.
- **Engine.** A resource model: artists with discipline, skill and cost per week; a quota per discipline; a dependency graph over shots and assets. A slip in one department must propagate into a real date and a real money delta, because burn is crew x weeks.
- **Takes.** Set at greenlight, re-planned monthly for 2-4 years

#### Sequence Launch

A formal handoff opening a boarded sequence for production: shot list, camera intent, asset needs, effects needs and a semi-locked length distributed to every department at once.

- **Who.** Director, CG Supervisor, Production Manager
- **Decides.** When a sequence launches and at which board version; what is deferred; which shots are hero and which are cheats; what the sequence is allowed to cost.
- **Engine.** Sequence state machine. Launching commits assets and crew to dates. Launching on an unstable board version must raise downstream retake probability — the core gamble of the form.
- **Takes.** A half-day meeting; sequences launch weekly to monthly across 12-18 months

#### Shot & Asset Breakdown

Panels become numbered shots with frame ranges, asset lists and department flags in the tracker (ShotGrid/Flow, Ftrack, Kitsu); assets are inventoried and ordered by first-needed date with reuse and variant decisions.

- **Who.** Production Coordinator / Asset Coordinator / Editorial
- **Decides.** Where cuts fall and how shots are named or split; build vs reuse vs buy vs cheat per asset; hero and background quality tiers; which assets are deferred and therefore risk blocking a launch.
- **Engine.** Shot entity (sequence, frame range, asset dependencies, per-department task list and status) plus an asset graph with quality tiers, build cost, first-need dates and blocking relations. This is the single source of truth every other system reads and writes.
- **Takes.** Days per sequence, revised on every cut change; a feature carries 500-2,000+ assets

#### Character Modelling

Sculpt and retopologise the design into clean, animatable geometry with edge flow built for deformation, plus blendshape-ready topology and level-of-detail versions.

- **Who.** Character Modeler
- **Decides.** Topology density and edge flow, how much detail is geometry vs displacement map, LOD count, whether to build on a shared base mesh.
- **Engine.** Model asset with poly budget and a topology-quality value that directly conditions rig quality, deformation failure rate and CFX stability. Approval rounds are scored against the design pack.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks per hero character

#### Environment Modelling & Set Build

Build the world the camera can see: architecture, terrain, modular kits, dressing libraries, instanced scatter — then dress each shot to its specific camera, including cheats that only work from that angle.

- **Who.** Set Modeler / Environment Artist / Set Dresser
- **Decides.** Full build vs matte painting vs card; how far the set extends; instancing strategy; which areas get hero detail; which per-shot cheats are permitted.
- **Engine.** Set asset with a coverage map of supported camera angles, instance and scatter counts feeding memory and render time, and per-shot dressing layers. A camera that exceeds the build must produce a real failure.
- **Takes.** 6-16 weeks per major set; hours to two days of dressing per shot

#### UV Layout & Texture Painting

UV layout, then painted and procedural colour, roughness, displacement and mask maps at feature resolution across UDIM tile sets, matched to the colour keys.

- **Who.** Texture Artist / Surfacing Artist
- **Decides.** Texture resolution and UDIM count, painted vs procedural, how much wear and story detail an asset carries, which maps exist at all.
- **Engine.** Texture sets whose resolution drives memory footprint and render time; a map budget per asset; a visible-detail check against the closest camera the asset ever gets, so over-painting is waste and under-painting is a retake.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks per hero asset

#### Look Development

Build materials and prove them on a turntable under standardised lighting rigs so the asset reads correctly in every condition it will meet. Signed off by the production designer and the DP-lighting.

- **Who.** Look Development Artist / Shading Technical Director
- **Decides.** Shader complexity (subsurface, coats, hair BSDFs, displacement), physical vs stylised response, and therefore what this asset costs per frame for the rest of the show.
- **Engine.** Per-asset shading cost that contributes additively to per-frame render time, plus a look-consistency score evaluated across N lighting conditions. Approval requires passing the turntable set. This is the single largest lever on total render cost.
- **Takes.** 2-8 weeks per hero asset

#### Groom

Hair, fur and feather systems: guide curves, clumping, density, frizz and variants for wet, dirty or aged states — millions of rendered curves on a hero character.

- **Who.** Groom Artist / Character Effects Artist
- **Decides.** Curve counts and guide density, procedural vs hand-placed, how many groom variants exist, whether hair simulates per shot or is baked.
- **Engine.** Groom asset whose curve count drives both simulation cost and render cost, plus a per-shot sim requirement and characteristic failure modes (poke-through, clumping, flicker) surfacing downstream in CFX and lighting.
- **Takes.** 3-10 weeks per hero character

#### Rigging

Skeleton, deformation, controls, constraints and correctives so an animator can hit any pose, plus the animator-facing UI. The rig defines the ceiling on what performances are possible at all.

- **Who.** Character Technical Director / Rigger
- **Decides.** Rig complexity vs interactive playback speed, control scheme, cartoon squash-and-stretch range, whether to invest in a reusable template rig for the series.
- **Engine.** Rig object with expressiveness, interactive playback speed (which directly scales animator throughput), and stability. A version graph is mandatory: republishing a rig can invalidate in-progress animation across dozens of shots.
- **Takes.** 4-12 weeks per hero character; template rigs for series

#### Facial Rig & Phoneme Set

FACS-style shape library or joint-based face rig with a viseme/phoneme set, eye, brow and tongue controls and correctives — the machinery every line of lip sync and every acting beat runs through.

- **Who.** Facial Rigger / Character Technical Director
- **Decides.** Shape count (dozens to 1,000+), FACS vs artistic shape design, whether lip sync is procedurally assisted or entirely hand-keyed, how much of the face is animator-accessible.
- **Engine.** Face rig with expressive range and per-shape build and evaluation cost; lip-sync speed derives from it. A dialogue change must force re-keying of every shot built on the old track, at a cost the player can see coming.
- **Takes.** 6-16 weeks per hero character

#### Costume & Cloth Setup

Garment panels, stitching, collision geometry and simulation parameters so cloth behaves with weight, plus a tailoring pass so it fits the rig correctly at rest and in extremes.

- **Who.** Character Effects Artist / Tailoring Technical Director
- **Decides.** Simulated vs modelled-and-skinned cloth, layer count, collision resolution and substeps, how much art direction is allowed to override physics.
- **Engine.** Garment asset with per-frame sim cost and a stability score. Per-shot sims must be able to fail — explode, interpenetrate, jitter — and require re-running at higher substep cost, consuming farm hours.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks per costume, then per-shot for the entire show

#### Asset Publish, Versioning & Dependency Management

Assets published as versioned USD layers that downstream departments reference, pinned or floating. This plumbing is what makes 300 people working on one image simultaneously possible.

- **Who.** Pipeline Technical Director / Asset Supervisor
- **Decides.** Version pinning policy, when to force a global asset update across the show, whether to freeze DCC and renderer versions mid-production, who is allowed to publish.
- **Engine.** A dependency graph with version pins. A republish either propagates cleanly or breaks downstream work; a forced global update must cost re-approval on every shot referencing the asset. Pipeline maturity multiplies every department's throughput.
- **Takes.** Continuous; 5-40 engineers on a feature-scale show

#### Rough Layout / Previs

The first 3D camera: proxy characters staged in the real set, action and camera moves blocked to the boarded intent, cut to length and returned to editorial to replace panels in the reel.

- **Who.** Layout Artist / Previs Artist
- **Decides.** Lens and focal length, camera position and move, staging, screen direction, shot length, whether to keep the boarded camera or find a better one.
- **Engine.** A virtual camera with focal length, sensor, DOF and animated move curves. Layout output replaces boards in the reel, exposes set-coverage gaps and shot-length drift, and firms the shot count the whole plan rests on.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per shot on a feature; a series budgets ~13 seconds per artist per day

#### Virtual Camera Scout

Director and DP walk the digital set with a tracked handheld virtual camera or VR rig and shoot live coverage, hunting angles the boards never found — the closest this form comes to a shooting day.

- **Who.** Director of Photography (Camera) / Layout Supervisor
- **Decides.** Whether to shoot handheld-feeling coverage at all, how filmic the camera language is (whether physical camera constraints are enforced), which live takes to keep.
- **Engine.** A capture mode generating take variants with human motion noise and operator skill; requires the set to be built far enough to scout; output feeds layout as reference or as final camera. Tradeoff: better cameras, later set builds.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days per sequence, at studios that use it

#### Final Layout

After blocking is approved, the camera is polished to final against the real performance: precise framing, lens choice, motion blur and DOF settings, handheld feel, and the shot length editorial then locks.

- **Who.** Final Layout Artist / Layout Supervisor
- **Decides.** Final framing and lens, camera shake, whether to reframe to hide or exclude unfinished work, whether to reframe to save animation or FX cost, exact cut points.
- **Engine.** Camera remains an editable object AFTER performance — impossible in live action. Reframing must change what is in frame and therefore render cost, and must break camera-dependent cheats made in dressing, matte painting and light-linking.
- **Takes.** 0.5-2 days per shot

#### Animation Blocking

Key poses in stepped mode establishing acting choice, timing and staging against the locked dialogue. Blocking is where the performance is judged — it is this form's equivalent of a take.

- **Who.** Animator / Character Animator
- **Decides.** The acting choice, pose selection, timing and comic rhythm, how far to depart from the boarded intent, how much reference to shoot, when to show the director.
- **Engine.** Shot performance state built from animator skill, rig quality, reference quality and difficulty (character count, dialogue density, physical action). Feature quota 3-5 seconds per animator per week must be the governing rate.
- **Takes.** 3-10 days per shot at feature quality

#### Animation Dailies Round

Work in progress played to supervisor and director, annotated live on the frame, returned as a numbered round. Shots typically take 3-8 rounds; a hero shot can take 15.

- **Who.** Animation Supervisor / Directing Animator / Director
- **Decides.** Approve, note or restart; which shots the director personally reviews; how many rounds a shot is allowed before it is declared good enough; which notes to overrule.
- **Engine.** A round loop per shot with a quality-vs-rounds curve, a director taste function, and a hard cost per round in artist-days. Approvals per week — not shots started — is the true throughput measure and must drive the completion forecast.
- **Takes.** 1-2 hours per department every working day, for years

#### Animation Spline & Polish

Convert stepped keys to interpolated curves, then add overlap, drag, breakdowns, eye darts, blinks, finger performance and lip-sync detail until the shot reaches animation final.

- **Who.** Animator / Polish Animator
- **Decides.** How much polish and where to spend it, whether to hand the shot to a polish specialist, which defects to fix now and which to let CFX absorb, when to stop.
- **Engine.** A diminishing-returns polish curve per shot. Skipped polish must surface downstream as concrete defects — foot slide, pops, interpenetration, dead eyes — that cost CFX and lighting time or come back as a note at finals.
- **Takes.** 3-10 days per shot; a series budgets ~6 seconds per animator per day

#### Crowd Animation

Fill the frame with agents driven by cycle libraries, behaviour rules, flow fields and terrain adaptation; hero-adjacent agents are hand-fixed. Replaces background casting entirely.

- **Who.** Crowd Artist / Crowd Technical Director
- **Decides.** Agent count, cycle library size and variety, procedural vs hand-animated, LOD by distance, how much variation is worth building.
- **Engine.** A crowd system where agent count drives authoring time AND render cost, with visible failure modes scaled inversely to library size: marching clones, synchronised gaits, foot slide, agents intersecting or walking through set geometry.
- **Takes.** Weeks to build the system; 2-10 days per crowd shot

#### Character Effects (Cloth & Hair Simulation)

Run cloth, hair, flesh and accessory simulations over approved animation per shot, then hand-fix interpenetrations, sliding and explosions. The pass that gives animation weight and consequence.

- **Who.** Character Effects (CFX) Artist
- **Decides.** Sim settings, resolution and substeps; when to art-direct over physics; when to reject the shot back to animation rather than fix it; which failures are visible enough to matter.
- **Engine.** Per-shot sim jobs costing farm hours, with a failure probability rising with fast motion, tight contact and layered garments, non-deterministic behaviour on re-run, and a published cache that lighting consumes.
- **Takes.** 0.5-3 days of artist time per shot plus overnight farm time

#### Effects Simulation

Water, fire, smoke, destruction, magic, dust and interaction elements authored per shot in Houdini and cached as volumes and geometry — by far the heaviest compute and storage in the show.

- **Who.** FX Artist / FX Technical Director
- **Decides.** Simulation resolution, element count, procedural vs cheated, how many low-res wedge tests before committing to a full-resolution cache, whether to build a reusable setup or a one-off.
- **Engine.** FX tasks where resolution drives cache size drives farm hours drives storage in terabytes. A wedge/iteration loop with overnight turnaround; a failed full-res sim must burn days and be recoverable only by re-running.
- **Takes.** 1-6 weeks per hero effects shot; sims run overnight per iteration

#### Master Lighting

A lighting recipe built once per sequence on a handful of key shots — key, fill, bounce, time of day, atmosphere — matched to the colour script; every other shot in the sequence inherits it.

- **Who.** Director of Photography (Lighting) / Sequence Lighting Lead
- **Decides.** The lighting concept per sequence, how many master setups a sequence needs, how much per-shot deviation is permitted, how physically motivated vs painterly the light is.
- **Engine.** A sequence light rig as an inheritable template with per-shot overrides, a consistency score across the sequence, and a direct mapping from light count and sampling settings to minutes per frame.
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks per sequence

#### Shot Lighting

Adapt the master rig to the shot: character-specific lights, eye lights and rims, contact shadows, atmosphere, frame balance, and the sample and AOV settings comp will need.

- **Who.** Lighting Artist / Lighting Technical Director
- **Decides.** Per-shot deviations from the master; sample counts traded against noise and render time; which render passes and AOVs to output; where to cheat with light-linking and hidden lights.
- **Engine.** A per-shot render recipe (samples, ray depth, resolution, AOV list) resolving to minutes per frame; a visible quality-vs-noise tradeoff at review; light-linking cheats that must break if final layout reframes.
- **Takes.** 1-4 days per shot at feature quality; many shots per day on a series

#### Render Submission & Farm Wrangling

Jobs submitted to the farm queue with priority, chunk size, memory limits and dependencies; wranglers babysit the overnight run, kill runaway jobs, requeue failed frames and defend the dailies deadline.

- **Who.** Render Wrangler / Systems Engineer
- **Decides.** Priority order and who owns the farm tonight; resolution and sample compromises to make the window; whether to kill an FX sim to save lighting; whether to burst to cloud at a premium.
- **Engine.** A farm with finite cores/GPUs, a priority queue, per-job memory and wall-time limits, a frame failure rate, and a hard nightly window before the next morning's review. Missing the window costs a whole crew-day of review.
- **Takes.** Every night of production, for years

#### Render & Storage Capacity Planning

Measure per-frame times, project total core-hours, and buy or optimise the difference. A 100-minute feature is 144,000 frames; heavy frames have been reported at up to 29 hours each (Monsters University, on a ~24,000-core farm).

- **Who.** Render Technical Director / Head of Systems / Head of Production Technology
- **Decides.** Farm size and hardware refresh, on-prem vs cloud burst, the per-frame time budget enforced on lookdev and lighting, cache retention and purge policy, when to force optimisation over artistry.
- **Engine.** Core-hours = frames x average minutes per frame / cores, plus a 10-25% re-render margin, against a farm whose size was fixed months earlier. Storage and IO are separate finite resources. This can make a schedule mathematically impossible — the form's hardest constraint.
- **Takes.** Continuous; capacity plan reset quarterly, hardware ordered 6-12 months ahead

#### Compositing

Assemble rendered AOVs and elements into the final image: integration, depth grades, atmospherics, lens effects, grain, and 2D fixes that would cost days of farm time to re-render.

- **Who.** Compositor / Compositing Supervisor
- **Decides.** Fix in comp vs kick back for a re-render; how far the comp is allowed to depart from the lit render; how much bloom, haze and lens character to add; which notes are cheaper here than upstream.
- **Engine.** A comp graph over the render's actual AOV set — a missing pass must force a re-render. A fixable-in-comp test per note trading comp hours against render hours. Series budget ~4 shots per artist per day; features far fewer.
- **Takes.** 0.5-3 days per shot at feature quality

#### Matte Painting & 2D Environments

Skies, distant cities, vistas and set extensions painted and projected onto cards or simplified geometry wherever a full 3D build cannot be justified.

- **Who.** Matte Painter / Digital Matte Painting Artist
- **Decides.** Where the 3D build stops and the painting starts; projection setup and card geometry; whether the camera move survives the projection or the set must be built after all.
- **Engine.** Paint assets bound to a camera range with a parallax limit. If the camera later exceeds that limit the painting visibly breaks and must be rebuilt or escalated into a real 3D build — a late, expensive discovery.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks per painting

#### Retake & Note Tracking

Every note from every review captured, assigned, prioritised, costed and tracked to closure in the tracker. The retake list is the daily management surface of the entire show.

- **Who.** Production Coordinator / Production Manager
- **Decides.** Which notes are actioned, waived or deferred; whose queue a retake enters; when a shot has taken enough rounds to be escalated or abandoned; what the director is told is impossible.
- **Engine.** Note objects with source, severity, department, cost estimate and status. The aggregate note burn-down — not shots started or even shots finished — must be the honest completion metric the player learns to read.
- **Takes.** Daily, for the entire production

#### Editorial Conform & Reel Replacement

Each night's approved output is cut back into the reel at its correct stage; the cut is continuously re-timed, and a shot's length change ripples into every department already working on it.

- **Who.** Editor / Assistant Editor
- **Decides.** Whether to accept a longer or shorter shot; when to lock a sequence's timing; what temp material stays; when to cut a shot that is already finished because the scene plays better without it.
- **Engine.** The reel timeline with per-shot stage and version. A length change must flag every in-progress downstream task on that shot as rework, with a real cost — including work already finaled and now discarded.
- **Takes.** Daily; sequence locks land 3-9 months before delivery

#### Music: Song Lock, Spotting and Score

Temp score lives in the reel from the start; the composer writes to successive reels for months and records late. Songs are written, demoed and locked YEARS early because characters must be animated to the final vocal.

- **Who.** Composer / Music Supervisor / Music Editor
- **Decides.** Temp vs original demos early; song count and who performs them; orchestra size and recording city; how much score is committed against unfinished picture.
- **Engine.** Music cues bound to reel timings — a re-cut must invalidate cues and cost re-writes. Song lock is a hard upstream dependency of animation, so a late song change invalidates every animated musical sequence.
- **Takes.** 6-12 months of writing; 3-15 orchestra recording days

#### Sound Design & Final Mix

Every sound in the film is created from nothing — there is no production track. Foley, design, creature vocals, walla and effects cut to picture, then a final mix on a dub stage with Atmos and print-master deliverables.

- **Who.** Supervising Sound Editor / Sound Designer / Re-recording Mixer
- **Decides.** Design approach per creature, vehicle and world; foley coverage depth; mix balance against dialogue that can never be re-recorded for noise; how many deliverable formats.
- **Engine.** Sound as a wholly authored layer with per-reel effort and cost; dialogue already clean so the only re-record is for changed lines; dub stage days as a fixed, bookable, expensive resource with a hard calendar.
- **Takes.** 12-20 weeks of sound editorial; 3-6 weeks on the dub stage

#### Colour, Stereo & Mastering

Final grade of rendered images in a theatrical suite, HDR and SDR trim passes, IMAX and stereo versions (native second-eye render or conversion), and QC of every master.

- **Who.** Digital Intermediate Colorist / Imaging Supervisor / Stereoscopic Supervisor
- **Decides.** How far the grade may depart from the rendered intent; HDR trim strategy; native stereo vs conversion; which deliverables exist; whether a defect is fixed in grade or sent back for a re-render.
- **Engine.** Master versions per deliverable, each with cost and schedule. Native stereo roughly doubles render hours; conversion buys a vendor cost instead. A late stereo or IMAX decision must force re-renders at the worst possible moment.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks of grading plus trims; stereo runs 8-20 weeks in parallel

#### Finals

A shot is reviewed at full resolution with every element present, on a calibrated theatre screen, and declared final. Only then does it leave the pipeline. A feature finals 1,200-2,000 shots this way.

- **Who.** CG Supervisor / Director / Producer
- **Decides.** Final or not; whether a defect is worth the re-render given the remaining farm hours; which shots get a second finals pass after the grade; what ships imperfect.
- **Engine.** A finals gate with defect-detection probability conditioned on review conditions (desk monitor vs theatre) — defects missed at the desk must surface in the theatre. Finals per week is the burn-down that actually predicts the delivery date.
- **Takes.** Finals ramp across the last 6-9 months; the last 5% takes the last month

#### Technical QC & Delivery

Automated and eyes-on checks for flicker, noise, dropped or duplicate frames, colour space, audio sync and format compliance against distributor specs, then delivery of masters and every derived version.

- **Who.** QC Technician / Post Production Supervisor / Digital Imaging Technician
- **Decides.** What fails QC and what ships with a waiver; delivery order; which territories receive which version; whether to hold delivery for a fix.
- **Engine.** A defect table by class (firefly, denoiser artifact, wrong colour space, dropped frame, sync drift) with per-class detection probability and per-fix cost, plus distributor spec objects that can reject a master outright.
- **Takes.** 4-10 weeks; final delivery 6-12 weeks before release

#### Localisation & Dub Versioning

20-40 language versions cast, directed and mixed; on-screen text, signage and graphic props re-rendered or re-comped per territory; occasionally mouth animation adjusted for key markets.

- **Who.** Localisation Producer / Dubbing Director
- **Decides.** Which languages get star casting; whether to re-render localised graphics or subtitle them; how much lip-sync mismatch is acceptable; which territories get a premium dub.
- **Engine.** Per-territory version cost; assets flagged as localisable, whose re-render costs comp and farm time; dub quality feeding territory-level box office and platform performance. Animation dubs far better than live action and earns proportionally more abroad.
- **Takes.** 8-16 weeks, overlapping final delivery

#### Marketing Shot Pull-Forward

Specific shots forced through the entire pipeline out of order — sometimes 18 months early — so a teaser can cut finished frames while the rest of the film is still storyboards.

- **Who.** Marketing Producer / Trailer Editor / Producer
- **Decides.** Which shots to pull forward and how much schedule damage to accept; whether to build assets early purely for marketing; whether to fake a shot for the trailer that will not exist in the film.
- **Engine.** A pull-forward request that reorders the dependency graph at a multiple of normal cost (assets built early, then changed and rebuilt), weighed against a measurable audience-awareness gain and a trailer-quality score.
- **Takes.** Trailer beats at roughly 18, 12, 6 and 3 months out

#### Series: Asset Library Lock & Reuse Amortisation

A series builds a fixed library once — characters, sets, props, rigs, master light rigs — then amortises it over 13-52 episodes. New assets per episode, not screen time, are the real cost driver.

- **Who.** Series Art Director / Asset Supervisor / Series Producer
- **Decides.** How large the launch library is; the per-episode new-asset allowance; whether to send a script back because it requires a new set; when a character variant is worth building.
- **Engine.** An amortisation model: library build cost vs per-episode marginal cost. Scripts must be scanned for new-asset demand against a hard per-episode budget, so writing and production economics are directly coupled.
- **Takes.** 6-12 months of pre-production before episode 1 enters production

#### Series: Vendor & Partner Studio Management

Blocks of episodes packaged and farmed to partner studios (India, Philippines, Vietnam, Korea, France, Canada) with briefs, model packs, quotas and a retake loop across time zones.

- **Who.** Overseas Supervisor / Vendor Producer / Line Producer
- **Decides.** What stays in-house vs goes out; package size and price; retake tolerance; which vendor gets hero episodes; whether to re-award mid-season after a bad delivery.
- **Engine.** Vendor entities with cost, quality, turnaround, capacity and drift; a retake loop delayed by a time-zone and shipping lag; quality problems that only surface at delivery, when redoing them costs more than the original saving.
- **Takes.** Continuous across a 12-26 month season

#### Series: Overlapping Episode Waves

Episodes run as overlapping waves — episode 9 in animation while 12 is in layout and 15 is recording — so every department stays loaded and one episode delivers every one to two weeks.

- **Who.** Series Producer / Production Manager
- **Decides.** Wave spacing and how many episodes are in flight; where to insert catch-up gaps; which department is the bottleneck and gets staffed up; which episode to sacrifice when the broadcast slot is immovable.
- **Engine.** A pipelined multi-episode scheduler with per-department capacity. A stall in one department must back up every episode behind it, and each episode has its own immovable delivery date against a broadcast or platform slot.
- **Takes.** 8-12 weeks per episode through the pipeline; 26 months for a 52x12min season

#### Pipeline & Tools Engineering

Build and maintain the USD-based scene assembly, publishing, versioning, review and farm tooling, plus DCC integrations and tracker hooks, that every other department stands on.

- **Who.** Pipeline Technical Director / Software Engineer
- **Decides.** Build vs buy; which DCC, renderer and OS versions to freeze for the show; whether to adopt new technology mid-production; how much artist downtime a tool migration is worth.
- **Engine.** Pipeline maturity as a multiplier on every department's throughput and failure rate, plus a version-freeze decision with real consequences: a mid-show upgrade risks look changes and mass re-renders, a freeze accrues technical debt.
- **Takes.** Continuous; 5-40 engineers on a feature-scale show

---

## 2. 2D and hand-drawn animation

*TV series (anime and Western), hand-drawn features, and the overseas-subcontract model most series actually run on*

### The unit of work

The CUT (Japan) / the SCENE (US — in animation a "scene" is one shot), and beneath it the DRAWING. A 22-minute TV anime episode is roughly 250-350 cuts (~300 typical). Footage is the older unit and still governs union timing rates: 35mm at 16 frames per foot, so 1 foot = 2/3 second and a 22-minute episode ≈ 1,980 feet. The TAG freelance timing classification was paid $4.95/foot on a basis of 70 feet per 8 hours (2024-27 table, not applicable on/after 1 Jan 2025) — i.e. a sheet timer's day is ~47 seconds of screen time. Per-craft daily units: an inbetweener's day is drawings (roughly 10-25 depending on line count — estimate; paid ¥200-300/drawing in Japan); a TV key animator's day is 1-3 cuts (¥2,000-5,000 per cut for 1st key, ¥1,600-3,200 for 2nd key — 2023-era figures); a classic Disney feature animator with assistants ran ~3.75 feet/day ≈ 14 seconds a week; a background artist's day is 1-3 BGs; a compositor's day is 6-15 cuts. PROGRESS is the cut table (進行表): every cut a row, every stage a column (LO / LO-check / genga / AD correction / douga / douga-check / paint / BG / composite / retake / OK), each cell moving not-started → out → in-hand → up → checked → OK. A good day is a stage-up date hit for a whole parcel: "LO up", "genga up", "douga up", "all cuts in composite". A bad day is one cut you cannot find because the freelancer holding it has stopped answering the phone. Note the asymmetry with live action: there is no coverage and no second take. Every frame is built exactly once, and doing it again means drawing it again.

### What is absent

- The shooting day itself. There is no call sheet, no unit, no company move, no wrap. Nothing is 'shot' — a modern 2D film is never photographed at any point in its life.

### What is reordered

- EDITING MOVES TO THE FRONT. The animatic/Leica reel IS the edit: the picture is cut to length against dialogue before a single finished frame exists. Downstream 'editing' is conform and trimming, never shot selection — you cannot choose a take that was never drawn.

### How the crew differs

- PRESENT HERE, ABSENT IN LIVE ACTION — Key Animator (原画): draws only the extremes that define motion, not the whole shot. There is no live-action analogue because no live-action worker authors motion.

### What goes wrong

- The animator goes dark. A freelancer holding 14 cuts stops answering the phone at genga-up. No one can compel a freelancer to deliver; the PA drives to their apartment, and the cuts are reassigned to a second key artist or drawn by the AD overnight. The live-action equivalent — an actor vanishing mid-shoot — is an insured event; this one is a Tuesday.

### The shape of the money

Labour is almost the entire negative cost. There is no equipment rental, no location fee, no set construction, no transport, no unit catering, no weather cover. In a TV anime episode the sakuga block — layout, key animation, second key, AD correction, inbetween and finishing — is typically 60-70% of the episode's cost, with pre-production and post-production roughly 10-20% each (industry-vendor estimate). Compare live action, where above-the-line plus the shooting crew plus locations and equipment dominate and the drawing-equivalent line does not exist.

SCALE. A 30-minute TV anime episode is currently quoted at ¥25-50M (roughly $160k-320k) with high-profile seasonal titles going well past that; the 2000s baseline was closer to ¥10-15M. ARCH's Nao Hirasawa has cited TV costs reaching as much as ¥300M per episode and ¥4bn per feature at the extreme top end (reported figure, treat as an outlier not a norm). Western half-hour 2D for cable or streaming is commonly $300k-800k per episode with prime-time network animation far above that (estimate). A hand-drawn feature runs 300-600 crew over 3-4 years; Princess Mononoke used over 144,000 cels, roughly 80,000 of which Miyazaki personally corrected or redrew.

WHO CARRIES THE RISK. This is the structural difference. The Japanese studio is paid a fixed production fee per episode by the production committee; the committee owns the copyright pro rata and each member exploits its own window (print, broadcast, streaming, discs, music, merchandise, games). Every overrun comes out of the studio's own margin. There is no completion bond, no contingency line held by a financier, and no insurable weather day. The de facto contingency is unpaid overtime by freelancers.

PIECE RATES, NOT DAY RATES. Japan pays per unit produced: roughly ¥200-300 per inbetween drawing, ¥2,000-5,000 per cut for first key animation, ¥1,600-3,200 per cut for second key. JAniCA's 2023 survey put average annual income around ¥2.63M for dōga staff and just under ¥4M for those working in the genga process; entry-level inbetweeners commonly clear under $10,000 a year. The US pays weekly union scale instead: TAG Local 839, 2024-27 agreement, second period (4 Aug 2024 - 2 Aug 2025) — Animator/Background/Layout/Model Designer journey $60.33/hr, $2,413.20/wk; Key Assistant Animator $57.81/hr; Assistant Animator and Storyboard Revisionist journey $51.63/hr, $2,065.20/wk; Breakdown journey $45.34/hr; Inbetweener journey $43.67/hr, $1,746.80/wk; Animation Checker journey $51.63/hr; Painter journey $42.90/hr; Special Effects journey $44.68/hr; Color Modelist journey $44.89/hr; Timing Director journey $67.60/hr, $2,704.00/wk; Animation Director journey $70.52/hr, $2,820.80/wk; Animation Art Director $70.03/hr. Storyboard-only flat for a half-hour subject is $4,186.81 (older license terms) rising to $4,354.27 with 240 P&H hours. Freelance timing was $4.95 per foot on a basis of 8 hours per 70 feet, a classification retired on 1 January 2025. The identical job therefore costs roughly an order of magnitude more in Burbank than in Tokyo, which is the entire economic reason the overseas relationship exists.

OFFSHORE SHARE. A 2024 survey found 45.2% of Japanese studios outsourcing part of production; China's share fell 14.7% on rising cost while Vietnam and the Philippines rose and 23.7% turned to the US for specialised digital work. Western series have shipped animation to Korea, Taiwan, the Philippines, Vietnam, India and China since the early 1980s; the domestic spend becomes board, design, timing and retake supervision only.

THE ASSET THAT GETS CHEAPER. Backgrounds and bank cuts are drawn once and reused free forever, so a long-running series' per-episode cost falls as the bank deepens. Nothing in live action behaves this way — a standing set still costs to light, dress and crew every single day it is used.

### Processes that exist only here

#### Series composition

Breaks the source manga, light novel or original premise into a cour: what adapts, what is cut, what is anime-original, where the 12th or 13th episode lands so the cour ends on a hook.

- **Who.** Series Composition Writer (シリーズ構成) / Showrunner
- **Decides.** Which arcs adapt; episode count (12/13/24/26/50); the cour break point; how much anime-original material to write; which chapters are compressed, merged or dropped.
- **Engine.** Source-material graph with chapter→screen-minute estimates; adaptation coverage and fidelity scores driving source-fandom reaction and publisher-member approval; per-episode content slots that later constrain cut count and action load.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks

#### Scenario meeting (hon-uchi)

Each script passes table meetings with director, producer and committee members, numbered draft by draft (first draft, second, third) until the kettei-kō, the locked final draft.

- **Who.** Series Composition Writer, Episode Writer, Director, Producer
- **Decides.** Which writer takes which episode; how many draft rounds to fund; whether to fight a publisher or sponsor note; whether to write tight or leave room for the board artist.
- **Engine.** Per-episode draft state machine with note queues per stakeholder (director, producer, publisher, broadcaster, sponsor). Each round costs days and a writer fee; unresolved notes reappear at storyboard as expensive rework.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks per episode, overlapping

#### Character design and settei

Turnarounds, expression sheets, mouth charts (vowel-shape sets), height comparisons, costume variants — the settei pack every one of 20+ animators draws from. Simplification here sets the whole show's cost.

- **Who.** Character Designer / Chief Animation Director (総作画監督)
- **Decides.** Line count per character (how many strokes the hair takes); number of costume variants; whether to build a simplified TV version of a complex source design; how large the mouth chart set is.
- **Engine.** Per-character complexity value (lines per drawing) that multiplies genga time, douga time and per-drawing paint time; an off-model tolerance band; a settei-completeness gate that blocks boarding and blocks any outsourcing package.
- **Takes.** 4-10 weeks

#### Prop, mecha and sub-character settei

Orthographic sheets with construction lines for every object anyone must draw twice: weapons, vehicles, interiors, background characters, food, phones. The animation equivalent of the props and set-dressing departments.

- **Who.** Prop Designer / Mechanical Designer / Sub-character Designer
- **Decides.** Which props get full settei and which are 'draw from reference'; whether mecha go 3DCG or drawn; how many sub-characters earn sheets; who designs them.
- **Engine.** Asset registry keyed to cuts. A cut referencing an unspecified asset raises retake probability and slows genga. The drawn/CG flag routes the asset to a different department, cost curve and reuse economics.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks, then continuous

#### Colour design (色彩設計)

Fixes every character and prop as a palette with separations — base, shadow (kage), second shadow, highlight — then builds alternate sets: night, sunset, firelight, moonlight, flashback, monochrome.

- **Who.** Colour Designer / Colour Stylist
- **Decides.** How many shadow levels the show carries (one or two); which alternate sets to build up front; whether a given effect is a drawn colour or a composite treatment.
- **Engine.** Per-asset palette objects with named separations; per-cut set binding. Paint time scales with separation count; a missing set stalls finishing; a wrong set is a detectable continuity error at composite.
- **Takes.** 3-6 weeks up front, then per-cut all run

#### Art board and BG style

Paints key boards fixing the world's light, texture and palette, then builds the BG bank of standing sets — classroom, bedroom, the street outside — that will be reused across the entire run.

- **Who.** Art Director (美術監督) / Background Supervisor
- **Decides.** Medium (poster colour on board vs fully digital); how many standing sets to bank before episode 1; detail level per BG; whether skies and clouds come from stock.
- **Engine.** BG asset library with per-asset reuse counts; per-cut choice of new / bank / modified-bank; painter style-match scoring against the board. Bank depth makes each later episode cheaper — the only asset in film that improves with use.
- **Takes.** 4-8 weeks up front

#### Storyboard / ekonte (絵コンテ)

The whole film drawn shot by shot with cut numbers, dialogue, action notes, camera moves and a length in seconds and frames for every cut. In anime this single document is what every department works from.

- **Who.** Storyboard Artist; in anime usually the Director or a hired ekonte artist
- **Decides.** Cut count and per-cut length; how much motion each cut carries; camera moves; what is shown versus implied; where in the episode to spend the limited action budget.
- **Engine.** The cut list is the master object: number, frames, characters, BG, FX, dialogue, camera. Sum of frames = running length. Per-cut difficulty drives every downstream cost, and any board revision cascades into every department at once.
- **Takes.** 3-6 weeks per TV episode; 1-2 years for a feature

#### Board revision / cleanup

Takes the approved rough board and brings it on-model, fixes staging and continuity, and raises it to the drawing quality the overseas vendor will animate directly from. Has no anime equivalent.

- **Who.** Storyboard Revisionist (US classification)
- **Decides.** Which panels are redrawn versus retraced; whether to add panels for clarity; how much to fix silently versus flag back to the board artist or director.
- **Engine.** Board quality score consumed directly by the vendor model: low board quality raises overseas retake rate. Labour is guild weekly — TAG journey Assistant Animator/Storyboard Revisionist $51.63/hr, $2,065.20/wk (2024-27, period 2).
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks per half-hour

#### Animatic / Leica reel / V-conte

Boards cut to time against scratch or final dialogue and a temp track. This is the edit. Picture is effectively locked here, before any animation exists, and finished shots are later dropped into these slots.

- **Who.** Editor / Animatic Editor
- **Decides.** Where to trim; whether to add or lose cuts; pacing scene by scene; whether to hold picture lock or reopen it and pay for the cascade.
- **Engine.** Timeline object owning running length and per-cut slot state. Post-lock changes invalidate downstream cut states and charge redone work. A per-cut lock flag gates release into layout and animation.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks per episode; iterated for a feature

#### Voice casting

Auditions are read to board panels or an animatic, never to camera. In Japan the sound director and committee choose, and the record-label member's roster and agency package deals weigh on the result.

- **Who.** Casting Director (US) / Sound Director 音響監督 (Japan)
- **Decides.** The cast; fee bracket (Japan's seiyuu rank system sets per-episode scale); whether to buy a name for marketing; how many roles one actor doubles.
- **Engine.** Talent pool with range, rank/fee, agency ties, schedule and draw. Character-fit scoring; doubling cuts cost but raises audience-notice risk; committee members' rosters act as hard constraints on the shortlist.
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks

#### Pre-record to track (US model)

Full dialogue recorded before any animation, usually one actor alone in a booth, many takes per line. The cut dialogue track becomes the immovable spine the animation is built onto.

- **Who.** Voice Director / Recording Engineer
- **Decides.** Take selection per line; whether to record wild alts and ad-libs; how many pickup days to budget; when to book the group loop session.
- **Engine.** Line-level take library with performance scores. The chosen take becomes an immutable timing constraint for track reading and animation; any later line change forces re-animation of every affected cut.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days per half-hour episode

#### Track reading / bar sheets

Breaks the recorded dialogue frame by frame into phonemes and writes them into the dialogue column of the exposure sheet, so the animator knows which mouth shape sits on which frame.

- **Who.** Track Reader / Timing Director
- **Decides.** Granularity (every frame versus accented syllables only); which mouth-chart set applies; where to cheat sync so it reads better than it is.
- **Engine.** Per-frame phoneme track bound to the x-sheet and to the character's mouth chart. Lip-sync accuracy scores against the audience read; a sloppy breakdown produces mouth retakes that are cheap to cause and expensive to fix.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days per episode

#### Sheet timing / timing direction

Writes the exposure sheet for every cut: which drawing is exposed on which frame, holds, ones/twos/threes, cel level order, cycles, and the camera column with pans, trucks and fades.
