- **Who.** Timing Director / Sheet Timer (US); Episode Director 演出 (Japan)
- **Decides.** Drawing count for each action; ones, twos or threes; hold lengths; how many cel levels; where to cycle or reuse; every camera move and its ease-in and ease-out.
- **Engine.** X-sheet object: 144-frame pages at 24fps (six seconds), level columns A/B/C/D, drawing numbers, camera column. Drawing count is THE cost driver; timing quality drives the motion-read score independent of drawing quality.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks per half-hour

#### Cut breakdown and assignment

Splits the boarded cut list into parcels for individual key animators — by scene block, by difficulty, by who can draw what — and books freelancers against LO-up and genga-up dates.

- **Who.** Production Desk (制作デスク) / Animation Producer / Production Assistant (制作進行)
- **Decides.** Who gets which cuts and how many; which cuts go to a star for a showcase sequence; what is outsourced; the deadline written on each parcel.
- **Engine.** Animator roster with speed, quality, specialism (effects, action, acting, mecha, crowds), reliability, per-cut rate and current bookings on rival shows. Assignment yields per-cut ETA distributions and expected quality, not a fixed date.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks per episode, revised daily

#### Layout (LO)

Turns a boarded cut into a full-size drawing on the frame: exact framing and perspective, character placement, the BG line drawing the art department will paint from, and the camera move marked on the sheet.

- **Who.** Key Animator drawing layouts / Layout Artist
- **Decides.** Real staging inside the boarded idea; perspective and implied lens; how much BG information to hand the art department; framing safety and how the move starts and ends.
- **Engine.** One layout artefact per cut that forks into two dependencies — the BG order and the genga order — and gates both. Perspective and framing errors that survive LO check surface only at composite, after every department has paid for the cut.
- **Takes.** 3-6 weeks per TV episode across the team

#### Layout check (LO-uchi)

Four departments sign the same drawing: staging and acting, drawing quality and model, background feasibility, and whether the camera move is achievable in composite. Corrections are drawn on overlay paper.

- **Who.** Episode Director (演出), Animation Director (作画監督), Art Director, Director of Photography (撮影監督)
- **Decides.** Accept, correct or send back; who absorbs the correction (the AD's own hand versus the animator's time); whether to simplify a move the compositor says will not work.
- **Engine.** Multi-approver gate with a per-approver correction load. Correction hours charge to the AD's finite weekly throughput — the hidden schedule killer. Rejection loops back with a new delivery date that ripples through the cut table.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per parcel

#### Key animation (genga 原画)

Draws the extremes and breakdowns that define motion, numbers them against the timesheet, and marks the inbetween charts — the spacing arcs that tell the inbetweener how many drawings go where. Rough, not final.

- **Who.** Key Animator (原画マン)
- **Decides.** How many keys the action needs; arcs, spacing and overlap; how much to leave to the inbetweener; whether to draw the effect or flag it for the FX animator.
- **Engine.** Per-cut key set with key count, chart spacing and quality; douga count computed as f(keys, chart, frames, ones/twos/threes). Individual animators carry identifiable style that should be visible in the finished cut.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per cut per animator; 3-5 weeks for an episode's genga

#### Second key animation (dai-ni genga / 二原)

A clean corrected pass over rough or unfinished genga that folds in the AD's and director's corrections, drawn without the layout frame. Exists because schedules no longer let key animators finish their own work.

- **Who.** Second Key Animator (第二原画)
- **Decides.** Whether a cut routes to nigen at all; in-house or outsourced; whether the slot is a rescue for a late cut or a training slot for a junior.
- **Engine.** Optional per-cut stage. Adds days and cost (≈¥1,600-3,200/cut, 2023-era estimate) but lowers douga error rate and AD correction load. Overuse flattens the original animator's individuality out of the show.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks per episode when used

#### Animation direction correction (作画監督修正)

Redraws or overlays corrections across every key drawing in the episode to pull 10-20 different hands onto one model at one quality level. Routinely the single largest individual workload on a TV episode.

- **Who.** Animation Director (作画監督)
- **Decides.** How hard to correct — full redraw versus light touch; which cuts to leave alone; whether to preserve a strong animator's style at the cost of the model.
- **Engine.** Per-cut off-model delta and correction effort against a hard AD weekly throughput cap. Correction raises episode consistency but consumes the days everyone else needs; uncorrected cuts read to the audience as visible quality variance.
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks per episode, always finishing late

#### Chief animation director pass (総作画監督)

A second correction layer applied across all episodes so the leads look the same in episode 11 as in episode 1 — often faces only. Series-level consistency insurance sitting above the per-episode AD.

- **Who.** Chief Animation Director
- **Decides.** Whether the show carries one at all; faces-only versus full-body; which episodes get a real pass and which get waved through when the deadline bites.
- **Engine.** A series-level consistency score separate from per-episode quality. The pass costs schedule exactly at peak crunch; skipping it is what produces the notorious off-model episode that outlives the show online.
- **Takes.** 3-7 days per episode

#### Pencil test / line test / rush check

Keys and inbetweens shot to the timesheet and played back as moving line drawings. The only place motion can be judged before money is spent on cleanup, paint, BG and composite.

- **Who.** Episode Director, Animation Director, Line Test Operator
- **Decides.** Accept the motion, add or remove inbetweens, retime the sheet, or redraw the cut; how thoroughly to test when the parcel is already late.
- **Engine.** Motion evaluation of the assembled drawing set against its sheet. Errors caught here cost drawings; the identical error caught after paint and composite costs 5-20x. Test thoroughness is an explicit days-versus-retakes trade.
- **Takes.** Continuous; about a day per parcel

#### Inbetween (douga 動画)

Draws every intermediate drawing between keys in clean single-weight line, on registered paper or digitally, following the chart's spacing exactly. The largest single volume of labour in any hand-drawn film.

- **Who.** Inbetweener (動画マン) / (US) Inbetweener and Breakdown Artist
- **Decides.** Line quality versus speed; how to space a chart the key animator drew loosely; when to query the key animator rather than guess an arc.
- **Engine.** Drawing-count model per cut driving pay and time: ¥200-300 per drawing in Japan; TAG Inbetweener journey $43.67/hr, $1,746.80/wk (2024-27 period 2). Throughput ~10-25 drawings/day (estimate); error rate feeds douga check rejection.
- **Takes.** 3-6 weeks per TV episode; the bulk of a feature

#### Inbetween check (動画検査)

Verifies every drawing against the timesheet — numbering, count, closed lines, correct cel level, registration, nothing missing — before anything is scanned, painted or composited.

- **Who.** Inbetween Checker / (US) Animation Checker
- **Decides.** Pass, fix in-house, or send back; how much to repair silently to protect the schedule versus how much to bounce and enforce quality.
- **Engine.** Per-cut defect list (open line, wrong number, missing drawing, level clash, bad registration). Defects passing this gate resurface as paint leaks and composite errors at far higher cost. Checker throughput is a genuine bottleneck.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days per parcel

#### Clean-up and tie-down (feature model)

On features the rough animation is redrawn on-model in final line by a clean-up team organised per character, each with a lead holding that character across all animators. The finished look lives here.

- **Who.** Clean-up Artist / Assistant Animator / Character Clean-up Lead
- **Decides.** Team size per character; how tight to hold the model; whether to preserve the rough's energy or the model sheet's accuracy when they conflict.
- **Engine.** Per-character clean-up crew with a lead and a volume-control score; roughs-to-clean throughput ratio (several clean-up artists per animator — estimate). A cost line features carry and TV series usually collapse into douga.
- **Takes.** 12-24 months on a feature

#### Effects animation

Draws everything that is not a character: fire, smoke, water, dust, debris, explosions, magic, plus the drawn light-bloom and shadow overlays composite will treat as separate levels.

- **Who.** Effects Animator / Special Effects Artist (特効)
- **Decides.** Drawn versus composited versus 3D; how many FX levels; how many drawings the effect gets; whether the FX carries the shot or supports the character.
- **Engine.** FX as its own drawing set with its own levels and colour separations, drawn by a specialist pool with distinct skill values. FX cuts are disproportionately expensive per second and are the classic source of schedule overrun.
- **Takes.** 1-4 days per cut; a standing team on a feature

#### Scan, trace and digital ink

Paper drawings scanned on peg-registered scanners, line-traced, cleaned of dirt and registered to the sheet. Historically this was hand-inking onto acetate cels, then xerography from around 1959-61.

- **Who.** Scanner Operator / Scan Checker; historically Inker and Xerography Operator
- **Decides.** Scan resolution and line threshold; whether to keep pencil texture; how much dirt cleanup to fund; whether the show is paper or paperless at all.
- **Engine.** Registration accuracy governs jitter; threshold governs line-weight consistency. A paperless route (Harmony, TVPaint, CLIP STUDIO, Stylos, OpenToonz) deletes this stage and swaps its whole error set for a different one.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per episode

#### Per-cut colour designation (色指定)

For each cut assigns which colour set applies — day, night, sunset, firelit, flashback — and specifies any one-off colours that cut alone needs, marked up on a print of the layout.

- **Who.** Colour Designer / Colour Setting Staff
- **Decides.** Which set per cut; when to invent and pay for a new set; whether to fix the colour relationship in composite instead of building a set.
- **Engine.** Per-cut colour-set binding validated against the BG's stated time of day and light source. A mismatch between character set and background lighting is a real, audience-detectable continuity error, not a soft quality score.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per episode

#### Paint / finishing (仕上げ)

Fills every drawing with its designated colours and separations — base, shadow, highlight — in RETAS PaintMan, CLIP STUDIO, Harmony or equivalent. Usually placed with a dedicated outsourced paint house.

- **Who.** Finishing Staff / Digital Painter / Paint Checker; historically the Ink and Paint Department
- **Decides.** In-house or outsourced; the per-drawing rate accepted; how much of the season's paint volume one vendor gets; how deep the check is.
- **Engine.** Per-drawing paint time scaling with separation count; leak and miscolour defect rates; vendor capacity and rate. Second-largest volume cost after inbetweening and the easiest stage in the whole pipeline to offshore.
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks per TV episode

#### Paint check / final check

Every painted drawing checked against the colour model and the sheet for leaks, wrong separations, missing shadow levels and level order, before anything reaches composite.

- **Who.** Paint Checker (仕上検査) / Final Checker and Mark-up
- **Decides.** What reject rate to enforce; whether to spot-check or fully check when the delivery deadline is 48 hours away.
- **Engine.** Defect classes with different downstream costs. An error passed here becomes a composite retake or an on-air defect that the audience screenshots. Check depth is an explicit player dial trading days against visible errors.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per episode

#### Background painting (美術)

Paints the background for every cut from the layout drawing: new paintings, bank pulls, or bank pulls modified for the angle. Frequently split out to a dedicated background studio.

- **Who.** Background Artist / Art Director
- **Decides.** New, bank or modified per cut; which painter gets which BG; level of finish; whether to build an oversize painting so a camera move can pan across it.
- **Engine.** Per-cut BG source and cost; bank hits are near-free and grow with the run; pans and multiplane demand oversize art; per-painter style-match scoring against the art board and against other BGs in the same sequence.
- **Takes.** 3-5 weeks per TV episode

#### 3DCG layer

Models, animates and cel-shades what is cheaper or impossible drawn — vehicles, crowds, complex mechanical props, moving environments — matched to the drawn line and stepped on 2s or 3s to sit with hand-drawn work.

- **Who.** 3DCG Director / CG Animator
- **Decides.** Which assets go 3D; outline width and shading ramp; whether to step the CG to match the drawn frame rate; whether to trace drawn line over the render.
- **Engine.** Per-asset drawn/CG flag with entirely different cost shapes: CG is expensive once and near-free after, drawn is linear forever. A match score against the drawn look that audiences reliably notice when it fails.
- **Takes.** Asset build 2-8 weeks; render days per cut

#### Compositing (撮影)

Assembles every layer of every cut — character levels, BG, FX, CG — and performs the camera: pans, trucks, racks, diffusion, glow, grain, filters, shakes. The final look is manufactured here, after all drawing is finished.

- **Who.** Director of Photography (撮影監督) / Compositor
- **Decides.** Filter and glow strength; depth of field; how the camera move executes; the per-cut grade; how much to fix in composite rather than order a drawing retake.
- **Engine.** Per-cut layer stack with transforms and effect nodes, driven by the timesheet's camera column. Over-processing measurably degrades the line the animators drew; composite can rescue or ruin an otherwise finished cut.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks per TV episode; 1-5 hours per cut

#### In-house retake round

Every composited cut is screened and given a numbered retake list — drawing, colour, BG or composite — routed back to the department that owns the error with a hard turnaround.

- **Who.** Director, Episode Director, Production Assistant, Retake Manager
- **Decides.** Which retakes to call and which to accept given the days remaining; whether to fix it cheaply in composite; whether to shorten or drop the shot instead.
- **Engine.** Retake objects carrying owner department, severity, cost and days. Accepting raises a visible-defect count that feeds reception. Retakes compete for exactly the same people as the next episode's schedule.
- **Takes.** 3-10 days per episode, repeated

#### Overseas package prep and ship

Assembles the animation package the vendor works from: locked boards, exposure sheets, model packs, colour models, BG keys, layouts or workbook, and written notes. Then ships it and runs a kickoff call.

- **Who.** Overseas Coordinator / Production Manager / Line Producer
- **Decides.** How complete a package to send (more domestic prep = fewer retakes but higher home cost); which vendor gets which episode; deadlines, penalties and what rounds are included.
- **Engine.** Package completeness score driving vendor retake rate and delivered quality. Vendor roster with per-minute bid, capacity, house style, timezone lag and reliability history; round-trip latency measured in real days.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks prep per episode

#### Overseas animation production

The vendor executes layout, key animation, inbetweens, paint and often composite from the package at a fixed per-episode or per-minute price, and returns a finished or near-finished episode.

- **Who.** Overseas Studio Supervisor; vendor Animation Director, Inbetweeners, Painters (Korea, Philippines, Vietnam, China, India)
- **Decides.** Which stages the vendor owns versus home; whether to station a supervisor on site; how notes are handled across a 13-16 hour time difference.
- **Engine.** Vendor quality/rate/capacity model with per-stage handoff points. The more stages sent, the cheaper and the less controllable; a vendor's house style leaks into the show visibly, episode to episode.
- **Takes.** 3-9 months per 11- or 22-minute episode, pipelined to deliver weekly

#### Vendor retake cycle

The returned episode is checked shot by shot, a numbered retake list goes back with reference material, the vendor redoes and re-delivers. Rounds are contracted and finite.

- **Who.** Retake Coordinator / Overseas Supervisor / Animation Checker
- **Decides.** How many rounds to buy; which errors justify a round-trip; which to fix at home at higher cost but zero latency; when to stop and ship.
- **Engine.** Retake list items each priced two ways — vendor cost plus latency, or in-house cost plus days. A locked airdate truncates the rounds available; unfixed items go to air and stay in the record.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks per round; typically 2-4 rounds

#### Gross outsourcing of a whole episode (グロス)

An entire episode — board through composite — is handed to another studio for a fixed fee, with only the main show's director and chief AD checking it. Standard practice for keeping a weekly slot fed.

- **Who.** Producer / Production Desk; the receiving vendor studio
- **Decides.** Which episodes to gross out (usually the low-stakes ones); the fee; whether to impose a chief AD pass; which studio, given its style.
- **Engine.** Episode quality is drawn from the vendor's own staff pool, not the show's, producing measurable style discontinuity. Cost becomes fixed and known instead of variable, and frees the home line for a marquee episode.
- **Takes.** Vendor runs 3-5 months in parallel

#### Progress management (制作進行)

Physically moves every cut between 15+ individuals and 5+ companies, chases missing artists, updates the cut table and drives cut bags around the city at night. One PA typically carries a whole episode alone.

- **Who.** Production Assistant (制作進行) / Production Desk
- **Decides.** What to chase first; whom to reassign; when to escalate to the desk; when to tell the director a cut is not going to arrive at all.
- **Engine.** The cut table is core game state; PA attention is a finite per-day resource spread over hundreds of cells. Uncontacted cuts rot silently until the deadline. Reassignment costs days and burns the relationship with the original animator.
- **Takes.** Continuous; 60-100 hour weeks (estimate)

#### Booking freelance key animators

Competes with every other show airing that season for the same finite freelance pool, negotiating per-cut rates, dates and how many cuts each animator will take on this episode.

- **Who.** Animation Producer / Sakuga Desk / Production Desk
- **Decides.** How far above the going per-cut rate to bid; which relationships to spend; whether to overbook against expected dropouts; whether to take a name animator for one showcase cut.
- **Engine.** A season-wide shared pool with commitments, reliability, loyalty and rate memory. Paying over rate poaches from rivals and inflates the market for everyone; overbooked animators deliver late or silently drop cuts.
- **Takes.** Continuous, 2-8 weeks ahead of each episode

#### Afureko (post-record to picture)

The Japanese model: the cast records together in one booth against picture that is part composited cuts, part line test, part still storyboard. Performance and sync are to whatever picture exists on the day.

- **Who.** Sound Director (音響監督), Voice Cast, Recording Engineer
- **Decides.** When to schedule the session against picture state; whether to record to incomplete picture; take selection; ad-lib and crowd (gaya) passes.
- **Engine.** Picture-completeness at session time drives both performance quality and later sync error count. Ensemble recording produces interaction quality that isolated-booth recording cannot; cast rank sets per-episode fee.
- **Takes.** 3-5 hours per episode, weekly

#### Music: BGM menu versus per-episode score

In Japan a BGM menu of roughly 40-80 cues is recorded once before any episode exists, and a music selector places cues per episode. Western TV animation more often scores to picture each episode.

- **Who.** Composer, Music Producer, Music Selector (選曲); (US) Composer and Music Editor
- **Decides.** Menu size and instrumentation; cue placement per scene; whether to commission new cues mid-run; the OP and ED artist, which is a committee revenue decision as much as a creative one.
- **Engine.** Cue library with mood and length tags plus reuse counters, versus per-episode scoring cost. OP/ED song choice routes revenue to a specific committee member and is therefore constrained by the cap table.
- **Takes.** Menu 4-8 weeks once; selection about a day per episode

#### Sound effects, Foley and the mix

Every sound in the film is manufactured — there is no production track of any kind. SFX are cut to picture, Foley performed to picture, then dialogue, effects and music are mixed (Japan calls the mix 'dubbing') to broadcast spec.

- **Who.** Sound Effects Editor (音響効果), Foley Artist, Re-recording Mixer
- **Decides.** Library versus custom-recorded effects; Foley coverage depth; mix balance and loudness target; whether to mix before every cut is final.
- **Engine.** Per-cut sound asset list with library-vs-bespoke cost. Mixing against unfinished picture forces a paid re-mix. Broadcast loudness, stems and M&E deliverables are hard gates that fail delivery, not soft notes.
- **Takes.** SFX 2-3 days; mix about a day per episode

#### Picture edit and conform (編集 / V編)

Drops composited cuts into the animatic as they arrive, trims the assembly to the exact broadcast running length, and builds the master with OP, ED, eyecatch and next-episode preview.

- **Who.** Editor
- **Decides.** What to trim when the episode is long — always finished, fully paid-for frames; where to cut without wrecking the timing; whether to hold a slot for a cut still in composite.
- **Engine.** Timeline with per-cut placeholder state and a hard slot length (e.g. a 22:00 or 23:40 programme spec). Overlength forces destruction of completed work; the slot is a constraint, not a preference.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days per episode, done repeatedly

#### First screening and delivery QC (初号試写 / 納品)

Full-episode screening for director, producer and committee, then technical QC — colour space, loudness, safe areas, flash and photosensitivity limits — and delivery of the broadcast master.

- **Who.** Director, Producer, Broadcast QC / Delivery Coordinator
- **Decides.** Accept, or call last retakes with hours left; whether to ship with known defects and fix them for the home-video release.
- **Engine.** Hard delivery deadline tied to the purchased broadcast slot. The 'fix it for Blu-ray' option creates a real, later, separately budgeted rework pass — a mechanic that exists in no other kind of production.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days, ending 1-7 days before air, often hours

#### Production committee formation (製作委員会)

Four to twelve companies each invest against the window they exploit — print, broadcast, streaming, discs, music, merchandise, games — and co-own copyright pro rata. The studio takes a production fee and often little or no equity.

- **Who.** Producer (studio) with committee members: publisher, broadcaster, record label, video distributor, toy or game company, ad agency
- **Decides.** Whom to invite; how much equity the studio keeps; whether to self-finance; which member holds which right; the size of the per-episode production fee.
- **Engine.** A cap table routing per-window revenue to members by share. The studio's P&L is fee minus cost, so every overrun lands on the studio alone. Members hold veto rights over content touching their window.
- **Takes.** 3-12 months before production starts

#### Slot purchase and airdate lock

In the Japanese model the committee buys the time slot, usually late night, and supplies the programme. The airdate is contractually fixed months before a single frame of the episode exists.

- **Who.** Producer / Ad Agency committee member / Broadcaster
- **Decides.** Which slot, which network, which cour; how many episodes; and crucially how many finished episodes to bank as buffer before episode 1 airs.
- **Engine.** Immutable weekly delivery deadlines. Buffer size is the single most protective decision available and is almost always sacrificed to start airing sooner — that trade is the mechanical origin of the crunch.
- **Takes.** Slot secured 6-12 months ahead

#### Bank and reuse management

Builds reusable finished sequences — transformation sequences, stock attacks, OP and ED, standing BGs, character banks — drawn once at high quality and re-exposed for the rest of the run.

- **Who.** Director / Production Desk / Animation Director
- **Decides.** What to bank; how much to lavish on the bank cut; how often it can be reused before the audience turns on it; whether to cut a shortened variant.
- **Engine.** Bank assets with quality, length and use count. Each reuse costs only composite time; audience fatigue rises with frequency of use. A well-funded bank cut is an explicit long-run cost strategy, not laziness.
- **Takes.** Bank cut 2-4 weeks once; pays back every episode after

#### Cel-era camera shooting (historical mode)

Painted cels are laid on the pegs over the background and photographed frame by frame against the exposure sheet, with pans, trucks, dissolves and multiplane executed mechanically on the stand.

- **Who.** Animation Cameraman on an Oxberry or rostrum stand
- **Decides.** How many cel levels to stack (each level darkens the image); exposure and filters; how the move is cranked; whether to shoot a test first.
- **Engine.** A levels-versus-density tradeoff that caps how many characters can share a frame. Shooting errors are invisible until the lab returns the film days later and cost a full re-shoot. Digital composite deletes this stage from c.1990.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per episode (historical)

---

## 3. Stop-motion and puppet animation (feature and series): armature puppets, replacement/3D-printed faces, clay, miniature sets, frame-by-frame photography

### The unit of work

THE ATOM IS THE FRAME: one still exposure, 1/24 of a second of screen time. Nothing else in the building is a unit.

UNIT OF WORK: seconds of approved footage per animator per week. Verified rates: Aardman averaged 4.2 sec/week per animator on Wallace & Gromit: Vengeance Most Fowl (peak ~5), with 30-35 animators giving just over 2 minutes of film a week; Aardman quotes ~2 sec/day per animator on the Shaun the Sheep features (20-28 animators); Coraline's 28+ animators produced 90-100 seconds of finished animation a week (~3.2-3.6 sec each); Chicken Run's best single day studio-wide in 1999 was 26 seconds. Hard sequences collapse this: Missing Link's elephant walk took nearly three months for roughly 20 seconds.

A GOOD DAY on one unit: 24-60 frames in the can — 1 to 2.5 seconds — with no camera bump, no puppet failure, no face pop and no frame deleted. A GREAT day is finishing a shot (typical shot 3-6 seconds, 72-144 frames, 3-15 days). A BAD day is negative: 400 frames rolled back after an armature joint strips. On twos (12 images/second, Aardman and Isle of Dogs house style) a day yields twice the screen seconds for the same number of exposures.

UNIT OF PROGRESS: shots final against total shots (a feature is ~1,100-1,500; Missing Link was 1,486), and percentage of the animatic replaced by real footage. The shop has its own units: faces printed per week (~2,000 on Missing Link from five printers running 24/7), sets delivered per month, puppet copies delivered per character.

CAPACITY EQUATION: weekly output = active units x seconds per unit per week. A 100-minute film is ~6,000 seconds; 30 units at 4 sec/week = 120 sec/week = 50 perfect weeks, which is why shoots run 12-24 months in practice — Coraline 18 months, Guillermo del Toro's Pinocchio 935 shooting days inside 1,000 production days, Vengeance Most Fowl just over a year. Units are capped not by money but by stages, built sets, duplicate puppets and trained animators.

SCHEDULE SHAPE: development 2-15 years (del Toro's Pinocchio, 15); design and fabrication 12-24 months, overlapping; animatic locked before the shop commits; shoot 12-24 months with units opening in waves as sets and puppets land; VFX/post overlapping the shoot and running 6-12 months past it; total 3-5 years. There is no principal-photography block with a start and an end date the way live action has one — units open and close continuously for two years.

### What is absent

- Locations entirely: no scout, no recce, no permit, no location fee, no locations manager, no unit manager. Geography is an art-department decision and every 'location' is manufactured, so the negotiation is with the model shop, not a landowner.
- Production sound: no mixer, no boom operator, no radio mics, no timecode slate, no room tone, no sync sound of any kind. The stage is silent by design and a frame is a photograph, not a recording.
- ADR: dialogue is recorded before animation, so there is nothing to replace. Later changes are fresh pickup sessions that invalidate finished animation rather than repair it.
- Takes and coverage: there is no second take of a performance and no master-plus-coverage. One angle is one physical shoot lasting days; every additional angle is a separate lock-off, a separate lighting pass and a separate multi-day shoot.
- The shooting day as a resettable unit: no call sheet with a page count, no company move, no wrap-and-reset, no actor turnaround, no six-day week logic. A unit stops mid-shot at the end of the day and the stage stays frozen overnight.
- Weather, daylight, magic hour, cover sets, rain towers, day-for-night. Every light is artificial and permanent; night is a DMX value.
- Actors on set, and everything attached to them: cast holds and availability windows, trailers, hair, makeup and wardrobe for humans, intimacy coordinators, stunt coordinators and performers, armourers, animal wranglers, background/extras casting and crowd ADs.
- Camera operating as a live craft: no focus puller chasing an unpredictable actor, no dolly grip, no Steadicam, no handheld, no drone, no gimbal. Focus is set and frozen for weeks; any camera move is a programmed motion-control table.
- Motion blur. A still exposure of a stationary object has none, so movement that should smear reads as strobing until blur is faked practically or added in post.
- Basecamp, catering on location, transport captain, unit drivers, facility trucks, generators, honeywagons.
- Second unit in the live-action sense. Every stage is a 'unit', but they are all first units shooting principal material with principal puppets.
- Daily rushes of many minutes of material. A day per unit yields 1-2.5 seconds, so there is nothing to screen until a shot is finished.
- Cast insurance and performer risk. The equivalent risk sits on physical assets that wear out: a puppet, not a person, is the thing that can be injured.
- Practical water and fire at true scale: surface tension and flame do not scale down, so real water and real fire are simply unavailable as a shooting option.

### What is reordered

- EDITING COMES FIRST. The film is cut as a full-length animatic timed to recorded dialogue and locked to the frame before the shop commits, because every shot's frame count is a build order, a stage booking and an animator-week. Photography fills slots in an edit that already exists; live action shoots first and finds the film afterwards.
- VOICE PERFORMANCE PRECEDES PHOTOGRAPHY BY A YEAR OR MORE. Actors finish in days (Vengeance Most Fowl gathered 20+ hours of takes) and the selected take becomes immutable law the animation is built to match. The acting a camera records and the acting an audience sees are separated by two years and two different people.
- CASTING EFFECTIVELY PRECEDES DESIGN. The voice shapes the face, and the face has to be sculpted, moulded or printed months before its first shot, so the vocal cast is fixed before the character physically exists.
- FABRICATION STARTS BEFORE THE SCRIPT IS FINAL. A hero puppet is a 16-week (Isle of Dogs) to 6-12 month (Pinocchio) build, so the shop is committed to characters, costumes and props that the script may still be rewriting. Design lock is forced by lead time, not by creative readiness.
- VFX IS SPECIFIED BEFORE THE SHOT IS SHOT, NOT AFTER. The rig plan, face-seam scheme and clean-plate list are agreed at shot launch; post's headcount is set by decisions made on stage weeks earlier. Missing Link removed 1,000+ rigs and had 446 shots that were seam and rig cleanup only.
- LIGHTING AND CAMERA ARE SET ONCE PER SET AND PERSIST FOR WEEKS, across many shots and many days, rather than being struck and relit per setup inside a day. Relighting mid-shot is a continuity break, not a creative option.
- SHOT APPROVAL COMES DAYS TO WEEKS AFTER SHOOTING, not at dailies the next morning. The overnight feedback loop live action relies on closes here only when the shot is finished, which is why a whole week of animation can be wrong.
- PICKUPS AND RESHOOTS RUN THROUGHOUT THE SHOOT rather than after it, and a 'reshoot' is a full re-animation from frame one against a set that may already have been struck.
- SET STRIKE IS A SCHEDULING EVENT, NOT A WRAP EVENT. Stages recycle continuously for two years; the decision to strike a set is the decision to make its reshoots cost a rebuild.
- SOUND DESIGN, FOLEY, SCORE AND MIX ARE THE ONLY PART OF THE PIPELINE IN THE NORMAL ORDER — last, after picture. Everything upstream of them is inverted.

### How the crew differs

- PRESENT HERE, ABSENT IN LIVE ACTION - Stop-Motion Animator: performer and camera operator at once. Owns one shot alone for days or weeks, works to an exposure sheet, and is the only person permitted to touch the puppet. Nobody can take over a shot in progress and make it match.
- Animation Supervisor / Supervising Animator (Brad Schiff, Will Becher, Brian Leif Hansen): casts animators to shots by temperament, and polices performance consistency across 30-90 units that never see each other's work.
- Head of Puppetry / Director of Character Fabrication (Georgina Hayns at Laika, Andy Gent at Arch Model): runs a factory of 70-100 sculptors, mould makers, armature machinists, casters, painters, costumiers and hair artists. There is no live-action department that manufactures the cast.
- Armature Designer and Armature Machinist: precision metalwork, CNC and increasingly 3D-printed metal. A pure engineering job with no live-action equivalent.
- Mould Maker, Silicone Technician, Foam Technician, Puppet Paint and Finish Artist, Hair Punching Artist: the skeleton, flesh, skin, makeup and hair of a performer who is a manufactured object.
- Miniature Costume Fabricator and specialist knitter: Coraline's knitter worked on needles finer than a pin at roughly three weeks per sweater. Wardrobe here is a textile-engineering problem, not a shopping problem.
- Rapid Prototype Supervisor, Facial Animation Designer, Print Technician, Face Finisher (Brian McLean's department at Laika, which won a Sci-Tech Award for Coraline): a digital-to-physical parts factory producing tens of thousands of faces per film.
- Puppet Hospital Technician / Puppet Wrangler: hair, makeup, wardrobe and surgery for a cast that physically deteriorates with every frame it is touched, working nights between shooting days.
- Stage Coordinator (Laika's hub is nicknamed 'The Glove'): traffic control for 40-91 simultaneous units, each holding a stage, a set, a puppet copy, an animator and a camera.
- Rigging department and Motion Control department as separate disciplines: Aardman's eleven teams on Vengeance Most Fowl were production, story, editorial, puppets, art, animation, rigging, camera, lighting, motion control and VFX.
- Model Makers and Miniature Scenic Artists: they build the world rather than dress a found one, including the same location at several scales.
- A permanent VFX paint and roto team whose entire job exists because of choices made on stage: removing face seams and rigs from frames that were shot with them deliberately.
- Animatic Editor, working a year before the first exposure, and a permanent Archivist, because the assets are physical and perishable.
- ABSENT: First and Second Assistant Director. There is no shooting day to run and no company to move; scheduling lives on the board with the stage coordinator and the animation producer.
- ABSENT: Script Supervisor. Continuity is the exposure sheet, the onion skin and the frame log, held by the animator alone.
- ABSENT: Production Sound Mixer, Boom Operator, Sound Utility. No sync sound exists anywhere in the process.
- ABSENT: Locations Manager, Location Scout, Unit Manager, Transport Captain, drivers, basecamp and location catering.
- ABSENT: Focus Puller, Dolly Grip, Steadicam and drone operators, Key Grip in the live sense. Focus is set and frozen; any move is a stepper-motor table.
- ABSENT: Hair, Makeup and Costume for human actors; Stunt Coordinator and stunt performers; Armourer; Animal Wrangler; Background Casting and crowd ADs. Every one of these has a fabrication-department counterpart instead.
- ABSENT: Camera Operator as a distinct craft. The animator triggers the exposure, the DP owns lighting and lens, and a moving camera belongs to the motion-control technician.
- ABSENT: ADR Supervisor in the usual sense. Dialogue is recorded first, so post records new lines rather than replacing old ones.
- ABSENT: Standby art department. The art department here is a manufacturer with a delivery schedule, not a crew standing by to adjust a real room.

### What goes wrong

- A puppet breaks mid-shot. An armature ball joint strips or loosens, a wire armature fatigues and snaps, a silicone seam tears at the shoulder, a foam latex part crumbles - 400 frames into a shot that has taken eight days. The stage stays frozen while someone decides between repair in place, swapping to an identical copy and re-matching the pose by eye, rolling back, or scrapping the shot.
- Someone bumps the set, the camera or the table. Everything shot so far jumps. There is no re-take of the earlier frames - only comp, or starting again.
- The animator cannot undo. Deleting a frame does not restore the puppet; the pose has to be physically rebuilt from onion skin, and a failed restore turns a one-frame mistake into a rollback.
- A lamp ages or drifts and the shot flickers. Nobody sees it until the frames are played at speed days later.
- A stepper motor loses steps and the motion-control move drifts, so the clean plate no longer lines up with the animation pass.
- A replacement face fails to seat. Worn magnets or pins let faces sit fractionally off, and the character's face pops between frames - visible only on playback, invisible on stage.
- The face print queue stalls. A printer goes down and a unit sits idle with an animator, a stage and a lit set waiting for parts (Missing Link needed ~2,000 faces a week from five printers running around the clock).
- A foam latex batch fails or oxidises. Every part cast that day is unusable, which can ground a character across every unit shooting it.
- Gravity and material relaxation. Silicone creeps, a raised arm sags a millimetre between exposures, and a held pose drifts across a night of downtime.
- Heat. Lights soften Plasticine and silicone, warp set pieces, and make clay go shiny; the longer the shot, the worse the state of the puppet.
- Handling wear. Paint rubs through, fingerprints mark clay, fur lifts and boils, a costume creeps - all of it accumulating over a shot that takes two weeks.
- Duplicates that do not match. Two copies of the same character painted in different batches cut against each other, and the mismatch only appears when the shots are assembled months later.
- Consumable sets and props. A set that burns, floods or collapses can be shot once; get the take wrong and the shop rebuilds it before anyone can try again.
- A rig that cannot be removed. A support crossing glass, water, a reflection or fur is un-paintable, and the shot has to be re-rigged and re-shot.
- The shot only reveals itself at speed. Weeks of work can turn out to be too slow, too small, off-model or simply unfunny at the moment of first playback, long after the decisions were made.
- Continuity across units. Two animators on two stages shooting the same scene three weeks apart deliver two different performances of the same character, and only the supervisor's memory connects them.
- Losing an animator mid-shot. Illness or departure orphans a half-finished shot that nobody else can match; in practice it is restarted.
- Vibration from outside: a lorry, a slammed door, construction on the next stage, footsteps on a shared floor. Every unit in the building can be hit at once.
- Power blips and camera sleep, which change exposure or drop a frame in the middle of an otherwise perfect day.
- Story change after the build. A rewrite that adds a location or a character means a 16-week to 12-month fabrication lead before anyone can shoot it, so scripts are effectively frozen by the shop's calendar.
- Stereo desync: on a native-3D show a bump between the left-eye and right-eye exposures ruins the pair and cannot be fixed by re-shooting one eye.
- Scale betrayal: water that will not behave, flame that will not scale, cloth that reads as sailcloth, texture with the wrong grain - failures that look like bad filmmaking but are physics.
- Set struck too early. A reshoot ordered after a stage has been cleared costs a full rebuild, so the strike decision is a bet on the cut.

### The shape of the money

Money buys years of resident labour inside a permanent building, not day-rates on location. Verified budgets: Coraline, ParaNorman, The Boxtrolls and Kubo and the Two Strings each around $60M; Isle of Dogs $35M; Missing Link reported around $100M; The Nightmare Before Christmas $18M in 1993; Chicken Run in the mid-$40Ms (estimate). Crew sizes: Pinocchio 357 people over 1,000 production days; Vengeance Most Fowl 200+; Isle of Dogs 70-100 in the puppet shop alone.

PROPORTIONAL SPLIT for a mid-size feature (ESTIMATE, inferred from department sizes and published production data rather than released budget top sheets): art and fabrication 25-35% - puppets, armatures, faces and the print farm, sets, props, costume, hair - the single largest block and the one with no live-action analogue at this weight; animation labour 20-30%; camera, lighting, rigging, motion control and stage operations 8-12%; VFX and post 12-20%, rising every year as CG extension and seam/rig cleanup grow (Missing Link touched nearly all 1,486 shots and rendered for 112 million processor hours); editorial, story and development 6-10%; music, sound and mix 3-5%; voice cast 2-5%, a fraction of live-action cast cost because even a major star records in five to fifteen days; facility overhead, insurance and bond the remainder.

HOW THAT DIFFERS. (1) There is no cast-cost cliff: names are cheap here, so star power does not distort the budget the way it does in live action. (2) There are no location fees, no weather days, no cast overtime and no catering-on-location; instead the facility burns continuously for 2-4 years whether or not a frame is shot. (3) The marginal cost of screen time is close to linear - seconds = animators x weeks x ~4 sec/week - so a picture is made cheaper by being shorter in seconds, never by being faster in days. (4) Money converts to speed only through units, and a unit needs a stage, a built set and a duplicate puppet, so cash cannot outrun the shop: Pinocchio was bid at 48 sets and delivered 99, which is a schedule problem before it is a money problem. (5) Cost is front-loaded and sunk: a scene cut after its sets and puppets are built is a total write-off, which is precisely why the animatic locks before fabrication starts. (6) Post is not a tail, it is a parallel standing department funded from day one, because every frame shot creates cleanup work. (7) Labour terms differ from a live-action crew: people are staff or long-term contract rather than dailies, so attrition, training and holiday - not turnaround penalties - are the labour risks. The Animation Guild (IATSE Local 839) master agreement covers Los Angeles animation; Portland, Bristol and other shops sit on different terms (estimate).

### Processes that exist only here

#### Character design lock and buildability review

Every design is frozen and audited for fabrication: can this silhouette take an armature, can that hair be punched, does the costume read at six inches, where does the face split. Nothing enters the shop until it survives this.

- **Who.** Production Designer / Character Designer
- **Decides.** Which designs lock now and which wait; what gets simplified to be buildable; head-to-body proportion; whether the face is replacement, mechanical, clay or hybrid.
- **Engine.** Per-design buildability vector (armature volume, undercuts, hair count, cloth scale) tested against shop capability. Lock emits a bill of materials, a build-time estimate and a first-shoot date. Unlocking later voids cut parts and printed faces at a stated cost.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks per principal; lock lands 12-18 months before that character shoots

#### Maquette sculpt and turnaround approval

A maquette is sculpted to final scale and approved from every angle. It becomes the master from which moulds, armature envelopes, printed face geometry and costume patterns are all derived.

- **Who.** Lead Character Sculptor
- **Decides.** Sculpt revisions and final proportions; which details survive at scale; where the face split line falls; digital or physical master.
- **Engine.** The maquette is the single source asset every downstream part references, versioned on approval. A post-approval change fans out rework costs and separate lead times to moulds, armature, faces, costume and hair.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks per character, several rounds

#### Scale decision

One number sets everything. Puppet height (Wallace is just over 6 in; Mr Link 16 in; Laika shrank Missing Link's puppets ~20% to save stage space) fixes set footprint, lens, depth of field, light levels and whether an animator can reach the middle of the set.

- **Who.** Director of Photography (Animation) + Production Designer
- **Decides.** Show scale and per-sequence exceptions; which sets get built twice at two scales; oversize heads for extreme close-ups (Kubo's 200% face, 4.5 in across).
- **Engine.** Global scale drives set area = world area x scale squared, hence build cost and stage-days. Depth-of-field model ties f-stop to exposure time and lamp output. An animator reach envelope gates which staging positions are animatable at all.
- **Takes.** Decided in pre-production; irreversible once sets are cut

#### Armature engineering and machining

Stainless ball-and-socket armatures are designed and milled per character — joint count, joint tension, tie-down threads in the feet, hidden rig points. Wire armatures for cheap and background puppets; Pinocchio used 3D-printed metal for load-bearing parts.

- **Who.** Armature Designer / Armature Machinist
- **Decides.** Ball-and-socket versus wire versus hybrid; tension per joint; number and placement of tie-down threads and rig points; size of the first batch versus later duplicates.
- **Engine.** Armature holds joint count, tension, a fatigue counter per joint and cycles-to-failure. Wire armatures carry a hard frame budget before fracture. Tension decays with use and feeds pose-creep probability on every frame.
- **Takes.** 6-12 weeks for the first armature; batched duplicates after

#### Mechanical head and face registration design

The head is engineered as a machine: coded magnets or pins that locate replacement faces repeatably, internal paddles for eye darts and blinks, hidden access for brow and cheek mechanics (Isle of Dogs dogs were mechanical, its humans were replacement).

- **Who.** Mechanical Designer / Head of Puppetry
- **Decides.** Replacement faces versus mechanical versus clay; how the face splits (full face, mouth only, brow only); magnet coding so a face cannot seat wrong; access hatches.
- **Engine.** Head stores a registration spec and a wear counter; registration wear raises the per-frame chance of a visible face pop. The split scheme sets how many seam pixels post must paint out on every single frame of that character.
- **Takes.** 8-16 weeks to prove the first head

#### Digital face library build

Faces are modelled and blended in Maya into a library of mouth and brow shapes, then converted to printable registered parts. Kubo's own face carried 11,007 mouths and 4,429 brows: over 48 million combinations.

- **Who.** Facial Animation Designer (Rapid Prototype)
- **Decides.** Library size per character; a fixed phoneme kit versus bespoke faces generated per shot (Missing Link was the first Laika film with custom faces for every character in every shot); which characters get which treatment.
- **Engine.** Face library is an indexed catalogue (character, mouth id, brow id, scale). Each shot consumes a sequence of ids. Bespoke mode mints new ids on demand and bills print time and lead. Library size sets both cost and expressive range.
- **Takes.** Months to build; extended continuously through the shoot

#### Face print farm scheduling

Full-colour resin printers run around the clock turning approved face data into physical parts. Missing Link printed 106,000+ faces, roughly 2,000 a week from five printers running 24/7; Coraline printed 6,333 faces for ~207,000 expressions.

- **Who.** Rapid Prototype Supervisor
- **Decides.** Queue priority against shooting dates; tray packing; how many duplicates of each face; printer, resolution and material per batch.
- **Engine.** Printer pool with throughput, uptime and a failure rate; a queue with due dates tied to shot start. A missed due date idles a whole unit. Per-face print hours, cost and scrap rate; a printer down for a week is a face famine across units.
- **Takes.** Hours per tray; 1-3 weeks from approval to shelf

#### Face finish, colour match and inventory

Printed faces are deburred, seam-sanded, checked against the colour master and shelved in coded trays. On Missing Link 90% needed no sanding; earlier powder-printed generations had to be hand-painted one by one.

- **Who.** Paint and Finish Artist / Face Wrangler
- **Decides.** Accept, rework or scrap each print; colour batch tolerance; how trays are issued to and recovered from units.
- **Engine.** Per-face QC pass rate and finishing minutes. A colour-batch id must match within a shot and across a scene or the mismatch lands in the cut. Issue/return inventory per unit with loss, damage and theft-by-pocket.
- **Takes.** Minutes per face at volume; a standing department

#### Mould making and silicone skin casting

Multi-part moulds are cut from the maquette and skins cast in platinum silicone over the armature, then seamed, tinted and flocked. Modern Aardman puppets are silicone made to look like Plasticine, with clay only at the mouths and faces.

- **Who.** Mould Maker / Silicone Technician
- **Decides.** Mould split lines, which become the puppet's visible seams and its stress points; silicone hardness; cored or solid; casts per mould before it degrades.
- **Engine.** Mould carries a cast-count life and each cast rolls for defects. Shore hardness trades pose-holding against tear risk. Seam positions map to named stress points that fail under specific pose classes during animation.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks per character to first good cast

#### Foam latex run

Foam latex is whipped, injected, cured and baked for lightweight bodies and creature parts — cheap, light, and organic. It oxidises, goes brittle, smells of ammonia and corrodes the metal it sits on.

- **Who.** Foam Technician
- **Decides.** Foam versus silicone per part; batch size; when to re-run parts mid-shoot before the existing ones crumble.
- **Engine.** Foam parts carry an age clock and a handling counter; past a threshold, crumble events become likely mid-shot. A failed batch invalidates every part cast that day, which can ground a character across every unit shooting it.
- **Takes.** A day per bake run; parts age out in roughly 4-12 months

#### Miniature costume fabrication

Costumes are made at 1/6 scale or smaller, where ordinary cloth reads as canvas. Coraline's sweaters were knitted on needles finer than a pin at about three weeks a garment, and 30 identical sets of her pyjamas were made.

- **Who.** Puppet Costume Supervisor / Miniature Knitter
- **Decides.** Fabric weight and weave per garment; number of identical copies; whether cloth is wired, glued, or left free to move and boil.
- **Engine.** Garment holds a weave-scale value (wrong weave is visible on camera), a copy count and a wear counter. Free cloth adds per-frame settle time and boil risk; wiring removes boil but costs animation minutes every frame.
- **Takes.** 1-4 weeks per hero garment; 10-30 copies per principal

#### Hair punching and fur taming

Hair is punched strand by strand or fur is laid and stiffened. Isle of Dogs used dyed alpaca, mohair and merino held with medical adhesive over foam bodies. Loose hair boils between frames unless gelled, waxed or wired.

- **Who.** Hair and Fur Artist
- **Decides.** Punch, wig or sculpted hair; how much movement to permit; gel and wax treatment; whether boil is a defect or the house look (Isle of Dogs kept it as 'the Trash Island wind').
- **Engine.** Per-puppet hair state with a boil coefficient. Treatment lowers boil but raises reset seconds per frame. Deliberate boil is a style flag the audience model reads as texture rather than error.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks per head; retreated between shots

#### Multiples plan

How many identical puppets exist decides how many units can shoot that character at once. Isle of Dogs built 1,105 animatable puppets plus 2,000 background figures; a hero pack took about 16 weeks.

- **Who.** Puppet Fabrication Supervisor / Senior Puppet Coordinator
- **Decides.** Duplicates per character and their delivery dates; hero, stunt, rigged, half-body and scale variants; which copy goes to which unit and which is held back as insurance.
- **Engine.** Puppet inventory with per-copy condition. A scene cannot be split across more units than there are copies. Delivery dates gate shooting dates. Copies drift in paint and hair and must be matched or the drift shows across a cut.
- **Takes.** 16 weeks to a first hero puppet; 6-12 months for a full family

#### Puppet trials

Before sign-off, animators shoot test footage with the new puppet: walk, run, sit, talk, extremes. Trials expose joints that pop, skins that crease, faces that will not seat and costumes that fight the pose.

- **Who.** Animation Supervisor
- **Decides.** Accept, reject or accept with a documented workaround; which poses are forbidden; how this character will be animated and by whom.
- **Engine.** Trial output is a per-character difficulty profile — frames per hour, failure rate by pose class, face-change cost — that feeds every later shot bid. A failed trial pushes the character's first shooting date and idles booked units.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks per character

#### Set design at scale and access engineering

Sets are designed around the animator, not the actor: wild walls, lift-out floors, hatches underneath for tie-down nuts, isolated platforms at waist height, and a footprint that suits both the lens and a human arm.

- **Who.** Art Director / Set Designer
- **Decides.** Footprint and height; which walls fly; drilled tie-down grid versus steel floor for magnets; where the animator stands and what they must reach without touching anything else.
- **Engine.** Set holds an access map (reachable volumes, removable pieces) and a tie-down grid. Camera positions that need a wall removed break the lock-off and cost setup time. Unreachable staging forces a rig, a redesign or a lost shot.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks design per set

#### Set fabrication and stage occupancy

Sets are built and handed to a unit that then owns that stage for weeks. Coraline ran 130+ sets across 52 stages in 183,000 sq ft; Missing Link built 110 sets for 65 locations; Pinocchio was bid at 48 sets and delivered 99.

- **Who.** Set Fabrication Supervisor / Model Maker
- **Decides.** Build order against the shooting board; permanent versus strike-and-rebuild; how many stages to open at once; what gets bought versus made (almost nothing can be bought).
- **Engine.** Stage is a scarce resource with an occupancy calendar; a set holds it from dress to strike. Weekly build capacity caps how fast new units can open. Rebuilding a struck set costs a full build again.
- **Takes.** 4-12 weeks per set; Coraline's largest ran 60 ft

#### Scenic surfacing and dirty-down

Every surface is painted and textured to read at miniature scale: grain, sheen and dirt all have to shrink with the object. Wrong texture scale is the fastest way to make an expensive set look like a model.

- **Who.** Scenic Artist / Surfacer
- **Decides.** Texture scale per surface; sheen under hot miniature lights; how much dirty-down; painted forced-perspective cheats.
- **Engine.** Per-surface texture-scale error feeding a 'reads as a model' score judged by the audience model rather than a pass/fail gate. Sheen interacts with the lighting rig to throw hotspots that only appear once the rig is lit.
- **Takes.** 1-4 weeks per set, overlapping build

#### Multi-scale doubles and forced perspective

The same location is built at more than one scale: a full set for dialogue, a smaller miniature for wides, an oversize partial for extreme close-ups. Isle of Dogs fabricated puppets in five sizes to match.

- **Who.** Model Shop Supervisor
- **Decides.** Which shots need which scale; where to cut between scales; forced-perspective build versus CG extension.
- **Engine.** A location is a family of builds keyed by scale, each with its own cost, stage and lighting rig. A cut between scales must match lens, light direction and texture scale or the audience model flags it. Scale mismatch is a visible defect.
- **Takes.** Each scale is a separate build

#### Animatable props and mechanisms

Props must be posable frame by frame: geared vehicles that roll a fixed distance per turn, bendable food, hinged books, and multiples of anything that breaks, because there is only ever one take.

- **Who.** Prop Fabricator / Mechanical Engineer
- **Decides.** Animatable versus static; gearing so one turn equals a known frame increment; how many copies of each destructible prop.
- **Engine.** Prop carries an increment spec (units moved per frame step) and a multiples count. Destructive props are consumed on use; running out halts the shot until the shop delivers, which is measured in days not hours.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks per prop

#### Storyboard to animatic lock

The film is cut before it exists. Boards are timed against the recorded dialogue into a full-length animatic and locked to the frame, because every shot's length is simultaneously a build order and a schedule.

- **Who.** Story Artist / Animatic Editor
- **Decides.** Shot count and the exact frame length of every shot; what is cut before it is built; a feature runs roughly 1,100-1,500 shots (Missing Link: 1,486).
- **Engine.** The animatic is the master shot list. Each shot's frame count converts directly into animator-weeks, face consumption, stage-days and set requirements. Changing a length after launch re-costs everything downstream of it.
- **Takes.** 6-18 months, revised continuously

#### Layout and set footprint derivation

Every shot is built in 3D first with real lens data and puppet proxies, to prove the camera can physically be where the board says, that the set is only as large as it needs to be, and that the move is riggable.

- **Who.** Layout Supervisor / Previs Artist
- **Decides.** Lens, camera height and path per shot; how much set to build and what becomes CG extension; whether the shot needs motion control at all.
- **Engine.** Layout emits a per-shot spec — lens, camera path, required set volume, rig class — that the physical build and motion control must satisfy. An impossible camera position is caught here or costs a rebuild on stage.
- **Takes.** Days per shot, months per film

#### Voice record and performance selection

The entire vocal performance is recorded before a frame is shot, in a handful of sessions, then becomes fixed law. Vengeance Most Fowl banked over 20 hours of dialogue takes; a star's whole contribution can be five to fifteen days.

- **Who.** Director / Voice Director
- **Decides.** The final take of every line; rewrites while the actor is still in the room; whether to book pickup sessions and what they will cost downstream.
- **Engine.** Selected takes are immutable inputs with waveform timing. Changing a selected line after its shot is animated invalidates the shot entirely. Actor availability is measured in days, so it constrains almost nothing else in the schedule.
- **Takes.** 3-15 days per principal, sessions spread across the shoot

#### Track read and X-sheet breakdown

The chosen dialogue is broken down frame by frame onto an exposure sheet: which phoneme, which accent, which breath lands on which of the 24 frames in that second. The animator shoots to that sheet.

- **Who.** Stop-Motion Animator / Track Reader
- **Decides.** Phoneme mapping to mouth or face ids; where to anticipate and where to hold; which accents drive the body rather than the face.
- **Engine.** The X-sheet is a per-frame table binding frame number to face id, audio sample and camera step — the shot's executable score. Face ids on the sheet become the physical pull list from the face trays.
- **Takes.** Hours to a day per shot

#### Shot bid and animator casting

Each shot is bid in stage-days before launch: frame count, characters in frame, face-change density, rig complexity and camera move produce a number of days and a named animator who will live with it.

- **Who.** Animation Producer / Production Manager
- **Decides.** Days allowed; which animator gets it (comedy, weight, subtlety and speed are different animators); whether to split the shot; the reshoot allowance.
- **Engine.** days = frames / (animator rate x difficulty), where difficulty combines character count, rig class, face changes per frame and set access. Variance is tracked per animator; a shot running over its bid triggers a rescue decision.
- **Takes.** Bid in hours; the shot then runs days to weeks

#### The board: unit allocation

One physical board maps every shot to a stage, a set, a puppet copy, an animator and a camera rig, tied together with string. Pinocchio peaked near 60 units with 40 animators; Missing Link ran 91; Aardman runs 40-plus live stages.

- **Who.** Stage Coordinator / Scheduling Coordinator
- **Decides.** How many units to open; which shots run in parallel; which unit gets the only copy of a puppet; what waits and for how long.
- **Engine.** Constraint solver over five scarce pools — stages, built sets, puppet copies, animators, camera and motion-control rigs. Opening a unit consumes one of each. Every unresolved conflict idles a paid crew and a built stage.
- **Takes.** Continuous; replanned weekly across 12-24 months

#### Shot launch

A shot is launched at a meeting on the stage where everyone agrees the performance intent, the lighting, the rig plan and exactly what post will be asked to remove. After it, the animator is alone for days.

- **Who.** Director + Animation Supervisor + VFX Supervisor + DP
- **Decides.** Performance intent and beats; approved rigs and where they may sit; practical versus CG; the point at which the shot would be abandoned.
- **Engine.** Launch writes an immutable shot contract (intent, rig allowance, VFX promises, frame count). The animator's later choices are scored against it, and post-hoc changes to the contract are the dominant source of reshoots.
- **Takes.** 30-90 minutes per shot

#### Set dress, camera lock-off and stage discipline

Camera, lights and set are locked mechanically and the stage becomes a no-touch zone for weeks. People walk carefully, doors are damped, nothing is leaned on. One bump destroys every frame shot so far.

- **Who.** Camera Assistant / Stage Technician
- **Decides.** Lock-off method; whether to float the floor; access rules and who may enter; where the animator's body and elbows may go.
- **Engine.** Stage holds a vibration risk value from neighbouring builds, traffic and door events. A bump event offsets camera or set by a measurable amount; the shot then restarts, rolls back, or goes to comp as a fix with a cost.
- **Takes.** Half a day to two days per setup

#### Miniature lighting and DMX programming

Small sources, fibre optics and flags light a set the size of a table, then the rig is frozen for the life of the shot. Cues run as DMX from the capture software so lights change by frame and work lights kill themselves at exposure.

- **Who.** Gaffer / Lighting Technician
- **Decides.** Fixture choice and position; whether light changes across the shot; DMX cue curves; how much heat the set, clay and silicone can take.
- **Engine.** Per-set lighting rig with a per-frame DMX program (up to 512 channels). Lamp output drifts with age, producing flicker only visible on playback. Heat accumulates and softens clay and silicone. Relighting mid-shot breaks continuity.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per set; cues programmed per shot

#### Exposure and lens strategy

Each frame is a long still exposure on a stills body — Aardman shoots Canon DSLRs through original 35mm prime lenses — often stopped well down for depth, with no motion blur unless it is manufactured.

- **Who.** Director of Photography (Animation)
- **Decides.** Stop and exposure time per frame; ones, twos, or Aardman's 'one-and-a-half' compromise for camera moves; practical blur versus post blur; body and glass per unit.
- **Engine.** Exposure model links f-stop, exposure seconds, lamp output and depth of field. Shooting on twos halves frames and cost but changes motion feel and forbids fast action. Absent blur is a look value the audience model reads.
- **Takes.** Set per show, adjusted per set

#### Motion control programming and move test

Camera moves are stepped between frames by motorised rigs driven from the capture software, and can repeat exactly for multiple passes. Missing Link ran 47 motion-control rigs, the oldest dating from 1928.

- **Who.** Motion Control Technician / Engineer
- **Decides.** Whether the shot moves at all; move shape and easing; how many repeat passes to program; which rig can physically reach the move.
- **Engine.** A move is a per-frame position table per axis at up to 16 axes. Steppers can lose steps, giving cumulative drift. Repeatability is what makes clean plates and element passes possible. A move adds setup days and blocks animator access.
- **Takes.** Half a day to several days to program and test

#### Stereo double-exposure pass

For a native stereo release every frame is shot twice: expose, slide the camera a programmed interocular, expose again. Coraline did this for the whole film, with the offset keyed to the characters' own eye spacing.

- **Who.** Stereographer / Camera Assistant
- **Decides.** Interocular and convergence per shot; native capture versus post conversion; which shots carry depth emphasis.
- **Engine.** Stereo roughly doubles exposures and per-frame time, halving a unit's seconds per week. Any bump between the paired exposures desyncs the eyes irreparably. Interocular is a per-shot value read as depth comfort or eye strain.
- **Takes.** Roughly doubles per-frame time for the whole show

#### Rig design and fit-up

Anything not standing on its own feet is held: threaded tie-downs through the floor, rare-earth magnets (standard sets hold about 10 kg), brass rod rigs, and aerial rigs for jumps and flight, all painted for easy removal.

- **Who.** Rigging Supervisor / Rigger
- **Decides.** Tie-down versus magnet versus aerial rig; where the rig enters frame; whether it will be painted out or hidden practically.
- **Engine.** Rig class carries hold strength, animator seconds per frame and a comp-removal cost. Magnets fail above a mass threshold. A rig crossing a transparent, reflective or moving element cannot be cleanly painted out and forces redesign.
- **Takes.** Hours to days per shot; sequence rigs built to order

#### Video reference and acting pass

The animator performs the shot themselves on video, repeatedly, before touching the puppet; on Pinocchio animators filmed live-action reference for every scene. This is where the performance is actually decided.

- **Who.** Stop-Motion Animator
- **Decides.** Acting choices, timing, where the beats land, what the body does while the face talks, how much to exaggerate for a 9-inch performer.
- **Engine.** Reference yields a timing curve the animator then approximates in discrete physical steps; reference quality feeds the shot's performance score. Time spent here is time not shooting and must come out of the same bid.
- **Takes.** 1-4 hours per shot

#### Pop-through and blocking test

Key poses are set, photographed and played back as a rough pass so the supervisor can approve the shape of the performance before the animator commits to shooting it straight through.

- **Who.** Stop-Motion Animator + Animation Supervisor
- **Decides.** Proceed, re-block, or take it back to the director; pose spacing; how many frames each beat gets.
- **Engine.** A cheap sampled preview creating an approval gate. Catching a problem here costs hours; catching the same problem after the shot is finished costs days to weeks. Gate pass rate scales with supervisor attention, which is finite.
- **Takes.** 2-6 hours per shot

#### Frame-by-frame shooting

The shot is animated straight ahead, one frame at a time, with onion skin and the X-sheet: roughly 4.2 seconds a week per animator on Vengeance Most Fowl, about 2 seconds a day on the Shaun the Sheep features.

- **Who.** Stop-Motion Animator
- **Decides.** Every increment of every joint on every frame; when a frame is good enough to move on; when to stop for the night, leaving the shot half-made and the stage frozen.
- **Engine.** The frame is the atom. Per-frame seconds drawn from the character difficulty profile. No rewind: deleting frames requires physically restoring the puppet, which rolls against a restore tolerance. Progress is monotonic or negative.
- **Takes.** 3-15 days for a typical 3-6 second shot

#### Face change choreography

On a replacement-face show the animator pulls a numbered face from the tray and seats it for each frame, logging every change. A mis-seated or mis-numbered face is invisible on stage and obvious at 24 frames a second.

- **Who.** Stop-Motion Animator / Face Wrangler
- **Decides.** Which face id on which frame; how faces blend across a beat; when the library will not do and a bespoke face must be printed.
- **Engine.** Per-frame face id from the X-sheet, checked against tray inventory. Wrong id or worn registration produces a pop detectable only on playback. Bespoke requests re-enter the print queue with a lead time that can stall the shot.
- **Takes.** Seconds to minutes per frame, continuously

#### Clay reshaping and replacement mouths

In the Aardman tradition mouths and brows are reshaped in Plasticine by hand each frame, or swapped from a set of sculpted replacement mouths, under lights that soften the clay and hands that mark it.

- **Who.** Stop-Motion Animator (clay)
- **Decides.** Reshape versus replace; how far to push an expression; when the clay is too tired, too shiny or too warm and must be replaced mid-shot.
- **Engine.** Clay state degrades with handling and heat (fingerprints, sheen, sag) and needs refreshing; refreshing risks a visible continuity jump. Higher per-frame cost than a face swap, but no print pipeline, no seam, no post cleanup.
- **Takes.** Adds minutes per frame

#### Frame continuity policing

Between every exposure the animator checks the new frame against the last: onion skin for drift, surface gauges for reference, fingerprints, lifted fur, a costume that has crept, a hand that has sagged under gravity.

- **Who.** Stop-Motion Animator
- **Decides.** Accept the frame, redo the pose, or stop and call the puppet department; how much drift is tolerable at this shot size.
- **Engine.** Per-frame defect roll driven by material state, rig class, heat and handling. Undetected defects accumulate into a shot quality score revealed only at playback. Checking costs seconds per frame against the day's output.
- **Takes.** Continuous, seconds per frame

#### Mid-shot puppet failure and recovery

A joint strips, a wire snaps, a silicone seam tears or a foam part crumbles 400 frames into a shot. The unit stops, the stage stays frozen, and someone decides whether the shot survives.

- **Who.** Puppet Wrangler / Puppet Hospital Technician
- **Decides.** Repair in place, swap to an identical copy and re-match the pose, roll back to the last good frame, or scrap the shot and relaunch it from zero.
- **Engine.** Per-frame failure roll from joint fatigue, material age and pose stress. Repair-in-place rolls against a pose-match tolerance. A swap needs an idle identical copy. A failed match forces rollback of N frames or a full reshoot at full cost.
- **Takes.** Minutes to days; a scrapped shot costs its whole bid again

#### Puppet hospital

A standing department repairs, repaints, re-hairs and re-costumes puppets returning from units each night — hair, makeup, wardrobe and surgery for a cast that physically wears out with every frame it is touched.

- **Who.** Puppet Hospital Technician
- **Decides.** Which puppets are serviced tonight; which copies are pulled from service or retired; what gets cannibalised for parts.
- **Engine.** Per-copy condition vector (paint, joints, skin, costume, hair) degrading per frame animated. Service restores partially and costs the unit a night. Retired copies permanently cut how many units can shoot that character in parallel.
- **Takes.** Nightly; a full refurbish takes days

#### Multi-pass and clean plate capture

With the camera locked or under motion control, extra passes are shot for post: an empty clean plate with puppets and rigs removed, matte passes, lighting elements, sometimes each character separately.

- **Who.** Camera Assistant / VFX Supervisor
- **Decides.** Which passes and in what order; plate before or after the animation; how much to hand to comp rather than solve on stage.
- **Engine.** Each pass costs stage time and depends on camera repeatability. A missing clean plate multiplies rig-removal cost later. Passes are per-shot assets post depends on; a shot delivered without them is a post problem nobody can fix cheaply.
- **Takes.** Minutes to hours after the shot; longer with a camera move

#### Practical and replacement effects

Fire, water, smoke and magic are replacement-animated in resin, glass, gel, wax and cotton, or handed to CG. Aardman used hair gel, wax, Vaseline and cast resin in camera; Laika builds practical effects then augments digitally.

- **Who.** Effects Animator / SFX Fabricator
- **Decides.** Practical versus CG per effect; how many replacement pieces to sculpt; whether the effect shoots on the same pass as the puppet or as an element.
- **Engine.** An effect is either a sequence of physical replacements (sculpt count, cost, per-frame swap time) or a CG task (shots, render hours). Water and fire never behave to scale, so practical versions carry an inherent realism penalty.
- **Takes.** Weeks to sculpt a replacement set; per-frame swaps on stage

#### Crowd and background puppet strategy

Crowds cannot be hired, they must be manufactured. Isle of Dogs built 2,000 background figures; the alternatives are static dressed puppets, forced-perspective miniatures, cutouts, or CG duplicates of scanned hero puppets.

- **Who.** Animation Supervisor / VFX Supervisor
- **Decides.** Built and animated, built and static, forced perspective, or CG; how many bodies and how much variation; which shots simply avoid crowds.
- **Engine.** Crowd options differ by orders of magnitude (built-and-animated = animator-weeks each; CG = asset and render cost). Repeated identical puppets read as repetition above a density threshold. Scanning ties CG crowds to physical builds.
- **Takes.** Months of fabrication, or a CG asset build

#### Shot turnover and approval

The shot is seen at speed for the first time only when it is finished, days or weeks after it began. This is where a performance that felt right at one frame an hour turns out to be too slow, too small or off-model.

- **Who.** Director / Animation Supervisor
- **Decides.** Approve, approve with a comp fix, order a partial reshoot from a chosen frame, or scrap and relaunch the shot.
- **Engine.** Approval compares the delivered shot against the launch contract and the animatic timing. Rejection converts into new bid days plus a stage and puppet booking. The gap between shooting and seeing is the form's core risk mechanic.
- **Takes.** Minutes to review; days to weeks of consequence

#### Retake versus fix-in-comp

Almost any defect can be painted out for money: a bump, a visible rig, a face pop, a fingerprint, a boiling costume. The producer weighs comp hours against re-opening a stage that may already have been struck and rebuilt.

- **Who.** VFX Supervisor / Producer
- **Decides.** Fix, reshoot, or live with it; whose budget takes it; whether the set, the puppet copy and the animator still exist in that configuration.
- **Engine.** Each defect carries a comp cost in artist hours and a reshoot cost in stage-days plus set availability; if the set is struck the reshoot includes a rebuild. These decisions aggregate into the post department's real workload.
- **Takes.** Decided in minutes; the comp work runs weeks

#### Rig and face-seam removal

Every replacement-face frame carries a seam across the face and most frames contain a rig. A standing team paints them out. On Missing Link 446 of 1,486 shots were rig and seam cleanup only, with over 1,000 rigs removed.

- **Who.** VFX Paint Lead / Compositor
- **Decides.** Paint versus roto versus machine-learning-assisted removal; per-shot triage; how much of the frame to rebuild rather than patch.
- **Engine.** Cleanup scales with frames x seam length x rig count, discounted by clean-plate availability. This department exists only because of on-stage choices, so rig policy and face scheme directly set post headcount and post schedule.
- **Takes.** Hours to days per shot, across the whole picture

#### CG extension and digital effects integration

Sets are extended, skies replaced, crowds multiplied and effects layered onto photographed miniatures. Missing Link needed CG set extensions on 465 shots and CG animation on 325, and rendered for 112 million processor hours.

- **Who.** CG Supervisor
- **Decides.** What to build versus what to extend; where the practical set stops; how much CG the film's identity can absorb before audiences stop calling it stop motion.
