- **Engine.** Build-versus-extend is a per-set cost trade the player makes. CG assets carry render hours against a farm with finite throughput. A visible style break between photographed and rendered elements costs audience trust, not just money.
- **Takes.** Months, overlapping the shoot

#### Stage strike and turnaround

When the last shot on a set is finished, the set is struck and the stage rebuilt for the next one, or held standing in case of reshoots. Space, not money, is what caps how many units can run.

- **Who.** Stage Manager / Construction Coordinator
- **Decides.** Strike or hold; what is archived and what is skipped; which set moves in next and when that unit reopens.
- **Engine.** Strike frees a stage after a turnaround cost; holding blocks a stage but keeps reshoots cheap. Struck sets convert any later reshoot into a rebuild. Storage has finite capacity, so holding everything is not an option.
- **Takes.** Days per strike; weeks for the next build

#### Editorial assembly against the animatic

The cut already exists; finished shots replace animatic panels one at a time across 12-24 months, so the film turns real in patches and the edit is a burn-down chart rather than a process of discovery.

- **Who.** Editor
- **Decides.** Whether a delivered shot still works in context; trims that change a shot's length and therefore its cost; which unshot scenes to cut before the shop builds them.
- **Engine.** The cut is a timeline of slots, each holding animatic, in-progress or final. Trimming an unshot slot saves its whole cost; trimming a shot slot is pure waste. Percentage-final is the production's true progress metric.
- **Takes.** Continuous through production

#### Archive and asset disposition

Puppets, faces, sets and props are catalogued, conserved and either kept for exhibition and sequels or destroyed. Aardman keeps two full-time archivists; foam and clay assets decay whether they are kept or not.

- **Who.** Archivist / Asset Manager
- **Decides.** What to conserve, exhibit, reuse on the next film or scrap; conservation spend against storage cost and shelf life.
- **Engine.** Assets carry decay rates and storage costs. Archived puppets cut fabrication cost on a sequel and feed exhibition and publicity value. Destroyed assets make sequel continuity a full rebuild at current prices.
- **Takes.** Ongoing; concentrated in the months after wrap

---

## 4. Documentary (all modes: observational/verite, participatory, essay, archival/compilation, natural history and wildlife, true crime and investigative, biographical)

### The unit of work

THE UNIT IS NOT THE PAGE. There is no page.

PRODUCTION unit = the ACCESS-DAY, and the thing counted at the end of it is the BANKABLE SEQUENCE: a self-contained scene with a beginning, a turn and an end that could survive into a cut. A verite day yields 4-10 hours of rushes and, on a good day, one sequence. Many days yield none. An interview day yields 1-6 hours of record and, typically, 3-8 usable minutes. Wildlife counts BEHAVIOURS SECURED per expedition: a good field day is one behaviour, and a 30-day trip that returns two is a success.

THE RATIO is the documentary's analogue of stop-motion's seconds-per-day, and it is the headline number of the whole form. Verified examples: Wiseman's "Monrovia, Indiana" — 120 hours to 143 minutes, about 50:1; "At Berkeley" — 250 hours shot over 12 weeks, cut to 244 minutes over 14 months, about 60:1; "Hoop Dreams" — 250 hours over five years to 171 minutes, about 87:1. Longitudinal verite and blue-chip natural history run far above that; 300:1 and beyond is normal when camera traps and expedition rushes are counted. A doc player who shoots less is not being efficient, they are narrowing what the edit can find.

POST unit = the STRUCTURAL PASS. Not minutes cut, not shots — a complete re-ordering of the whole film, 3-10 days each, and a feature takes twenty to sixty of them. Secondary counters that a producer actually tracks weekly: minutes of fine cut (the Alliance of Documentary Editors' rule of thumb, cited in the IDA's 2025 budgeting guidance, is roughly one month of editing per 10 minutes of screen time — a 90-minute film is about nine months in the room); hours of rushes transcribed and indexed; seconds of archive cleared; percentage of the cut that is rights-clean; number of fact-check claims sourced; number of releases outstanding. A good day in the cutting room is one where the film gets shorter and clearer, or where a structure that does not work is proved not to work fast.

SCHEDULE SHAPE. There is no contiguous "shoot." Production is a string of blocks separated by months, interleaved with editing, and gated by money landing and by events in the world happening.

- News/current-affairs magazine segment: 3-10 days total.

- Broadcast strand half-hour (Panorama-style): 3-8 weeks from commission to transmission.

- Broadcast strand hour (Frontline/Dispatches/Storyville acquisition): 4-9 months.

- Independent feature doc: development and research 6-18 months; production spread across 1-4 years in blocks; edit 6-14 months; finishing 12 weeks for grade/sound/graphics plus 4 weeks of deliverables (the IDA's $1.3M sample schedule); festival run and sale 6-18 months after lock. Typical total 2-5 years; the field's own folk figure for an indie feature doc is 4-5 years.

- Wiseman model: 4-12 weeks shooting, then 9-16 months alone in the edit, roughly one film a year.

- True crime limited series (4-6 x 45-60'): 12-24 months, with legal vetting occupying the last 8-12 weeks.

- Archival-only feature: almost no production; 12-24 months of research, licensing and edit running as one process.

- Blue-chip natural history series: 3-4 years. "Blue Planet II" — over four years, 125 expeditions in 39 countries, more than 6,000 hours of underwater filming and 1,000 hours in submersibles, for seven 60-minute episodes.

The schedule is a CALENDAR OF OTHER PEOPLE'S EVENTS — a verdict date, an election, a migration, a surgery, a parole hearing, a field season — not a strip board. You cannot move any of them, and missing one deletes a scene from the film permanently.

### What is absent

- SCREENPLAY AND THE WRITER. There is no script to shoot. No drafts, no polish, no writer hired before production, no WGA-covered writing credit in the normal sense. The film is written in the edit, and the credit fight that follows (editors routinely function as co-writers) is a real industry sore point.
- SCRIPT BREAKDOWN. No 8ths of a page, no page count, no element tagging, no day-out-of-days, no strip board. Nothing to break down. The nearest object is an access calendar of events you do not control.
- STORYBOARDS, PREVIZ AND THE SHOT LIST DERIVED FROM SCRIPT. You cannot storyboard something that has not happened. Wildlife keeps a 'shot wish-list' and blue-chip keeps sequence treatments, but these are hopes, not plans.
- CASTING DEPARTMENT. No casting director, no auditions, no chemistry reads, no callbacks, no SAG-AFTRA principal contracts, no background casting. Contributors are found through research and pre-interview, and they are not performers and are usually not paid.
- REHEARSAL, TABLE READ, BLOCKING. Nothing is rehearsed. Blocking the camera to a rehearsed move is the specific thing verite is defined against.
- TAKES. There is no second take and no 'going again'. This is the single deepest structural difference: the coverage a fiction director builds across the day, a documentary director must get in one pass while it is happening.
- ART DEPARTMENT. No production designer, no art director, no set decoration, no construction, no props master, no set dressing, no picture cars. The world is already dressed. (Reenactment units in true crime are the sole exception and they are tiny.)
- COSTUME AND WARDROBE CONTINUITY. No costume designer, no fittings, no ageing/distressing, no continuity polaroids. People wear what they wear. What replaces it is a continuity problem you cannot fix: a subject cuts their hair between two filming blocks eight months apart and the two scenes can no longer be intercut.
- HAIR AND MAKEUP DEPARTMENT. At most a powder puff before a sit-down interview, and in journalism-mode work even that is contentious.
- STUNTS, ARMOURY AND PHYSICAL SFX. No stunt coordinator, no armourer, no squibs, no rigging for effect. Danger in documentary is real and is handled by risk assessment, security advisors and HEFAT-trained crew, not choreographed.
- THE AD DEPARTMENT. No 1st AD, no 2nd AD, no call sheets in the fiction sense, no dispatch, no unit move plan, no lunch at the sixth hour. A two-person verite crew has a plan and a phone.
- SCRIPT SUPERVISOR AND CONTINUITY. No lined script, no continuity notes, no eyeline/axis policing on the day. Continuity is discovered as a problem months later in the edit.
- LARGE ELECTRICAL AND GRIP CREWS. No gaffer, best boy, sparks, key grip, dolly grip, condor or lighting truck on a verite shoot. Available light, one small LED panel, and in interviews a two-head kit the DP rigs themselves.
- DAILIES SCREENINGS. No projected dailies, no lab report, no performance notes the next morning. Rushes are backed up in a hotel room and often unwatched for months.
- PERFORMANCE ADR AND LOOPING. Dialogue repair and noise reduction, yes. Re-performing a line, never — it would be a fabrication.
- RECRUITED TEST SCREENINGS WITH SCORE CARDS. Replaced by rough-cut screenings for six trusted peers, story labs, and contractual funder viewings.
- RESHOOTS AS PERFORMANCE FIXES. You cannot reshoot a real event. Going back out is a new negotiation with a person who has aged, moved on, or lawyered up.
- THE SINGLE GREENLIGHT. There is no moment of being greenlit. Financing accretes over years from grants, pre-sales, deferrals and the producer's own money, and production starts before it is complete.
- COMPLETION BONDS. Rare outside large commissioned series. Nobody will bond a film with no script and no end date.
- SECOND UNIT in the fiction sense, and VFX SUPERVISION as a department. Graphics and occasional animation exist, but there is no plate photography, no tracking markers, no VFX bid package.

### What is reordered

- WRITING COMES AFTER SHOOTING. In live action the script precedes everything; here the structure is discovered in the assembly, months or years after the material was shot. The paper edit is the first draft of the screenplay and it is written from transcripts of things people already said.
- NARRATION IS WRITTEN LAST OF ALL — after picture is effectively locked, to fill the specific comprehension gaps the cut leaves. In live action, dialogue is the first thing written.
- CASTING IS CONTINUOUS AND RUNS THROUGH PRODUCTION. New contributors are found in year two because the edit revealed the film needed them. In live action casting closes before the shoot.
- RESEARCH NEVER ENDS. It is a development stage in live action; here it runs from first idea to legal vetting, and the fact-check is the last research pass, performed on the finished film.
- RIGHTS AND CLEARANCES HAPPEN AT AND AFTER PICTURE LOCK, NOT IN PREP. Live action clears everything before the camera rolls. Documentary builds chain of title backwards: you cut the film, then find out what it costs and whether you can legally have it. Hi-res archive masters are only ordered after lock.
- LEGAL REVIEW READS THE CUT, NOT THE SCRIPT. Live action's legal clearance is a script pass in prep. Documentary vetting is a lawyer watching a 94-minute assembly with a timecoded transcript, and their notes force recuts weeks before delivery.
- THE BUDGET IS NEVER LOCKED. It is re-forecast at every funding tranche. Live action locks a budget at greenlight and manages variance against it; a doc budget is a moving finance plan for years.
- MUSIC SUPERVISION HAPPENS AGAINST A CUT THE FILM WAS BUILT ON WITH TEMP. Composers score a picture that is still changing under them, sometimes for six months.
- DISTRIBUTION IS PURSUED AFTER THE FILM EXISTS. Independent docs are finished on grants and premiered at a festival, then sold. The finance-then-make order of live action is inverted, which is why festival strategy is a production department, not a marketing one.
- POST IS NOT A PHASE AFTER PRODUCTION — THE TWO INTERLEAVE FOR YEARS. Editing starts while shooting continues, and the edit commissions further shooting. There is no wrap.
- THE 'PITCH' USES FINISHED FOOTAGE. A sizzle reel cut from real material you paid for yourself precedes financing; in live action the equivalent (a proof of concept) is optional and unusual.

### How the crew differs

- PRESENT HERE, ABSENT FROM LIVE ACTION — ARCHIVAL PRODUCER. Reports to the producer; researches, sources, budgets and clears all archival footage, stills, audio and ephemera; negotiates per-second rates; maintains the licence log; orders hi-res masters after picture lock. On archive-driven films they are engaged from development, not post. Their trade body, the Archival Producers Alliance, published the first documentary generative-AI guidelines in September 2024.
- ARCHIVE RESEARCHER / FOOTAGE RESEARCHER. Works under the archival producer on finding aids, institutional catalogues and orphan material.
- RIGHTS AND CLEARANCES COORDINATOR. Owns the clearance database: every clip, still, song, logo, artwork, document and face, with owner, scope, term, price and status. Live action clears in prep; this person clears backwards from a locked cut.
- FACT CHECKER. Independent of the edit. Produces the annotated transcript sourcing every claim in the finished film. Comes on board once there is a cut, not before. No live-action equivalent exists at all.
- CLEARANCE ATTORNEY / MEDIA LAWYER doing pre-broadcast vetting of picture, not script. Writes the fair use opinion letter the E&O insurer requires — a practice pioneered for documentary by Michael Donaldson after the 2005 Documentary Filmmakers' Statement of Best Practices in Fair Use.
- COMPLIANCE EDITOR (UK broadcast). Applies the broadcaster's editorial guidelines to the cut: fairness, privacy, right of reply, undue prominence, disclosure of staged or captive material.
- STORY CONSULTANT / DOCUMENTARY STORY PRODUCER. Brought into the edit to find a structure the people who shot it can no longer see.
- FIELD PRODUCER / SHOOTING PRODUCER / SELF-SHOOTING DIRECTOR (Assistant Producer in the UK). One person who researches, casts, directs, shoots and does sound. Has no fiction analogue.
- FIXER, LOCAL PRODUCER AND TRANSLATOR. Replaces the location manager, the transport captain and half the production office, and carries risk after the crew leaves.
- TRANSCRIBER / LOGGER. Converts hundreds of hours into the searchable index the edit runs on.
- CONTRIBUTOR WELFARE ADVISOR / DUTY-OF-CARE LEAD. A trauma-informed practitioner supporting participants through filming and release — a role the field formalised after the Documentary Accountability Working Group's 2022 framework 'From Reflection to Release'.
- VERIFICATION PRODUCER (OSINT / UGC). Establishes provenance of phone video, social media material and photographs, and screens for synthetic material.
- ACCESS PRODUCER. Full-time liaison to a police force, prison service, hospital trust, army unit or campaign, managing the relationship that the film depends on.
- IMPACT / OUTREACH PRODUCER. Designs and runs the campaign the film is made to serve, with NGO partners and screening tours.
- NARRATOR / VOICEOVER ARTIST, and the ESSAYIST-DIRECTOR who writes and speaks their own text.
- WILDLIFE SPECIALISTS: wildlife camera operator, camera trap technician, naturalist or behavioural consultant, scientific advisor, dive supervisor, licensed animal handler, expedition logistics manager, permit compliance officer.
- SECURITY ADVISOR AND HEFAT-TRAINED MEDIC on conflict, cartel or hostile-environment shoots.
- POST PRODUCER who is also the rights gatekeeper — no delivery without the clearance binder.
- ABSENT FROM HERE, CENTRAL TO LIVE ACTION — SCREENWRITER, and the whole development-to-draft apparatus.
- SCRIPT SUPERVISOR. No continuity notes, no lined script, no take numbers.
- CASTING DIRECTOR, casting associate, background casting, extras wrangler.
- 1st AD, 2nd AD, 2nd 2nd AD, set PAs running a call sheet. The AD department does not exist on a verite shoot.
- PRODUCTION DESIGNER, art director, set decorator, construction coordinator, props master, standby props, greensman, picture car coordinator.
- COSTUME DESIGNER, costume supervisor, standby costume, ager-dyer.
- KEY HAIR AND MAKEUP, prosthetics, SFX makeup.
- STUNT COORDINATOR, stunt performers, armourer, special effects supervisor, rigging crew.
- GAFFER, best boy electric, sparks, genny op, KEY GRIP, dolly grip, crane and remote head techs. A doc DP carries a two-head LED kit in a bag.
- LOCATION MANAGER and location scout, replaced by the fixer and the field producer.
- CRAFT SERVICE, catering truck, transport captain, unit drivers, honeywagons, base camp.
- VFX SUPERVISOR and plate photography; ADR MIXER for performance; COMPLETION BOND COMPANY REPRESENTATIVE; and the studio executive who visits the set.

### What goes wrong

- THE SUBJECT WITHDRAWS CONSENT after two years of filming. A revocable release, a broken relationship, or a family decision, and the film has no protagonist and no legal right to the material it is built from.
- THE STORY DOES NOT HAPPEN. The trial is postponed eleven months, the campaign concedes, the surgery is cancelled, the animal never returns, the person you followed does nothing at all for eighteen months. There is no writer who can make something happen.
- SOMEONE ELSE IS MAKING IT. Two crews find the same subject, exclusivity was never signed, and a rival premieres four months ahead of you at a bigger festival. Years of work become an also-ran.
- THE ENDING IS NOT THE ONE YOU SOLD. The pitch deck described a film that the world declined to produce. The commissioner's contract now describes something that does not exist, and the delivery gap is contractual, not artistic.
- ACCESS IS REVOKED MID-PRODUCTION. A new communications director reads the access agreement differently, a press cycle spooks the institution, and you are outside with half a film and a non-refundable travel spend.
- BACKGROUND MUSIC IN A SCENE YOU CANNOT CUT. A radio playing in the kitchen in your best sequence. Both master and publishing needed, found at delivery, and the scene has to be muted, replaced or lost.
- THE ARCHIVE QUOTE COMES BACK AT FOUR TIMES THE BUDGET LINE, or the rights holder refuses at any price, or an estate refuses because of what the film says about them.
- FESTIVAL-ONLY RIGHTS THEN A WORLDWIDE SALE. The film was cleared cheaply for festivals; it sells all-media worldwide and every archive and music licence must be renegotiated at commonly 3-10x, or replaced in a locked cut.
- THE FAIR USE CLAIM IS REJECTED by the E&O insurer or the distributor's lawyer, after the film has been cut around it. Either license late at a bad price or recut the argument.
- FACT-CHECK BREAKS THE SPINE. The most powerful line in the film is a contributor's sincere memory and is demonstrably false. It cannot stay as stated and the structure depends on it.
- LEGAL NOTES AFTER PICTURE LOCK force a recut of the third act, which means redoing the conform, the grade, the mix and the graphics, three weeks before delivery.
- A RIGHT-OF-REPLY LETTER PRODUCES A CREDIBLE REBUTTAL you cannot answer — or an injunction application filed days before transmission.
- A CONTRIBUTOR IS HARMED BY RELEASE: doxxed, fired, prosecuted, deported, ostracised, or worse. The production knew the risk, chose the scene, and owns the outcome.
- DEFAMATION OR FALSE LIGHT from the living named alongside the dead — true crime's standing hazard. Also misappropriation of likeness, and privacy claims from people who never signed anything.
- OUTTAKES ARE SUBPOENAED. Material you promised was private becomes discoverable, sources are exposed, and reporter's privilege may not protect a filmmaker in that jurisdiction.
- THE UNRELEASED FACE. Someone identifiable in the background of your best scene never signed, cannot be found, and objects. Blurring costs money and reads on screen as guilt.
- MEDIA LOSS. A card formatted before the second copy finished, a stolen bag in Nairobi, a drive that fails after three years in a cupboard, or footage seized at a border with sources on it.
- THE RATIO DEFEATS YOU. Four hundred hours, no logging discipline, and an editor who cannot find the shot she remembers. The film is in the rushes and unreachable.
- TEMP LOVE. The film was cut to a track that costs $60,000 to license and the commissioned score never replaces it convincingly. Everyone can hear what is missing.
- THE EDIT FINDS A FILM YOUR FUNDERS DID NOT FUND. A social-issue grant paid for what turned out to be a character study, and the grant terms describe something else.
- FUNDER EDITORIAL PRESSURE, OR THE APPEARANCE OF IT. A funder who is also a subject, an underwriter who saw a cut, a PBS editorial-control test failed — and the film becomes unbroadcastable on a technicality about independence rather than content.
- THE EXPEDITION RETURNS WITH NOTHING. Thirty field days, correct season, right hide, experienced operator, and the behaviour simply did not occur. There is no second take in nature and no reshoot window until next year.
- UNDISCLOSED CAPTIVE OR STAGED WILDLIFE FOOTAGE surfaces after broadcast and becomes the story, taking the broadcaster's credibility with it.
- SYNTHETIC MATERIAL ENTERS THE ARCHIVE CHAIN — an AI-generated 'period photograph' sourced in good faith from a stock library — and is identified after release.
- FOIA DENIED, OR ANSWERED IN FOURTEEN MONTHS, landing after picture lock with the document that would have changed the film.
- A TRANSLATION ERROR IN A SUBTITLED SEQUENCE, discovered by a native-speaking journalist the week after the premiere.
- THE FILM OUTLIVES ITS CONTEXT. It takes four years; the law changes, the war ends, the cause is won or lost, and what was urgent is now a period piece nobody commissioned.
- THE PROTAGONIST STARTS PERFORMING. They learn the camera, begin playing themselves, and the observational film quietly becomes a fiction that everyone can feel and nobody can name.
- DEFERRED-FEE BURNOUT. A crew and a director four years into an unpaid film with no end date, and the person who must finish it is the person least able to see it.
- THE PRODUCER'S OWN MONEY IS GONE. Cash-flowing a grant that arrived late, or a shoot block committed before a tranche cleared, and the film stops mid-edit for six months while the producer takes other work.

### The shape of the money

THE MONEY GOES INTO TIME, RIGHTS AND THE CUTTING ROOM — not onto the screen on a shoot day. Roughly the inverse of live action's shape.

A characteristic independent feature documentary (call it the IDA's $1.3M sample, which is a realistic professional-rate budget), proportionally:

- ABOVE THE LINE (director and producer fees): 15-25%. The IDA's own guidance puts director and producer fees at 5-10% of the budget EACH, structured as flat project fees rather than weekly rates, because the job runs for years and carries revenue participation.

- CONTRIBUTORS / CAST: effectively ZERO. This is the single largest structural difference in the whole budget. There is no cast cost, no SAG-AFTRA scale, no overtime, no per diems for talent, no star salary. Paying contributors is an editorial and ethical decision that most broadcast standards constrain or forbid; where fees appear they are life-rights options (biographical), consultancy fees (true crime, forensic experts), or reimbursed expenses. True crime and some biographical work are the exceptions, and paying is itself a credibility cost.

- PRODUCTION CREW AND SHOOT EXPENSES: 15-25%, spread across dozens of short blocks over years rather than one compressed period. Crew is 2-6 people, not 60-150. Equipment is owned or hired by the week, not a truck package.

- TRAVEL AND LOGISTICS: 5-15% on a domestic film; 25-40% on international, conflict or expedition work. For blue-chip natural history this is the dominant line — permits, charters, boats, ice camps, dive support, freight for specialist rigs.

- EDITORIAL: 20-30%, and usually the largest single below-the-line block. An editor for 30-50 weeks plus an assistant editor plus a cutting room. The Alliance of Documentary Editors' rule of roughly one month per 10 finished minutes means a 90-minute film carries about nine months of editor salary. Live action pays an editor for a fraction of that.

- ARCHIVE, STILLS, DOCUMENTS AND MUSIC LICENSING: 5-15% on a typical film; 25-40%+ on an archive-driven one, where it can exceed everything else combined. Anchors: $49-79 per second at Producers Library depending on theatrical/non-theatrical and regular/premium tier; $80-100 per second for TV news at a 30-second minimum; roughly $25 for a public-domain service copy up to about $200 per second for premium historical material. Indie song sync around $250-5,000; major-label catalogue commonly $20,000 and up. The IDA warns archival licensing is the most common source of post-production overrun.

- FINISHING (conform, grade, sound design, mix, graphics, DCP, captions, audio description, deliverables): 8-12%. The IDA sample allows 12 weeks for colour, sound and graphics plus 4 weeks for deliverables.

- LEGAL, E&O, FACT CHECK, CLEARANCE: 3-6% — a line live action barely has. E&O for an indie feature doc runs roughly $6,000-15,000 at $1M/$3M limits (ESTIMATE; higher with heavy fair use or true crime content). Fair use opinion letters, pre-broadcast vetting and fact-checking are each separate professional fees.

- TRANSCRIPTION: a real, doc-only line. At roughly $1-2 per recorded minute for human transcription (ESTIMATE), 300 hours of rushes is about $18,000-36,000 before anyone cuts anything.

- OFFICE, INSURANCE, ADMIN: 4-6%.

- ACCESSIBILITY: the IDA template reserves about 2% (about $20,000 on $1.3M) across crew sections for transcripts, captions, audio description, interpreters and an accessibility coordinator.

- FESTIVALS, PUBLICITY, IMPACT: 2-6%.

- CONTINGENCY: 6-10% per IDA guidance, and it is nearly always consumed by archive and legal, not by production.

HOW THE CASH BEHAVES, which matters more than the proportions. Live action closes financing, then spends. Documentary spends first (development and sizzle out of pocket), then raises in tranches across years from mismatched sources — grants with 3-9 month decision latency, territorial pre-sales, fiscal-sponsored donations at a 5-8% sponsor fee (estimate), deferrals and in-kind. The schedule is therefore gated by money landing, not by availability. A producer routinely carries months of payroll personally. Deferred fees are normal and frequently never recouped. Production services work is priced differently again: roughly a 10% production services fee where the producer takes no distribution revenue.

WHAT THIS MEANS AGAINST LIVE ACTION: a fiction feature spends its money in a 30-50 day block with 100 people on payroll and post as a tail. A documentary spends a small amount per shoot day across three years, then spends the bulk of the budget on one editor, one archival producer and a lawyer, in a room, arguing about a film that does not exist yet.

### Processes that exist only here

#### Subject and Story Sourcing

Finding a story that is already happening and will keep happening on camera: a court docket, a scientist's field season, a family, a trial date, a person about to do something irreversible.

- **Who.** Director / Development Producer
- **Decides.** Which subject; which mode (observational, participatory, essay, archival, wildlife, true crime, biographical); whether the story has a clock; whether to commit three years without knowing the ending.
- **Engine.** A world of latent stories, each with a hidden outcome curve, an access difficulty, an archive depth, a rival-production risk and a decay clock after which the story is over or someone else has it. The player sees only surface signals.
- **Takes.** 2 weeks to 2 years of unpaid looking

#### Desk Research and the Source File

Court records, FOIA and FOI requests, company filings, land registries, newspaper morgues, academic papers, prior coverage. Builds a chronology and a source file before a camera is switched on.

- **Who.** Researcher / Development Producer
- **Decides.** Which records to request and how early (a FOIA can take 6-18 months to land); how much to spend pre-financing; which claims are provable; what the film's factual spine will be.
- **Engine.** A queryable record world: documents with a release latency, a fee, a redaction probability and a hidden truth value. Requests filed now resolve months later. A chronology object that every later edit claim binds to.
- **Takes.** 4-20 weeks, then continuous

#### Pre-Interview and Contributor Casting

Off-camera conversations to find who can actually tell it. Testing recall, articulacy, willingness, legal exposure, and whether they fall apart when a lens is present.

- **Who.** Producer / Assistant Producer (UK)
- **Decides.** Who is a contributor and who stays a source; who appears named, anonymised, or not at all; what is asked on camera versus never; whether to spend the best material off-camera.
- **Engine.** Per-person articulacy, recall accuracy, stamina, volatility, legal exposure, and a freshness value that degrades each time they tell the story. Pre-interview buys knowledge and spends freshness.
- **Takes.** 1-3 hours each, dozens of people

#### Access Negotiation and the Access Agreement

The contract that gets you inside the hospital, prison, campaign or family. Covers exclusivity, duration, areas, who may be filmed, and whether anyone but you gets a say in the cut.

- **Who.** Executive Producer / Producer with Entertainment Lawyer
- **Decides.** What to concede — a sensitivity viewing, topic vetoes, name approval, exclusivity, a fee — against what must be kept: final cut and editorial independence.
- **Engine.** A negotiable contract with clause-level terms (exclusivity, cut approval, holdback, indemnity, termination) that become hard constraints on what the edit may contain and what legal will pass at delivery.
- **Takes.** 2 weeks to 18 months

#### Life Rights and Estate Option (biographical)

Optioning a living subject's or an estate's cooperation and their private material: letters, home movies, diaries, photographs, unreleased music, likeness for marketing.

- **Who.** Producer / Entertainment Lawyer
- **Decides.** Authorised (access, family archive, estate music, a softer film) or unauthorised (freedom, fair use, no private material). Option fee, term, renewal, and whether to sign a consultation clause.
- **Engine.** Two mutually exclusive production paths with different archive pools, legal risk and cost. Estate goodwill is a live variable that gates private material and can be withdrawn mid-edit over a single scene.
- **Takes.** 1-9 months

#### Archive Feasibility Audit

Before financing: does the material exist, who owns it, what condition is it in, and what does a minute of it cost. Some films are killed here and should be.

- **Who.** Archival Producer
- **Decides.** Whether the film is affordable at all; whether to design around an expensive rights holder; institutional sources at a service fee versus commercial libraries at a per-second rate; whether to budget fair use.
- **Engine.** A catalogue of holdings per subject: owner, format, condition, digitisation cost, per-second rate card, minimum duration, most-favoured-nation flags, orphan status. Queryable before the budget locks.
- **Takes.** 2-8 weeks

#### Test Shoot and Sizzle Reel

Three to eight minutes of real footage that proves access, proves the characters and proves the filmmaker can shoot. Funders buy the sizzle; almost nobody buys a treatment alone.

- **Who.** Director with Editor
- **Decides.** What to shoot on your own money; which scene leads; whether to show the subject's face; how much of the film's best material to spend on a trailer nobody may commission.
- **Engine.** Sizzle quality computed from actual captured assets — access proof, contributor charisma, image and sound quality, one scene containing a turn — and it is the largest single input to commissioner and grant response.
- **Takes.** 3-10 shoot days, 2-4 weeks cutting

#### Treatment, Deck and Comparables

Five to twenty pages: logline, mode, characters, access status, visual approach, intended structure, archive plan, team, comparables, audience, budget top sheet, finance plan.

- **Who.** Producer
- **Decides.** How to describe a structure that does not exist without promising one you cannot deliver; which comparable titles to name; whether to state the ending you hope for.
- **Engine.** The deck is a set of promises bound to later delivery. An over-promised structure, or an ending that does not arrive, opens a measurable delivery gap with the commissioner at the first contractual viewing.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks

#### Pitch Forum and Commissioner Meetings

Seven minutes at the IDFA Forum, Hot Docs Deal Maker, Sheffield MeetMarket, Sunny Side or Good Pitch, across a horseshoe table of commissioners from ARTE, ZDF, BBC, NHK, CBC, PBS strands and streamers.

- **Who.** Producer with Director
- **Decides.** Which market; how much to reveal; which territory to sell first; whether to take a small pre-sale that unlocks matching money or hold out for an all-rights streamer buy.
- **Engine.** Named funders with slate appetites, budget ceilings, territory and media requirements, editorial conditions and delivery specs. Every pre-sale locks rights away and narrows later distribution options.
- **Takes.** 7 minutes on stage, 6-18 months of follow-up

#### Grants and Fiscal Sponsorship

Applications to ITVS, Sundance Documentary Fund, Ford/JustFilms, Chicken and Egg, Perspective Fund and national bodies, plus a 501(c)(3) fiscal sponsor so individual donors can give tax-deductibly.

- **Who.** Producer / Grants Writer
- **Decides.** Which funders' values to write toward without distorting the film; what to claim about impact; whether to take money carrying an editorial condition or a firewall obligation.
- **Engine.** Funds with deadlines, 3-9 month decision latency, hit rates, size bands, restricted uses, matching rules, fiscal sponsor fee on grant income (roughly 5-8%, estimate), and reporting that consumes producer weeks.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks per application; 3-9 months to answer

#### Finance Plan and Rights Carve-Up

Assembling a budget from mismatched pieces — territorial pre-sales, grants, equity, deferrals, in-kind, producer cash — and mapping which rights each piece takes away.

- **Who.** Producer
- **Decides.** Which territories and media to sell early; what to hold back; how deep to defer crew fees; how much of your own money to risk; recoupment order and profit participation.
- **Engine.** A rights grid (territory x media x term) sellable in pieces, with holdbacks and conflict detection. A cash-flow timeline where grants arrive in tranches, so the shooting schedule is gated by money actually landing.
- **Takes.** 6-24 months, running under production

#### Access Calendar and Shooting Plan

Not a strip board. A calendar of things that will happen whether you are ready or not: a verdict, an election, a migration, a surgery, a release date, a funeral.

- **Who.** Producer / Production Manager
- **Decides.** Which events to cover and which to miss; how many crew days to hold in reserve for the unannounced call; whether to commit a shoot block before the money confirming it has arrived.
- **Engine.** World events with dates — some fixed, some probabilistic, some secret until 48 hours out — plus a reserve pool of crew days and travel lead times. Missing an event deletes that scene from the film forever.
- **Takes.** Re-planned weekly for the film's life

#### Kit Spec and Footprint

Choosing how visible the film is: one shooter with a small camera and radio mics, or a two-camera interview package with lights and a sound recordist. Codec, resolution, media volume, backup plan.

- **Who.** Director of Photography with Producer
- **Decides.** Footprint against image quality; whether to carry lights into a room where they would change behaviour; codec against years of storage cost; whether to shoot to a 4K/HDR delivery spec you may never need.
- **Engine.** Kit choices drive observed-behaviour fidelity (a smaller crew means less performance from subjects), image quality, sound intelligibility, data volume per hour, setup time, and which finishing options remain open.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks, revisited each block

#### Risk Assessment, Insurance and Security Protocol

Kit and liability cover, workers' comp, travel and medical, HEFAT-trained crew and a medic for hostile environments, and a security plan for the contributor as well as the crew.

- **Who.** Production Manager / Security Advisor
- **Decides.** Where the crew may not go; whether a subject's safety requires anonymisation or delayed release; whether to carry a tracker; what happens to the media if the crew is stopped at a border.
- **Engine.** Per-location risk of detention, seizure, injury and subject retaliation. Insurance with real exclusions. A seizure destroys any media not already copied off-site and can expose confidential sources.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks per territory

#### Verite Shooting

Being in the room while it happens, for hours, with no second take. Available light, radio mics on the principals, close in on wide-ish glass, staying on the person rather than cutting away.

- **Who.** Director / Self-Shooting Director with Sound Recordist
- **Decides.** When to roll and when to stop; where to stand; who to stay on when two things happen at once; when to lower the camera because a person needs you to; whether to intervene at all.
- **Engine.** Continuous scenes in which dramatic beats fire probabilistically while the camera is or is not rolling and pointed. Card, battery and sync limits. A subject-comfort value that changes what actually happens in the room.
- **Takes.** 4-10 hours of rushes per shoot day

#### Coverage Without a Script

Getting the cutaways, the hands, the door, the wide, the reverse, the exit — the connective material that lets an editor compress three hours into four minutes without a jump cut.

- **Who.** Director of Photography
- **Decides.** When to break from the action to grab the shot that will save the scene; how much to shoot of what is obviously not the story; whether to sacrifice a moment for the shot that makes the moment cuttable.
- **Engine.** A per-sequence cuttability score from shot-type coverage, axis, sound continuity and duration. Low cuttability means the editor cannot compress it, which silently costs screen time and clarity a year later.
- **Takes.** Continuous, inside every scene

#### Sit-Down Interview Setup

Room choice, a background that says something, eyeline just off lens or straight down it via Interrotron or EyeDirect, two cameras (wide and tight), lavalier plus boom, room tone, and a chair someone can sit in for four hours.

- **Who.** Director of Photography with Sound Recordist
- **Decides.** The subject's world or a black limbo; eyeline; whether the interviewer is heard or seen; one camera or two; how hard to light a face and what that lighting asserts about the person.
- **Engine.** Setup parameters that drive intelligibility, cuttability (a second camera makes invisible cuts possible), subject comfort and disclosure, and the visual grammar the rest of the film must then obey for consistency.
- **Takes.** 1.5-3 hours to rig, 1-6 hours to record

#### The Interview Itself

Hours of questions in an order designed to arrive somewhere. Asking for answers in full sentences because the question will be cut out. Waiting through silence. Coming back to the hard thing near the end.

- **Who.** Director
- **Decides.** Question order; when to push and when to leave silence; whether to tell a subject what another contributor said; whether to ask the question that ends the relationship and gets the film.
- **Engine.** Per-subject trust, fatigue, defensiveness and a disclosure threshold. Questions carry risk and reward; pushing early collapses trust; the same question at hour four returns a different answer. Output is tagged transcript.
- **Takes.** 2-6 hours per subject, often twice

#### Participatory and Presenter-Led Segments

The filmmaker inside the frame: the doorstep, the unannounced approach, the on-camera argument, the piece to camera that carries the argument between scenes.

- **Who.** Director-Presenter / Correspondent
- **Decides.** When to put yourself in the film; whether to ambush; whether to disclose that you are recording; whether the confrontation is a scene or a stunt that discredits the rest.
- **Engine.** Ambush yields high-value footage with a spike in privacy and recording-consent risk (one-party versus two-party consent modelled by jurisdiction) and permanent loss of that subject's cooperation and their circle's.
- **Takes.** Minutes, decided in seconds

#### Release Wrangling

Signed appearance releases, location releases, minor releases with a guardian signature, crowd-notice signage in public spaces, and a database of who signed what for which territories and media.

- **Who.** Production Coordinator / Assistant Producer
- **Decides.** When to ask — before the interview and lose spontaneity, or after and risk refusal; whether to accept a restricted or revocable release; whether to shoot at all without one.
- **Engine.** Every identifiable person in every clip carries a consent record with scope and territory. Unreleased faces block the clip at legal review; blurring costs money and reads on screen as an admission of something.
- **Takes.** Continuous; the file is never complete

#### Contributor Duty of Care

Ongoing informed consent rather than one signature, trauma-informed interviewing, independent support for vulnerable contributors, safety planning, and honesty about what the film will and will not do.

- **Who.** Producer / Contributor Welfare Advisor
- **Decides.** Whether to keep filming someone who is deteriorating; whether to pay for therapy; whether a scene that serves the film harms the person in it; what you promise about the cut and whether you can keep it.
- **Engine.** Contributors persist as entities with wellbeing, dependency on the production and post-release exposure. Harm is a tracked outcome feeding reputation, future access, funder standing, legal risk and festival press.
- **Takes.** The whole production and years afterwards

#### Data Wrangle, Backup and Field Logging

Two copies before any card is formatted, one off-site, checksummed. Logging shot notes, names, locations and consent while the day is fresh. Naming that still works across 300 hours and three years.

- **Who.** Data Wrangler / Assistant Producer
- **Decides.** Backup discipline against a 5am call; how much to log in the field versus pay for in post; proxy resolution; whether to carry unencrypted media through a border.
- **Engine.** Media as physical objects with loss and corruption probability. Unlogged rushes are cheaper now and vastly more expensive later — index coverage directly scales editorial search speed for the next three years.
- **Takes.** 1-2 hours every shoot day

#### Fixer, Translator and Local Production

The person who gets you into the country, the neighbourhood and the room, secures permits, tells you what not to film, and stays behind carrying the risk after you fly out.

- **Who.** Fixer / Local Producer / Translator
- **Decides.** Who to hire and what to pay; what to credit them as, or whether to credit them at all for their safety; what exposure they are left with once the film transmits.
- **Engine.** Fixers as characters with networks, reliability, price and their own exposure risk. Translation fidelity as a hidden variable that can silently corrupt a subtitled sequence and only surfaces at fact-check or after release.
- **Takes.** Continuous, per territory

#### Wildlife: Behavioural Recce and Field Planning

Working with field scientists and guides to pick a location, a season and one specific behaviour — a hunt, a hatch, a migration, a courtship — that can plausibly be filmed inside a fixed window.

- **Who.** Field Director / Naturalist Consultant
- **Decides.** Which behaviour to chase; which season; how many weeks in the field; whether to follow a habituated study population (reliable, less novel) or gamble on behaviour never filmed before.
- **Engine.** Behaviours with a per-field-day probability conditioned on weather, season, population density and prior disturbance. Scientific advisors raise that probability and simultaneously constrain approach distance.
- **Takes.** 6-18 months ahead of each expedition

#### Wildlife: Hides, Long Lens and Camera Traps

Weeks in a hide before dawn, 600-1200mm glass, remote cameras left for months, thermal for night work, and the professional tolerance for flying home with nothing.

- **Who.** Wildlife Camera Operator / Camera Trap Technician
- **Decides.** Hide siting; how long to habituate before rolling; how many traps and where; whether to pull an expedition that is not working or extend it and break the budget.
- **Engine.** An expedition resolves as a long draw: field-days consumed against behaviour probability, with a distribution in which many trips return nothing usable. Camera traps generate huge low-yield material that still must be reviewed.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks per expedition; 20-120 field days per sequence

#### Wildlife: Specialist Rigs

High-speed (Phantom), macro-probe, motion-control timelapse, gyro-stabilised aerials, rebreather diving, submersibles, low-light sensors, drones where permitted.

- **Who.** Specialist Camera Operator / Dive Supervisor / Aerial Operator
- **Decides.** Which rig for which behaviour; how much of the budget goes into one shot; whether the shot justifies getting a 200kg rig somewhere with no road and no power.
- **Engine.** Rigs with day rates, crew requirements, transport weight, failure rates and exclusive shot classes they alone unlock. A blue-chip sequence needs a mix; one rig failing removes a whole shot type from the cut.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks per deployment

#### Animal Welfare, Permits and Captive Disclosure

National park and protected-species permits, approach distances, no-baiting rules, licensed handlers for controlled sequences, and honest disclosure when something was filmed in captivity or on a set.

- **Who.** Production Manager / Licensed Handler / Compliance Officer
- **Decides.** Whether to use a captive animal or a controlled environment for a shot that cannot be got wild, and whether and how to disclose it — on screen, in the making-of, or not at all.
- **Engine.** Permit objects with conditions, inspection risk and revocation. Undisclosed captive footage carries a delayed scandal probability that fires after transmission and damages the broadcaster as well as the producer.
- **Takes.** 3-12 months for permits

#### Reenactment and Dramatic Recreation (true crime)

A miniature fiction shoot inside the documentary: hands, feet, a car at night, a shape behind a door. Actors, a location, wardrobe, occasionally a designer for a day.

- **Who.** Director with 1st AD and a small drama crew
- **Decides.** Whether to reconstruct at all; how literal; whether faces are shown; whether to depict a contested version of events; how the audience is told this is a reconstruction.
- **Engine.** A bolted-on live-action pipeline with cast, sets and shoot days. Reenactment style feeds a credibility variable, and depicting a contested fact as fact directly raises the defamation score at legal vetting.
- **Takes.** 1-6 shoot days per film or series

#### Provenance and Verification

Establishing that a photograph is what it claims, where a phone video came from and when, whether a home movie has copyrighted music playing in it, and whether a generated image has entered the file.

- **Who.** Researcher / Verification Producer
- **Decides.** What evidentiary standard the film applies; whether to use material of uncertain provenance; whether any synthetic or AI-generated element is used, and how it is disclosed to the audience.
- **Engine.** Every asset carries provenance state (verified, claimed, unknown, synthetic) plus a hidden truth. Publishing unverified false material triggers correction, funder withdrawal and legal exposure after release, not before.
- **Takes.** Hours to weeks per asset

#### Transcription and Logging

Every interview and most verite turned into timecoded searchable text and linked back to media, so a phrase search jumps to the frame (Avid ScriptSync/PhraseFind, or text-based editing).

- **Who.** Assistant Editor / Transcriber
- **Decides.** Human transcription or machine plus cleanup; which rushes to transcribe at all; the tagging scheme by character, theme and claim; how foreign-language material is translated and timed.
- **Engine.** The transcript-to-media index is the film's primary search surface and its coverage directly gates edit speed. Cost scales with hours: roughly $1-2 per recorded minute for human transcription (estimate).
- **Takes.** 1-4 weeks rolling; about $90 per hour of rushes (estimate)

#### Selects and String-Outs

Reducing 200-400 hours to watchable reels: a selects pass per character, per event, per theme. The first honest reading of what was actually got as opposed to what everyone remembers getting.

- **Who.** Editor
- **Decides.** What is a select and what is not; whether to organise by character, chronology or theme; how ruthless to be at a stage where nothing is yet load-bearing.
- **Engine.** Rushes as a tagged pool; selects as derived sequences carrying forward quality, cuttability, sync, consent and rights state. The pool's ratio sets how long this takes and bounds what the editor can ever find.
- **Takes.** 4-12 weeks

#### Paper Edit and Radio Cut

Structure built in text first — transcript paragraphs ordered on a page — then a sound-only assembly that proves the argument works before a single picture is chosen.

- **Who.** Editor with Director
- **Decides.** Whose voice carries the story; whether there is narration at all; the order of revelation; which of five possible films this is; what gets cut that everyone loves.
- **Engine.** A story graph of beats, each backed by specific clips and each asserting specific factual claims. Reordering beats recomputes comprehension, emotional shape and duration. This object is the script, and it stays mutable to the end.
- **Takes.** 2-8 weeks, redone many times

#### Assembly and the Search for Structure

The first cut of the whole thing, usually three to six hours. Then months of restructuring: acts found and abandoned, a minor character promoted to lead, chronology broken and rebuilt.

- **Who.** Editor
- **Decides.** Chapters, chronology or braided strands; where the film starts; whether to give the ending away early; how many characters the audience can hold; what the film is actually about.
- **Engine.** The edit is authorship: comprehension, pace, emotional curve and length are computed from the beat graph rather than authored by a script. The structural pass is the unit of work — days each, and each can ruin the film.
- **Takes.** 4-14 months (about one month per 10 finished minutes)

#### Rough Cut Screenings and Story Consultancy

Showing an unfinished film to six people who will say what is not working — at labs (Sundance Edit and Story Lab, Catapult, BAVC) and to broadcasters contractually entitled to a viewing.

- **Who.** Director and Producer with Story Consultant and funders
- **Decides.** Who sees it and when; which notes to take and which to refuse; whether a note reveals a missing scene that means going back out; whether to change editor.
- **Engine.** Feedback as noisy signal: notes correlate imperfectly with the real defect. Funder viewings are contractual gates with approval states. Changing editor costs weeks and destroys tacit knowledge of 300 hours of rushes.
- **Takes.** Every 6-10 weeks through the edit

#### Returns and Pickups

Going back out because the edit found a hole: a missing interview, a scene the film needs, a person who must now be asked about what another contributor said about them.

- **Who.** Director
- **Decides.** Whether the subject still cooperates months later; whether to restage something that once happened naturally; whether to ask the question the film has only now learned to ask.
- **Engine.** Subjects age, move, refuse, get sick, die, or hire lawyers while you cut. The world advances in real time. Some holes cannot be filled and must be solved structurally, with archive, or with narration.
- **Takes.** 1-10 days, several times over

#### Archive Research and Sourcing

Finding the actual frames: national archives, newsreel libraries, broadcaster libraries, regional collections, home movies, orphan reels in a garage, photographs, audio, documents and ephemera.

- **Who.** Archival Producer / Archive Researcher
- **Decides.** Which shot, from which of three owners at three prices; whether an institutional copy at a service fee beats a commercial copy at $80 a second; whether to pay to digitise an unscanned reel.
- **Engine.** A searchable archive world with holdings, formats, condition, owners, per-second rate cards and duplicate availability across sources. Finding the cheaper equivalent of a famous clip is a real skill with a real saving.
- **Takes.** 3-12 months, running through the edit

#### Archive Licensing and Rate Negotiation

Licences per clip priced per second by media, territory and term, with minimum durations (commonly 10-30 seconds), most-favoured-nation clauses, and extra fees if the film later goes theatrical or worldwide.

- **Who.** Archival Producer / Rights and Clearances Coordinator
- **Decides.** Festival-only rights now and re-licence later, or all-media worldwide in perpetuity up front; which clips to trim to the minimum billable duration; which to drop outright when the quote returns.
- **Engine.** Licences as objects with scope, term, price and a re-licence multiplier (commonly 3-10x to widen scope later). Real rate anchors: $49-79/sec at Producers Library, $80-100/sec for TV news at a 30-second minimum, up to ~$200/sec premium.
- **Takes.** 2-16 weeks per rights holder

#### Fair Use Determination and Opinion Letter

Identifying material used for critique, commentary or evidentiary purpose that need not be licensed, against the Documentary Filmmakers' Statement of Best Practices in Fair Use, then getting a lawyer's opinion letter an E&O insurer will accept.

- **Who.** Clearance Attorney with the Director
- **Decides.** What to claim as fair use and what to license; how long a clip can run and stay defensible; whether the film's commentary on the clip is genuine or bolted on to justify the use.
- **Engine.** Each unlicensed use scored on purpose, amount taken and whether the film actually comments on it. The opinion letter gates E&O cover; a weak claim is rejected by insurer or distributor, forcing a late licence or a recut.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks after picture lock

#### Incidental and Background Music Clearance

The radio in the kitchen, the song at the wedding, the ringtone, the television in the corner. Each needs both a master and a publishing licence, or the scene is muted, replaced or lost.

- **Who.** Music Supervisor / Rights and Clearances Coordinator
- **Decides.** Kill the scene, mute the music, replace it with a cleared track, or pay. Whether to ask the sound recordist to turn the radio off before rolling and lose the truth of the room.
- **Engine.** Every clip carries detected background music with owner, split and price. Unlicensed background is the classic late-stage bomb: found at delivery, it forces a recut of a scene the film's structure already rests on.
- **Takes.** Found late; 4-12 weeks to resolve

#### Score and Needle Drops

A score written against a cut that keeps moving under the composer, plus licensed songs. Temp music the film was built on has to be replaced, and the replacement is often worse.

- **Who.** Composer / Music Supervisor
- **Decides.** Score, source or silence; whether music is telling the audience how to feel about a real person; where to spot; how much of the budget one song is worth.
- **Engine.** Music as a per-scene emotional modifier with a cost and a licence scope. Temp-love as a real penalty on the replacement's reception. Indie sync roughly $250-5,000 a track; major-label catalogue commonly $20,000+ (estimates).
- **Takes.** 8-16 weeks

#### Narration: Writing and Record

Written last against a locked structure, carrying only what pictures and contributors cannot. Scratch guide by the director, then a session with the voice: presenter, actor, essayist or the filmmaker.

- **Who.** Writer-Director with Narrator / Voice Artist
- **Decides.** Whether the film has narration at all (Wiseman never, blue-chip always); first or third person; how much to explain; who speaks it and what that voice claims about authority and objectivity.
- **Engine.** Narration as a text layer over the beat graph, each line filling a specific comprehension gap at a cost in screen time and intimacy. Too much reads as television; too little loses the audience at a measurable rate.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks writing, 1-3 days to record

#### Graphics, Maps, Timelines and Animation

Lower thirds naming everyone consistently, maps, timelines, document treatments, redaction reveals, and animation standing in for events that were never filmed.

- **Who.** Motion Designer / Animation Director
- **Decides.** Naming conventions and whether title cards editorialise; whether animation substitutes for archive that does not exist; whether a document is shown, read aloud, or both.
- **Engine.** A graphics system bound to the contributor and chronology databases so a corrected name or date propagates everywhere. Animated substitution has a cost per finished second and a distinct credibility effect versus real footage.
- **Takes.** 6-14 weeks

#### Fact Check

An annotated transcript of the finished film: every spoken and written claim, narration line, caption, date and number sourced to a document or two independent sources, and the file preserved for legal and the insurer.

- **Who.** Fact Checker
- **Decides.** Which standard applies (broadcast journalism or authored film); what to do when a beloved line is unprovable; whether a contributor's sincere but wrong memory may stay in, and how it is framed.
- **Engine.** Every claim in the beat graph resolves against the research world with a truth value and a source count. Unsupported claims must be cut, qualified or re-sourced, and each fix costs edit days at the most expensive point.
- **Takes.** 4-10 weeks from the first fine cut

#### Legal Vetting and Pre-Broadcast Review

A lawyer watches the cut with the transcript and the fact-check file and flags defamation, privacy, contempt, recording consent, right of publicity, false light and contractual breach, line by line with timecodes.

- **Who.** Media Lawyer / Compliance Editor
- **Decides.** What to cut, what to qualify, what to defend; whether to name a person or obscure them; whether a juxtaposition implies a claim the film cannot prove.
- **Engine.** A timecoded note list with severity and a remedy cost. Jurisdiction matters: UK libel, US actual-malice for public figures, contempt while proceedings are live. Notes land after lock and force recuts, regrades and remixes.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks plus rounds

#### Right of Reply

Writing to everyone the film criticises, setting out the specific allegations with a deadline, then carrying their answer fairly — on screen, as a caption, or in narration.

- **Who.** Producer with Media Lawyer
- **Decides.** How specific to be, since specificity invites an injunction; the deadline; whether a late substantive response means recutting the film's conclusion days before delivery.
- **Engine.** Letters trigger timed responses: silence, denial, correction, legal threat, or an injunction attempt before transmission. A credible correction is a genuine editorial problem the cut must answer, not a box to tick.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks before lock

#### Picture Lock, Conform, Hi-Res Replacement and Grade

Replacing every proxy and watermarked archive screener with the licensed master, matching frame rates and aspect ratios across 16mm, 2-inch tape, VHS, phones and 8K scans, then making ten years of mismatched sources sit in one film.

- **Who.** Online Editor / Colourist / Post Supervisor
- **Decides.** Reframe or pillarbox archive; how far to upscale, stabilise and restore; whether archive keeps its era or is pulled toward the present; whether a subject's face stays consistent across five years of ageing.
- **Engine.** Each archive item has a screener and a master that can mismatch in frame rate, aspect, duration or audio. Mismatches cost conform days. Mixed-source films need far more shots individually touched than a single-camera drama.
- **Takes.** 2-5 weeks total

#### Sound Design, Dialogue Repair and Mix

Rescuing dialogue recorded in a moving car on one radio mic, building atmospheres for silent archive, and mixing narration, contributors, verite, atmos and music to a broadcast loudness spec.

- **Who.** Sound Designer / Dialogue Editor / Re-recording Mixer
- **Decides.** How far to repair before it stops sounding true; whether to add sound to silent archive and what that asserts; where music sits against a person crying; stems and M&E for foreign versions.
- **Engine.** Per-clip audio quality derived from the shoot (mic choice, environment, recordist skill) with a repair cost and a hard ceiling. Some field audio is unrecoverable, forcing the line to be cut or covered by narration.
- **Takes.** 3-6 weeks

#### Compliance, Deliverables and the E&O Binder

Music cue sheet, archive licence log matched to timecode, chain of title, release database, fair use log and opinion letter, E&O certificate, captions, audio description, subtitles, textless, M&E, stems, QC to the broadcaster's spec.

- **Who.** Post Producer / Post Supervisor
- **Decides.** Which distribution spec to build to; what to fix on a QC fail; whether to buy audio description and localisation now or wait until a sale demands it.
- **Engine.** A delivery checklist whose every item is generated from state built years earlier — one unsigned release or unlogged licence blocks delivery. Payment tranches release only on accepted delivery, so each gap is a cash-flow event.
- **Takes.** 3-6 weeks; roughly $50,000-150,000 for a feature (estimate)

#### Contributor Screening Before Release

Showing the finished film to the people in it before the world sees it, usually with no right of veto. Often the hardest afternoon of the entire production.

- **Who.** Director / Producer
- **Decides.** Whether to screen at all; who sees it and with how much notice; whether to change anything; what support to arrange for a person about to be watched by millions.
- **Engine.** Each contributor reacts according to their state and what the film does to them: endorsement, withdrawal, public repudiation, legal action, or harm. Outcomes drive festival press, funder standing and the producer's future access.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks before premiere

#### Festival Premiere, Sales and Impact Campaign

Premiere strategy (Sundance, Berlinale, IDFA, Hot Docs, True/False, CPH:DOX, Sheffield), a sales agent, an acquisition or a self-release, and an outreach campaign with partner organisations and a screening tour.

- **Who.** Producer with Sales Agent and Impact Producer
- **Decides.** Which festival to hold for and which to refuse; whether to take a streamer's all-rights offer over a theatrical run; whether the film has an impact goal and who pays for it.
- **Engine.** Festivals with submission deadlines, premiere-status rules that a prior screening destroys, programmer taste and slot scarcity. Buyer offers conditioned on reviews, awards, subject heat, and whether rights were pre-sold away.
- **Takes.** 6-18 months after lock

---

## 5. Unscripted and reality television

*competition, docusoap, dating, makeover, survival and talent formats. Format-led, cast-led production in which there is no script before the shoot and the script is written in the edit.*

### The unit of work

There are three units, and they are not pages.

FIELD: the contributor-day, and inside it the BEAT BANKED. Progress is "did we get the moment" — a confrontation, a decision, a confession, a reaction shot of the person who wasn't speaking. A story producer's day is scored against the story areas assigned that morning, not against minutes shot. Raw volume is the cost, not the progress: a docusoap crew banks roughly 4-8 hours of media per camera per day; a 90-camera fixed rig running 24 hours banks ~2,000 camera-hours a day. Shooting ratios (estimates from working practice): observational docusoap 30:1 to 100:1; travelling competition 100:1 to 300:1; continuous fixed-rig 1,000:1 and upward — a 24/7 rig with ~90 sources cutting three 44-minute episodes a week is arithmetically near 7,000:1. A good field day is three to five usable beats plus the OTF lines that make them cuttable; a bad one is fourteen hours of media containing nothing an editor can use.

POST: the CUT MINUTE and the CUT STAGE. An unscripted editor produces roughly 2-4 finished minutes a day (estimate), so a 44-minute episode is 3-6 weeks in a suite, and the schedule is literally a grid of editor-weeks. The tracked state per episode is its stage: stringouts built → paper cut → assembly (routinely 60-120 minutes long) → producer's cut → EP cut → broadcaster cut → notes rounds → lock → online/mix → delivery. A good post day is an act that plays without the story producer having to explain it.

DELIVERY: the LOCKED EPISODE against an airdate. Unscripted is almost always sold against a transmission date fixed before a frame is shot, and on strips the airdate is the master clock: Love Island-style formats shoot in the evening and transmit the following night, so the unit becomes the 24-hour cycle and the only question is "does it make air".

SCHEDULE SHAPE: typical primetime competition — 8-20 weeks development and bible, 6-16 weeks casting (overlapping), 6-12 weeks prep/rig build, 3-10 weeks continuous shoot (Survivor runs a 26-day game since season 41, down from 39), post beginning on shoot day one and running 8-20 weeks past wrap. Docusoap: 8-14 weeks shooting in bursts across a 6-9 month calendar, post trailing each block. Studio talent format: months of regional auditions, then a weekly live cycle of 5-6 days per show. Daily strip: one 24-hour pipeline repeated 40-60 times.

### What is absent

- The screenplay. There is no script, no drafts, no table read, no writers' room, and outside the narration writer usually no credited writer at all — story staff are production personnel, generally not covered by the WGA MBA.
- Script breakdown, scene numbering, the lined script, the stripboard and the one-line schedule. Nothing can be broken down because nothing is written; the schedule is built from contributor availability, rig days and daylight.
- Pages per day. There is no page count, so no day-out-of-days, no eighths, no 'we owe a page and a half'.
- The script supervisor. Continuity is not maintained on set at all — it is discovered in post, from timecode, wardrobe and daylight, and is a constraint on what the edit may assemble.
- Storyboards, previs, shot lists and blocking. Coverage is opportunistic; a camera plan is a set of standing assignments, not a list of shots.
- Rehearsal (except live-gallery camera rehearsal and talent-format performance runs). Nobody rehearses a scene, because performing it twice destroys the thing being filmed.
- Actor casting: no auditions for roles, no chemistry reads with agents attached, no series-regular deals, no SAG-AFTRA performer contracts for contributors — participants sign appearance and contributor agreements, not performer contracts.
- Costume design, fittings and a costume department. Contributors wear their own clothes (wardrobe notes exist only for continuity); wardrobe/stylist and make-up exist for the host and judges.
- The 1st AD and the AD hierarchy. Their functions split between the production manager, floor manager, unit manager and the story producer, who is the person actually 'running the day'.
- The director as authorial head. On most formats the director is a gallery or field craft role; on many docusoaps there is no director at all — a self-shooting PD or a field producer covers it, and there is no DGA director's cut.
- Dailies screenings. Nobody watches dailies; loggers and the story team read logs and transcripts, and the first thing anyone 'screens' is a stringout.
- Reshoots of anything that happened. You cannot reshoot an eviction, an argument or a proposal. The only reshoot that exists is a pickup interview.
- ADR. Contributors are not looped; a line that cannot be heard is replaced with a different line, an OTF, or narration.
- Composed score to picture on most shows, and with it the composer, the spotting session and the scoring stage. Music is library cues chosen by a dubbing editor or music supervisor.
- Second unit in the scripted sense, the stunt department on most formats (challenge/safety teams replace it), dialect and acting coaches, and the intimacy coordinator (dating formats use welfare and safeguarding staff instead).
- A greenlight based on a script. The asset that is bought, financed and licensed is the format and the bible; on many shows the broadcaster commissions from a taster tape and a document and never reads anything resembling a script.
- Post-production as a distinct phase that begins at wrap. It begins on shoot day one and never has the pipeline to itself.
- On live transmission shows — evictions, results shows, finals — post does not exist at all. Picture lock is transmission.

### What is reordered

- Casting happens where writing would. The cast board is the only creative decision made before the shoot and it is irreversible: choosing twelve people is choosing every story the season can contain.
- The script is written AFTER photography. The paper cut — beats, timecodes, quoted lines, act breaks — is produced by a story producer from footage that already exists, and is the document the editor works from.
- Budget, episode count, running time and airdate are locked before anyone knows what the content is. The tariff is agreed against a format document, not against a shooting script.
- Graphics and music are designed in prep as a fixed, reusable package and then populated per episode from data, instead of being made to a finished picture at the end.
- Post runs parallel to the shoot from day one, and the feedback runs backwards: story notes from the edit become field assignments the next morning ('we have no one saying why she left — get it tonight').
- The pilot moves earlier and shrinks: a 3-8 minute taster tape shot on development money before the commission, or no pilot at all, with episode one of the run standing in.
- Clearance is split to both ends: releases, consents, psych and medical sign-off before anyone is filmed; defamation, fairness and compliance viewing after the cut exists.
- Reshoots move into post and change in kind — pickup interviews only, shot weeks later to match, to patch a hole the edit found.
- The cut approval chain replaces the director's cut: story producer's cut → EP/showrunner cut → broadcaster or streamer cut → notes rounds → lock.
- Publicity starts before the show is finished. Cast are announced, press-trained and photographed while the season is still shooting or cutting; on strips the audience is watching episode 4 while episode 30 is being shot.
- Casting for the next series begins while the current one is transmitting — transmission is itself the top of the casting funnel.
- Set build replaces location scouting in sequence terms: one long rig build and a single strike bracket the whole shoot instead of a rolling scout-and-move.
- On fast-turnaround strips the entire order collapses into a 24-hour loop: shoot, log, stringout, cut, compliance, deliver, transmit, repeat — with no phase boundaries at all.

### How the crew differs

- PRESENT AND FOUND NOWHERE IN SCRIPTED: the story department. Supervising Story Producer, Story Producer, Field Story Producer, Story Editor, Story Assistant. This is the writing staff under a production job title, and on most shows it is deliberately not a WGA-covered writing credit.
- A standing casting department that runs for the whole life of the show rather than a pre-production casting director: Casting Director (Unscripted), Casting Producer, Casting Associate, Casting Coordinator, street casters, and a next-series funnel that never closes.
- Welfare and duty-of-care staff: Welfare Producer or Duty of Care Lead, an independent clinical psychologist available on site, safeguarding leads, a designated person empowered to stop filming, family liaison, and an aftercare provider retained past transmission.
- The challenge department: Challenge Producer, challenge designers, riggers and safety officers, and the crew stand-in team (on Survivor, the Dream Team) who physically play every game before the contestants do.
- Gallery and outside-broadcast roles on formats that have no scripted equivalent: Technical Director (US) / Vision Mixer (UK), Vision Engineer on shading and racks, EVS operator, robotic camera operators, Gallery or Log Producer, and on rig shows a Live Story Producer calling cameras onto emerging stories.
- Loggers. Full-time, shift-working staff whose only job is timecoded description of what happened, with no scripted counterpart at all.
- Contributor handlers, cast wranglers and a Sequester or Integrity Producer who controls the information environment around the cast.
- Media management at a scale scripted never sees: DITs, Data Wranglers, Media Managers and a Post Coordinator whose day is measured in terabytes verified.
- Field hybrids: the self-shooting Producer-Director (UK) or Shooter-Producer (US) who directs, operates, produces the interview and manages the contributor simultaneously — one person doing what four do on a scripted set.
- Compliance Editor, and in the US a broadcaster Standards & Practices executive with authority over game rules and results.
- Interactive/Vote Producer and an independent vote auditor; Digital and Social Producers running live feeds and cast accounts during transmission.
- The Narration Writer, often the only person on the production with a writing credit.
- ABSENT: screenwriters and the writers' room; the script supervisor; the 1st and 2nd AD hierarchy (replaced by Production Manager, Unit Manager and Floor Manager); costume designer and fitting rooms; storyboard artist and previs; composer, orchestrator and scoring crew; ADR mixer; second unit director; dialect and acting coaches; and on most formats the stunt coordinator, whose safety function is absorbed by the challenge and safety teams.
- ABSENT in authority terms: the director as author. Many unscripted shows have no director credit at all. Creative authority sits with the Showrunner/Executive Producer (US) or Series Producer and Series Editor (UK), and the person actually running the shooting day is the Story Producer, not an AD.
- DIFFERENT LADDERS: UK unscripted runs Runner → Researcher → Assistant Producer → Producer-Director → Series Producer → Series Editor → Executive Producer. US unscripted runs PA → AP → Segment/Story Producer → Supervising Producer → Co-EP → Showrunner. Neither maps onto scripted departments.
- DIFFERENT LABOUR STATUS: US unscripted is substantially non-union, which is a large part of why it is cheap; IATSE has been organising unscripted crews since the early 2020s with contracts on some shows, hosts are typically engaged under SAG-AFTRA's network television agreements, and story staff are generally outside the WGA MBA. UK crews are freelance under PACT/BECTU norms. Contributors are not performers in any union sense; they sign contributor agreements, and organising efforts among cast are recent and thin. (Broad-strokes summary; verify specifics per show.)

### What goes wrong

- The story does not happen. You shoot for six weeks and the cast is polite to each other. There is no script to fall back on, no scene to reshoot, and the only remedies are casting a disruptor in, engineering a situation (which risks reading as fake), or delivering a series nobody watches.
- A contributor walks. On a format built for sixteen players, one quit breaks the elimination math for every remaining week; you cannot recast, and the format's internal arithmetic — pairs, teams, tribe swaps, vote counts — has to be rewritten on the fly with the licensor and compliance watching.
- A contributor is harmed. Self-harm, breakdown or death during or after transmission is the failure mode unique to this kind. In the UK it triggers Ofcom investigation under the participant-welfare rules added to the Broadcasting Code in 2021 (due care over the welfare of contributors who might be at risk of significant harm), potential parliamentary scrutiny, and it has ended shows outright — The Jeremy Kyle Show was cancelled in 2019 after a participant's death.
- The result is influenced. Producer intervention in a contest outcome is not a creative misjudgement but, in the US, potentially a federal offence under 47 U.S.C. 509, and it destroys the format's licensability worldwide. Even the allegation, once a contestant says it on a podcast, is unrefutable without the rushes.
- A frankenbite is exposed. A line built from two different sentences, a reaction lifted from another day, a conversation assembled from two cameras and one OTF — defensible until a contributor, a live-feed watcher or a leaked rush proves the construction. Defamation claims, regulator findings on unfair treatment, and a permanent credibility cost.
- Casting vetting failed. A cast member's criminal history, abuse allegation or decade-old posts surface mid-transmission. The episodes are already cut; you are choosing between editing a person out of a season built around them and defending them nightly.
- Sequestration is breached. A contestant learns something from outside — a result, a family event, a strategy posted online — and the game is contaminated. Depending on when it is discovered, the remedy ranges from an on-camera adjudication to voiding a round already transmitted.
- Live feeds spoil your own show. On 24/7 formats the audience knows the outcome before the edit reveals it, boot lists circulate for weeks ahead, and the edited show's structure has to be rebuilt around an audience that is already ahead of it.
- A single moment is lost forever. No camera was pointed at it, the radio mic was flat, the card was not verified before the wipe, or timecode drifted and the multicam group will not build. Unlike scripted, it cannot be shot again — the proposal, the fight, the collapse happened once.
- Weather, illness or disaster on a survival or travelling format, with a cast who are contractually in your care in a remote place. Medevac times, insurance, force majeure, and a game designed for an environment that has stopped cooperating.
- The licensor's format police fail you. You changed something the bible declares non-negotiable, the flying producer reports it, and approval for transmission or for the next season is withheld — a failure mode that only exists when you are making someone else's format.
