- A rival launches a near-copy. Formats are weakly protected as ideas, so the defence is registration, bible documentation and passing-off, and the fight is commercial and slow while the copy takes your territory.
- A public vote fails. Technical failure mid-window, fraud, block voting, a mis-stated price point or a result announced before the audit completes. In the UK this is a serious licence matter; the premium-rate phone-in scandals of 2007 produced multi-million-pound fines and ended careers.
- Live transmission failure with real people: a source lost, a contributor swearing before the watershed, a result mis-read on air, someone refusing to leave the house, a fight in front of a studio audience. There is no take two and no compliance pass.
- Continuity discovered too late. The episode is built from three days, and the shirt changes mid-conversation, the daylight jumps, or a character references something the audience has not been shown yet. Found in the edit, fixable only by losing the best material.
- Coverage debt comes due. The edit needs someone to explain why they voted that way and nobody ever asked. Pickup interviews weeks later are contaminated by the contributor now knowing how the season ended, and it shows.
- Access is withdrawn. An institution, family or business pulls consent mid-run — after an incident, a bad episode, or a change of leadership — stranding a half-shot series with a subject that cannot be replaced.
- Logging and transcription were skimped. Thousands of hours exist and are effectively unsearchable, so the story is limited to what someone happened to remember, and the post schedule inflates invisibly until it misses delivery.
- A cast member's real life intervenes: arrest, bereavement, divorce, bankruptcy or a viral scandal, occurring after the season is shot and before it airs. You are transmitting a version of a person that reality has overtaken, and the compliance and welfare questions are live every week.
- Minors and vulnerable adults: child performance licensing, chaperones and tutoring on family formats, capacity to consent on makeover and medical formats, and the certainty that an informed-consent failure will be judged retrospectively and in public.
- Fatigue on a strip. A 24-hour pipeline run for eight weeks degrades every stage at once — logging accuracy, compliance attention, welfare vigilance, gallery reflexes — and the errors that result cluster exactly where they are most expensive.
- Contributor litigation and public dispute. Unlike actors, contributors never agreed to a character; when they see the edit, the depiction clause is tested in court and in the press, and the discovery process opens the rushes, the logs and the story team's own notes.

### The shape of the money

Above-the-line barely exists. There is no script cost, no writers' fees, no residuals-bearing cast. On a scripted drama, script plus principal cast can be 25-40% of the budget; on unscripted the whole ATL block is commonly 5-12% (estimate) and on an observational series it can be almost nothing. The single largest ATL line, where it exists, is the host or judging panel on a big talent or competition format — a marquee panel can cost more than the entire below-the-line of a docusoap, which is exactly why cheap formats avoid presenters.

Contributors are not a salary line. They are stipends (widely reported figures: Big Brother US houseguests around $1,000-1,250 per week; Survivor players a per-week stipend scaling with how long they last; Love Island UK around £250 per week — all reported, not audited), plus travel, per diems, sequester hotels, medicals, psych screening, wardrobe allowances, welfare and aftercare. Prize money is a separate, usually bonded or escrowed line, and on most formats it is under 5% of the budget — the prize is the format's engine, not its cost.

The money is in below-the-line labour weeks, technology and post. Three blocks dominate:

1. CREW WEEKS. Unscripted crews are small per unit but run for a very long time and there are many units. Story and casting departments alone can be 10-20% of the budget (estimate) — that is the writing budget, spent on production job titles.

2. TECHNICAL PLANT. On fixed-rig and studio formats the rig, gallery, OB facility, fibre, lighting for 24-hour shooting, radio mic inventory and the shared media store can be the largest single block. On travelling formats this becomes location, logistics, medevac cover and challenge construction instead.

3. POST AND MEDIA. Post plus story is routinely 30-45% of an unscripted budget (estimate) versus perhaps 15-25% on drama, because the storytelling happens there. Storage is a real, sizeable line — hundreds of terabytes to petabytes per season, plus LTO archive and multi-year retention. Logging, transcription and assistant-editor hours are budgeted as production, not as overhead, because they gate everything downstream.

Music inverts: library and blanket licences instead of a composer and a scoring stage, so music is a small line except on talent formats where performance clearance becomes significant. Graphics invert too: a big one-off package design in prep, then near-zero marginal cost per episode.

Two lines have no scripted analogue: the format licence fee where you are producing someone else's format (a single-digit percentage of the local production budget per episode is typical, plus paid consultancy weeks for the flying producer — estimate), and welfare/duty of care, which since the late-2010s UK scandals has become a real six-figure annual cost on large formats (estimate).

Revenue shape differs as much as cost shape. A drama recoups on licence and library sales of the finished tape; an unscripted hit recoups on the FORMAT — licence fees, consultancy, distributor commission of roughly 25-35% (estimate), and territory after territory paying to make their own version. The global format trade is estimated in the low billions of euros annually. That is why the bible, not the episode, is the asset, and why indies fight to retain format rights even at the cost of deficit-financing the first series.

Headline ranges, all estimates: UK per-hour tariffs run from roughly £50-150k/hr for daytime factual to £200k-1m+/hr for a primetime peak format; US cable docusoap roughly $150-450k per episode; US network primetime competition roughly $1-3m per episode; streamer unscripted roughly $500k-1.5m per episode. Producer fee is typically around 10% of budget, and contingency is thin to nonexistent because the tariff is fixed and the overage is yours.

### Processes that exist only here

#### Paper Format Origination

Turning a premise into a repeatable engine: who competes or is observed, the recurring weekly mechanic, how someone leaves each episode, what the end state is. One page, before anything else exists.

- **Who.** Development Producer / Creative Director, Development
- **Decides.** The mechanic, cast size, elimination rule, episode count, returnability, and the single line that sells it. Whether this is an event series or a strippable machine.
- **Engine.** A typed format object — cast N, round structure, elimination function, prize, episode grid, jeopardy points — that the simulation can actually run to produce outcomes, plus a similarity score against a database of existing formats.
- **Takes.** 1-10 days

#### Format Engine Stress-Test

Running the game on paper before it costs anything. Does it produce a different winner each time? Does it stall in week four? Does one contestant become unbeatable? Does a round fill 44 minutes or 9?

- **Who.** Development Executive / Format Consultant
- **Decides.** Twists, immunity, where jeopardy sits in the hour, public vote versus contestant vote, act-break placement, and what the safety valve is if the game resolves early.
- **Engine.** Monte-Carlo the rules against synthetic cast profiles to output arc variety, winner predictability, dead-air risk and jeopardy-per-episode; surface degenerate strategies the audience will find before you do.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days, rerun after every rule change

#### Taster Tape / Sizzle

Three to eight minutes of really-shot material that proves tone and cast rather than story. This is what a commissioner buys from; it replaces the scripted pilot and is paid for out of the indie's own development money.

- **Who.** Development Producer + Development Editor
- **Decides.** Which real people to shoot on spec, how much of your own cash to risk, tone, title, and whether to shoot or cut it from scavenged and archive material.
- **Engine.** Development spend as at-risk capital; a commissioner model scoring tape on tone, cast magnetism and mechanic legibility with channel-fit weighting; a conversion probability from tape to greenlight.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks; low five figures is typical (estimate)

#### Format Bible Authoring

Writing the tradeable asset. Episode-by-episode structure with timings, rules and every edge case, casting archetypes, set plans, graphics and music spec, schedule and budget templates, crew structure, and the 'why it works' analysis.

- **Who.** Format Producer / bible writer, with Distribution
- **Decides.** What is licensable versus what stays yours; how prescriptive to be; which elements are declared non-negotiable format points a licensee may not change.
- **Engine.** The bible as a structured document with per-section completeness scores; each section raises a local adaptation's success probability and the licence fee a distributor can command. An incomplete bible is a cheaper, riskier asset.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks, updated continuously through series one

#### Format Registration & Rights Chain

Establishing evidence of authorship and the chain from deviser to producer to distributor. Formats are not protected as ideas — protection comes from documented expression, contracts, trade marks and passing-off.

- **Who.** Head of Business Affairs; distributor; FRAPA registration
- **Decides.** Registration and trade-mark strategy, deviser royalty, distributor commission and term, territory holdbacks, and whether to sue when a rival launches a near-copy.
- **Engine.** A rights ledger — deviser share, producer share, distributor commission (25-35% is typical, estimate), holdbacks — paying out on every downstream sale, and litigable with an outcome distribution rather than a binary.
- **Takes.** Days to establish; runs for the life of the format

#### Commission & Tariff Negotiation

Unscripted is bought at a tariff — a price per hour for a slot — not a bespoke negotiated budget. You agree volume, slot, delivery dates, and which rights the broadcaster takes.

- **Who.** Managing Director / Head of Unscripted; broadcaster Commissioning Editor
- **Decides.** Volume versus margin; whether to deficit-finance in exchange for keeping format rights; stripped daily versus weekly; slot, watershed and airdate.
- **Engine.** A tariff table by channel, slot and genre in currency-per-hour; rights splits for commissioned versus licensed versus indie-retained; a margin model where the production fee is typically around 10% of budget (estimate).
- **Takes.** 2 weeks to 18 months from pitch to greenlight

#### Tariff Budget & Shoot-Pattern Design

Budgeting backwards from a fixed price per hour, with no cast salaries and no writers to pay. The money is crew weeks, rig, media and edit suites. Then choosing the shoot pattern the budget can actually sustain.

- **Who.** Line Producer / Production Executive
- **Decides.** The three-way trade between crew size, shoot days and post weeks; number of edit suites and story producers; prize money and stipends; contingency in a business with almost none.
- **Engine.** A budget model with unscripted-only lines — story department, logging, storage per TB, welfare and aftercare, format fee, bonded prize — plus a hard constraint that below-the-line dominates and post weeks are the binding resource.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks, revised at every greenlight stage

#### Access Agreement

On observational and institutional formats the first asset is access: a written deal with a hospital, airport, hotel, police force, school or family granting the right to film, on agreed terms, for an agreed period.

- **Who.** Series Producer / Executive Producer with Production Counsel
- **Decides.** What the institution gets in return (usually nothing but a viewing), veto scope, whether they see cuts, safeguarding and confidentiality rules, and where cameras may not go.
- **Engine.** Access as a revocable permission with a trust level that moves with every incident and every transmitted episode; losing access mid-run strands a half-shot series with no replacement subject.
- **Takes.** 3-12 months to negotiate; can collapse in a day

#### Casting Funnel Build

Industrial-scale sourcing: open calls, casting portals, social advertising, agents-for-real-people, and street casters approaching strangers in gyms, bars and salons. Big formats take tens of thousands of applications.

- **Who.** Casting Director (Unscripted) / Casting Executive
- **Decides.** Channels and spend per channel, geography, incentives, how wide to open the net, and whether to headhunt known personalities rather than find unknowns.
- **Engine.** A funnel with per-channel conversion (applications → tapes → interviews → finals), cost per applicant, and an archetype-coverage tracker against the bible's casting grid so gaps are visible before it is too late.
- **Takes.** 6-16 weeks, frequently begun before the commission is signed

#### Casting Tape & First-Round Interview

Self-tape, then a recorded interview designed to find whether a real person performs: can they tell a story, do they react, will they state an opinion on camera, do they collapse or expand under a hostile question.

- **Who.** Casting Producer / Casting Associate
- **Decides.** Who advances, which questions provoke, and the difference between someone interesting in a room and someone castable on screen.
- **Engine.** Hidden per-person attributes — camera confidence, volatility, articulacy, likeability, conflict appetite, stamina, fragility — revealed only partially and noisily by each screening stage.
- **Takes.** 10-25 minutes per tape; weeks in aggregate

#### Mass Audition Rounds

Talent formats run a pyramid: arena or regional open auditions in front of producers, then producers' picks, then the filmed judges' audition, then boot camp or callbacks, then the live shows. Tens of thousands seen, dozens filmed.

- **Who.** Talent Executive Producer / Audition Producer
- **Decides.** City list and venue days, how many pre-screens sit between the queue and the judges, who is filmed as a story rather than as an act, which sob-story threads are followed.
- **Engine.** A pipeline with per-round pass rates, per-city yield, and act-quality versus story-quality as separate scores — the format needs both, and picking only one produces an unwatchable live series.
- **Takes.** 3-6 months of auditions before the first filmed round

#### Background Vetting & Social Scrub

Criminal record, civil litigation, credit, employment verification, prior media appearances, and a full scrape of every social account back to the beginning. This is where you find the thing that would end the show in week three.

- **Who.** Casting Coordinator with a third-party investigator
- **Decides.** Depth of check, red-line offences, what is disclosed to the broadcaster, and whether to cast someone with history and manage it rather than lose them.
- **Engine.** A hidden history field per contributor with a discovery probability that scales with check depth and, after transmission, with press interest — undiscovered items detonate at the worst available moment.
- **Takes.** 3-10 days per finalist

#### Psychological Screening

Standardised testing plus clinical interview to assess fitness to take part and risk of harm: an instrument such as the MMPI-2/2-RF or PAI, plus history of self-harm, substance use, current treatment and support network.

- **Who.** Licensed clinical psychologist, independent of production
- **Decides.** Pass, fail or conditional; who needs ongoing support during the shoot; what the clinician may tell production and what stays confidential; whether to overrule a clinical concern.
- **Engine.** A resilience and risk score the player never sees in full, feeding a harm model during the shoot and for months after transmission. Overruling a clinician is available, cheap today and expensive later.
- **Takes.** 2-4 hours per finalist plus a written report

#### Medical & Fitness to Participate

Physical examination, bloods, ECG on physical formats, STI panel on dating formats, medication review, allergies and diet, plus fitness clearance for remote or extreme locations.

- **Who.** Production medic / occupational health physician
- **Decides.** Medical exclusions, how prescribed medication is managed in isolation, the medevac plan, and on-site medical staffing levels.
- **Engine.** Illness and injury probabilities by format type and environment, time-to-definitive-care by location, and the format-math consequence when a player is pulled from a game built for an exact number.
- **Takes.** Half a day per finalist

#### Final Cast Board & Chemistry Mix

The only writing done before the shoot. Twelve to twenty-four people chosen as a system — who will clash, who will pair, who narrates, who is the audience's way in, who the villain is before they know it.

- **Who.** Executive Producer with the broadcaster's Casting Executive
- **Decides.** The line-up and the reserves, archetype balance, who enters late as a disruption, and on dating formats the opening pairing matrix.
- **Engine.** A relationship-potential matrix across every pair of hidden attribute sets, producing likely conflicts, alliances and romances. This matrix is the seed from which every story the season can grow is generated.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks of board sessions; reserves held until the door closes

#### Participant Agreements & Releases

Contributor agreement, appearance release, confidentiality with liquidated damages, a depiction clause consenting to the edit and to unflattering portrayal, prize terms, image and exclusivity rights, aftercare obligations.

- **Who.** Production Counsel
- **Decides.** Stipend and prize terms, post-show exclusivity and holdback length, penalty size, which welfare promises are contractual rather than moral, and the dispute mechanism.
- **Engine.** Contract terms as enforceable state: a contributor leak either is or is not actionable; a depiction clause either does or does not cover the edit they later dispute in public.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks to paper; signed before anyone travels

#### Duty of Care Protocol

The written plan: informed consent process, on-site psychologist, safeguarding leads, alcohol policy, right to withdraw at any time, phone and social handover, family liaison, and defined aftercare beyond transmission.

- **Who.** Welfare Producer / Duty of Care Lead
- **Decides.** Welfare staffing ratios, alcohol rules, whether cast keep phones, aftercare duration, escalation thresholds, and who has authority to stop filming — including whether that person is independent of the EP.
- **Engine.** Welfare as continuous per-contributor state (sleep, isolation days, alcohol, conflict exposure, public exposure) driving breakdown risk, plus a regulator and press model that audits the protocol retrospectively after any incident.
- **Takes.** Written in prep; run daily through the shoot and for months after transmission

#### Game Rules & Compliance Sign-off

Rules covering every edge case, the adjudication chain, tie-breaks, disqualification, and the firewall preventing production from influencing outcomes. In the US, rigging a contest of intellectual skill is a federal offence (47 U.S.C. 509).

- **Who.** Production Counsel with broadcaster Standards & Practices / Compliance
- **Decides.** Who adjudicates and whether decisions are appealable, what producers may and may not say to players, prize bonding and tax withholding, and how a broken rule is remedied on camera.
- **Engine.** A rules object the simulation enforces: producer interventions are logged and can constitute breaches with escalating exposure — reshoot, disqualification, regulator finding, cancellation, criminal referral.
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks to draft; a live document all season

#### Challenge Design & Dream Team Testing

Designing, building and physically testing every game before contestants see it. Timing it, breaking it, finding the exploit, checking camera positions, and rehearsing the safety rig with crew bodies in the water.

- **Who.** Challenge Producer with the challenge department and a crew stand-in team
- **Decides.** Difficulty curve and target duration, whether it rewards skill, endurance or luck, camera and commentary plan, safety rigging, and the contingency if it ends in ninety seconds.
- **Engine.** Each challenge carries a duration distribution, jeopardy profile, injury risk, exploit vector and coverage cost. Untested challenges have fat tails in all four, and the tails are where the season is lost.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks build per major challenge; a full test day before each

#### Transformation Build & Reveal

On makeover and renovation formats a real physical build — house, garden, business, body, wardrobe — must finish to broadcast deadline, be concealed from the subject, and be revealed in one continuous covered moment.

- **Who.** Build Producer / Production Designer with contractors
- **Decides.** Scope versus days, what is real versus dressed, trade scheduling, how much the production pays for versus sponsors, and the choreography and camera plan of the reveal.
- **Engine.** A build schedule with trade dependencies, weather, permits and overrun risk, against an immovable reveal date; a concealment state that can fail; and a reveal that yields the episode's only irreplaceable emotional beat.
- **Takes.** 3 days to 12 weeks per build, depending on scale

#### Fixed-Rig Design & Tech Survey

Designing a set to be filmed from inside its own walls: camera positions behind mirrored glass, remote heads, cable routes, gallery adjacency, and sightlines arranged so no camera ever sees another camera.

- **Who.** Technical Manager / Broadcast Engineer with the Production Designer
- **Decides.** Camera count and mix (robotic, POV, hot-head, hand-held), microphone count and plant positions, lighting that works believably for 24 hours, and where the deliberate dead zones are.
- **Engine.** A spatial coverage model in which every point of the set has a camera coverage quality and a mic pickup quality. Uncovered space is precisely where the story you cannot use will happen.
- **Takes.** 4-12 weeks design; 2-6 weeks install

#### Rig Install, Timecode & Comms

Installing and commissioning the whole plant, then binding it together: a single time-of-day timecode across every camera, recorder and radio mic, talkback and comms to every crew position, and an RF frequency plan.

- **Who.** Rigging Engineer, Vision Engineer, A1
- **Decides.** Record architecture (in-camera versus central), codec and proxy strategy, timecode master, frequency allocation, and how much redundancy the budget will carry.
- **Engine.** Sync integrity as a real, tracked variable. Unjammed or drifting sources make multicam grouping fail downstream and cost assistant-editor days for every shoot day affected — a failure invisible on the day it happens.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks; re-checked every single day

#### Sequestration & Game Integrity

Isolating contributors from the outside world and, where the format requires, from each other: phone confiscation, chaperoned movement, silent crew, sealed jury houses, separated hotel floors, controlled food and mail.

- **Who.** Integrity Producer / Sequester Coordinator with contributor handlers
- **Decides.** Isolation strictness, handler-to-contributor ratio, what crew are permitted to say, how eliminated players are held until the final, and how family contact is granted as a story beat.
- **Engine.** An information-leak model where every contact event carries a contamination probability; a triggered leak invalidates a result and forces a rule adjudication, a reshoot, or a disclosure that the audience will judge.
- **Takes.** Continuous for the whole shoot; jury sequester can run for weeks after elimination

#### Story Bible & Arc Mapping

Before a frame is shot, the story department maps the arcs it hopes will exist: who the season is about, the likely turns, which relationships to cover heavily, and what a typical episode would be if the format runs to form.

- **Who.** Supervising Story Producer
- **Decides.** Point-of-view characters, the beat template for an episode, which questions every OTF must answer, and where to spend disproportionate coverage on a hunch.
- **Engine.** A story graph of hypothesised beats with confidence values, updated nightly against what the field actually delivered, accumulating explicit coverage debt for beats that were needed and never shot.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks to write; rewritten continuously

#### Daily Story Meeting & The Call

Every morning the story team reads yesterday's logs, decides what today's story is, and assigns story areas to each field crew. This is the closest unscripted ever comes to a shooting script, and it is one day long.

- **Who.** Story Producer with the Series Producer / Showrunner
- **Decides.** Which contributors get cameras today, which conversations to engineer, who is pushed together, which threads to close, and who is deliberately left uncovered.
- **Engine.** Assignment of finite crews to contributors, each pairing yielding beats as a function of crew skill, contributor state and situation. Unassigned contributors still generate off-camera events that you will hear about and cannot use.
- **Takes.** 45-90 minutes every shooting morning

#### Producing on the Fly

The core craft: setting up a situation, asking the question from behind the lens, holding silence until someone fills it, and knowing the moment to stop producing because the scene has started to run itself.

- **Who.** Field Producer / Shooting Producer-Director (UK: self-shooting PD)
- **Decides.** Intervene or observe; ask the direct question now or wait an hour; call crew in or hold back; and how hard to push a contributor who has started to come apart.
- **Engine.** An intervention dial per scene with two-sided cost: under-produce and bank nothing usable; over-produce and bank material that reads as fake, breaches integrity rules, or harms a contributor in a way that surfaces later.
- **Takes.** All day, every day; 10-14 hour days are standard

#### OTF / Confessional Interviewing

The on-the-fly or diary-room interview, in which the contributor narrates their own story in the present tense. It produces the connective tissue the whole edit runs on, including clean sentences that can carry any cut.

- **Who.** Field Producer or Story Producer, in the booth or a side room
- **Decides.** When to pull someone out, what to steer them to say, whether to feed the phrasing, how many takes, and whether to record the same line as both past and present tense for edit flexibility.
- **Engine.** Every OTF is a typed asset — subject, beat, tense, emotional register, standalone-usable flag. The edit fails weeks later precisely when the required connective line was never banked, and the failure is traceable to this decision.
- **Takes.** 20-60 minutes per contributor, several times a week

#### Multi-Camera Unscripted Coverage

Coverage without blocking or rehearsal: A-camera on the dominant speaker, B-camera hunting reactions, a roving operator for the unexpected, plus locked-off and POV cameras nobody is operating.

- **Who.** Director of Photography / Camera Supervisor
- **Decides.** Camera assignment per contributor, who follows whom, when to break and reposition and risk missing the turn, and whether to burn a camera on a hunch.
- **Engine.** Per-camera, per-beat coverage quality. The edit can only build a scene from what two cameras saw at usable angles, so reaction coverage is a scarce resource the story department is effectively spending in advance.
- **Takes.** Continuous through every shooting day

#### Control Room / Gallery Operation

On fixed-rig and studio formats the gallery watches every source live, cuts the live output, and — the part that matters most — drives robotic cameras onto a story in the two seconds before it becomes one.

- **Who.** Director with Technical Director (US) / Vision Mixer (UK), Vision Engineer, EVS operator, robotic camera operators
- **Decides.** Which source to record or take to air, where to point the robots, when to send a crew into the house, when to cut away from something unbroadcastable.
- **Engine.** A live multiviewer of N sources scanned under time pressure with limited attention. Anything no camera was pointed at is permanently unrecoverable — the only place in the pipeline where inattention destroys an asset outright.
- **Takes.** The length of the show; 24-hour formats run gallery shifts around the clock

#### Live Logging

Timecoded notes written as the action happens: who said what to whom, the emotional beat, whether it is usable. On 24-hour formats the log is the only index anyone will ever have of thousands of hours.

- **Who.** Log Producer with loggers in the gallery
- **Decides.** Tag taxonomy, logging depth, loggers per shift, and what counts as a beat worth recording at three in the morning.
- **Engine.** Logs as searchable records with accuracy and latency values. The edit can only find what was logged, so logging quality gates story discovery six weeks later — cheap to skimp on today, catastrophic in post.
- **Takes.** Live and continuous; around the clock in shifts on rig shows

#### Continuous Radio Mic Management

Every contributor is wired all day, every day. Battery swaps, re-rigs with every wardrobe change, frequency coordination across twenty or more packs, plant mics in every room, and a mix that keeps every source separate.

- **Who.** Production Sound Mixer with A2 / sound utility
- **Decides.** Iso tracks versus mix, plant placement, frequency plan, and who gets priority when the RF spectrum is congested.
- **Engine.** Per-contributor audio availability and quality tracked as state. An unusable track kills an otherwise perfect scene, and iso-track discipline determines whether the edit can construct a conversation at all.
- **Takes.** Continuous; 30-60 minutes of rigging per contributor per day

#### Ingest, Sync & Proxy Pipeline

Offload every card and recorder, checksum-verify, make two copies plus LTO, then build multicam groups from timecode across every camera and iso track, transcode proxies, and bin it so a moment can be found by person and day.

- **Who.** DIT / Media Manager with Assistant Editors
- **Decides.** Copy topology and verification level, shuttle versus upload, when cards may be wiped, naming and bin convention, and what gets synced first to unblock the story team.
- **Engine.** Storage as a costed resource in TB per day with a non-zero loss probability per unverified copy, plus assistant-editor hours as the true bottleneck: unsynced media is invisible to the edit, and the backlog caps story discovery.
- **Takes.** Runs behind the shoot all day and all night; roughly 1-2 assistant days per shoot day (estimate)

#### Transcription & Searchable Metadata

Everything spoken is transcribed and bound to timecode so the story team can search dialogue rather than watch footage. Increasingly machine-generated, always checked, and linked into the edit system for phrase search.

- **Who.** Transcription vendor with Story Assistants
- **Decides.** Machine versus human transcription, turnaround tier, whether transcripts are bound to clips in the NLE, and which material is left untranscribed to save money.
- **Engine.** A searchable corpus with an error rate and a latency. A story producer's ability to find a line is literally a query against this corpus, and the gaps in it are stories that will never be told.
- **Takes.** 24-72 hour turnaround per shoot day

#### Stringout Building

Before any cutting, every piece of material touching one subject is pulled into a single long sequence — every mention of the affair, every scene in the kitchen, every OTF about the vote. Stringouts run for hours.

- **Who.** Assistant Editor / Story Assistant
- **Decides.** Which stringouts exist (per character, per thread, per day), how ruthless the first pass is, and what is discarded before a story producer ever sees it.
- **Engine.** Stringouts as the intermediate object between raw hours and a cut. Their existence and quality determine which stories the edit can even perceive, and building them costs real, schedulable days.
- **Takes.** Days per episode; a 44-minute episode commonly starts from 40-100+ hours of selects (estimate)

#### Paper Cut

The script, written after photography: a card wall or document of beats with timecodes and quoted lines, act by act, telling the editor what the episode is and which real moments it is made of.

- **Who.** Story Producer
- **Decides.** The spine, the A/B/C stories, which turn lands on which act break, whose point of view carries the hour, the cold open, and what is cut out of existence.
- **Engine.** Beats as movable cards bound to real clips, with act-length targets and hard continuity constraints — clothing, daylight, who knew what when. An invalid arrangement must be detectably invalid before the edit starts.
- **Takes.** 3-7 days per episode

#### Editor's Assembly to Producer's Cut

The editor builds from the paper cut, then fights it: finding better material, killing beats that do not play, inventing structure the story team never saw. First assemblies routinely run 60-120 minutes for a 44-minute slot.

- **Who.** Editor with the Story Producer
- **Decides.** Pace, music, how long to hold a reaction, whether to reorder a night, how much is told in OTF versus action, and which of the story producer's beats to quietly lose.
- **Engine.** Editors as agents with speed, story instinct and comic or dramatic bias, producing roughly 2-4 finished minutes per day (estimate). Suite-weeks are therefore the backbone of the whole post schedule.
- **Takes.** 3-6 weeks per episode, overlapping across multiple suites

#### Story Editor Narrative Pass

The pass that makes reality legible: reordering events out of chronology, using a reaction filmed on another day, building a conversation from two cameras and one OTF line — and deciding where that stops being true.

- **Who.** Story Editor / Supervising Producer
- **Decides.** What reordering is honest, whether a line may be lifted from another context, how hard to push a contributor's characterisation, and whether to build a moment that did not occur in that shape.
- **Engine.** A truth ledger per beat — happened / reordered / composited / implied — producing an integrity score that drives contributor dispute risk, regulator exposure and press damage if the construction is ever exposed.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks per episode

#### Pickup Interviews to Patch Story

The edit finds a hole — nobody ever said why she walked out — so contributors are brought back weeks later to say it, dressed, lit and framed to match. Unscripted's only form of reshoot.

- **Who.** Field Producer with the Story Producer
- **Decides.** Whether to patch or cut around it, who comes back, how to phrase the question without putting the words in their mouth, and how to match continuity.
- **Engine.** Availability, cost and continuity-match quality per pickup. A contributor can refuse, have cut their hair, or now know how the season ended — which contaminates the answer in a way the audience can feel.
- **Takes.** Half a day per pickup; days of scheduling to get one person in a room

#### Broadcaster Notes Rounds

Cuts go up the chain and come back with notes. Unscripted notes are structural and about people — 'we do not care about her', 'the game is confusing', 'find a hero' — and can demand material that does not exist.

- **Who.** Showrunner / Executive Producer with the Commissioning Editor or streamer executive
- **Decides.** Which notes to take, which to argue, what is fixable in the cut versus needs a pickup versus is impossible, and how much relationship capital to spend refusing.
- **Engine.** Notes as constraints on the beat graph: some satisfiable from unused footage, some only by new shooting, some unsatisfiable at any price. Refusals cost standing with the buyer, tracked across the series and the next commission.
- **Takes.** 2-4 rounds of 2-5 days per episode

#### Narration Scripting & Record

Where a format uses narration it is written to near-locked picture, carries every piece of exposition the footage cannot, sells jeopardy into the break, and is recorded for several episodes in a single session.

- **Who.** Narration Writer — usually the only credited writer — with the host or voice artist
- **Decides.** How much the voice explains versus withholds, tone (wry, urgent, deadpan), how hard to push jeopardy, and whether narration is rescuing a cut that does not work.
- **Engine.** VO lines as timed objects that measurably raise comprehension of a confusing cut while imposing a quality penalty for telling rather than showing — the cheapest fix in post and the most visible one.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days writing per episode; 2-4 hours in the booth per session

#### Graphics Package & Data Automation

Name supers, leaderboards, vote counters, countdown clocks, maps, day cards. Designed once as a package in prep and templated so each episode is populated from data rather than designed from scratch.

- **Who.** Graphic Designer with the Post Producer
- **Decides.** The package design and how much information the show carries on screen, automation versus bespoke builds, and the branding rules a licensed format obliges you to follow.
- **Engine.** Templated graphics with per-episode data binding: near-zero marginal cost for templated items versus real cost per bespoke one, plus a legibility factor governing how comprehensible the game is to a casual viewer.
- **Takes.** 4-8 weeks in prep; hours per episode thereafter

#### Music: Library Scoring & Performance Clearance

Unscripted runs on production library music under blanket licence rather than composed score — cues chosen by function and reused all series. On talent formats, every performed song must additionally be arranged, played and cleared.

- **Who.** Music Supervisor / Dubbing Editor; on talent formats also the Musical Director
- **Decides.** Library versus commissioned theme versus commercial sync, how hard to score emotion, per-episode music budget, song choices for performances and whether they clear for international sale.
- **Engine.** A cue library with mood and tempo tags, per-use costs and territory clearance flags. A commercial sync costs an order of magnitude more and may block the format's own international exploitation.
- **Takes.** Continuous through the edit; song clearance runs 2-6 weeks ahead of each live show

#### Compliance & Legal Viewing

A line-by-line viewing for defamation, fairness to contributors, unfair-edit risk, undue prominence of brands, harm and offence — then the blur-and-bleep pass on logos, bystanders, documents and minors.

- **Who.** Compliance Editor with Production Counsel and broadcaster Standards & Practices
- **Decides.** What is removed, who gets a right of reply, what is blurred, language treatment for the slot and watershed, and whether the episode carries a warning or a helpline end card.
- **Engine.** A findings model: each flagged item carries a complaint likelihood, an upheld-finding severity and a remediation cost, weighted by slot, watershed and contributor vulnerability. Under UK rules this is a licence matter, not a PR one.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per episode, plus re-views after every change

#### Online, Mix & Delivery Versioning

Conform from proxies to full resolution, grade wildly mismatched sources — cinema camera, action cam, night vision, phone — into one look, mix dialogue recorded on radio mics in bathrooms, and build the deliverable set.

- **Who.** Online Editor, Colourist, Dubbing Mixer, Post Supervisor
- **Decides.** The look, loudness spec, how much to spend rescuing unusable audio, and the versioning matrix (broadcast, streaming, international textless, airline, clean-of-music).
- **Engine.** Per-source technical quality that grade and mix can only partly repair, and a deliverables checklist in which any missing item fails delivery, delays payment, and on a strip means missing air.
- **Takes.** 3-6 days per episode; hours on a fast-turnaround strip

#### Fast-Turnaround Nightly Cycle

On daily strips the whole pipeline collapses into hours: shoot through the evening, log and stringout overnight, cut before dawn, compliance at breakfast, deliver by afternoon, transmit tonight, repeat for eight weeks.

- **Who.** Series Producer with the Post Supervisor and shift-based story and edit teams
- **Decides.** Shift structure and handovers, how many parallel edit teams, what is pre-built in advance, and the hard lock-or-lose time each day beyond which a beat simply does not make the show.
- **Engine.** A rolling 24-hour queueing pipeline where every stage has a capacity and a latency. A late beat either displaces something else or misses air permanently, and crew fatigue degrades every stage simultaneously.
- **Takes.** 24-30 hours from event to transmission, daily, for 6-8 weeks

#### Live Transmission Show

Evictions, results shows and finals go out live with no post at all: a rundown timed to the second, a live audience, remote sources, and a result that has to be handled cleanly on air whatever it is.

- **Who.** Director, Live Producer, Floor Manager, Host, Production Manager on timings
- **Decides.** Running order, item durations, which item is dropped when the show over-runs, how the host handles a shock result, and what happens if a contributor refuses to leave.
- **Engine.** A running-order clock with per-item over-run probability, live failure modes (source loss, comms failure, an unbleeped word, a collapsing contributor) and the pipeline's only true irreversibility.
- **Takes.** 30-90 minutes on air; 1-2 days of camera scripting and rehearsal

#### Public Vote Operation & Audit

Running the audience vote across telephony, app and web: terms and the free-entry route, fraud and multiple-vote controls, regional reporting, and an independent audit of the count before any result goes to air.

- **Who.** Interactive Producer with an independent verification auditor
- **Decides.** Vote windows and price points, charity split, whether percentages are published, how a mid-vote technical failure is handled, and whether a void vote is announced or absorbed.
- **Engine.** A voting model with regional bias, organised block voting and fraud vectors, plus an audit that can fail. A mishandled vote is a serious regulatory breach and a criminal-adjacent scandal, not an embarrassment.
- **Takes.** Vote windows from minutes to days; audit in real time before the result

#### Live Feeds & Companion Content

Twenty-four-hour streams, clip channels, spin-off and aftershow programmes, and cast social accounts run by production while the contributors are still inside and unable to see any of it.

- **Who.** Digital Producer / Social Producer / Feed Director
- **Decides.** Feed cut-away rules for music, legal and welfare, what is clipped and promoted, and whether the feed is permitted to spoil the broadcast edit's reveals.
- **Engine.** An audience-knowledge state that can run ahead of the edit: feed-watchers already know outcomes, which devalues edit reveals, drives spoiler communities, and changes how the main show must be structured.
- **Takes.** Continuous through the run, on shifts

#### Aftercare & Transmission Support

The duty that starts when filming ends: pre-transmission viewing calls, social media and press handling, financial and representation advice, therapy provision, and proactive contact for months after the series has aired.

- **Who.** Welfare Producer with an independent psychologist and a press liaison
- **Decides.** Aftercare duration and intensity, who gets proactive outreach, whether a contributor is warned about their own edit before the country sees it, and how family are supported.
- **Engine.** A post-transmission harm model per contributor driven by edit portrayal, volume and valence of audience reaction, and pre-existing risk — with outcomes that reach the production, the broadcaster and the regulator.
- **Takes.** From weeks before transmission to 6-12+ months after

#### Format Sale & Local Adaptation

Selling the bible into other territories at market, then sending someone who ran the original to sit inside the local production and protect the non-negotiable format points while local culture reshapes everything else.

- **Who.** Format Executive at the distributor with a Consultant Producer, the 'flying producer'
- **Decides.** Which territories and in what order, licence fee and term, how prescriptive to be about local changes, who flies and for how many weeks, and when to refuse a sale that would damage the format.
- **Engine.** Per-territory fit, local tariff and success probability modulated by bible completeness and flying-producer weeks. A sale yields a licence fee — a single-digit percentage of the local budget is typical (estimate) — plus consultancy and a share of the tape.
- **Takes.** Markets are seasonal; sale to local transmission typically runs 6-18 months

---

## 6. Multi-camera sitcom shot before a live studio audience

*the network/streaming half-hour on a five-day weekly cycle, in both its film-style variant (four film or digital cinema cameras, no live switch, e.g. Friends, Frasier, The Big Bang Theory) and its tape/booth variant (broadcast cameras, a technical director cutting live, e.g. All in the Family, most Nickelodeon and Disney Channel half-hours).*

### The unit of work

The episode is the atom: one script becomes one finished show in one five-day week, twenty-two times a year. Inside the week the unit changes every day, and none of them is the live-action page-per-day.

Monday the unit is the timed read — minutes over slot, plus a map of which jokes died at a table of sixty people. Tuesday and Wednesday the unit is the scene on its feet and laughs per page: a multicam script runs 45-55 double-spaced pages for roughly 21 minutes of air (about 25-30 seconds a page, estimate), and the working expectation is three to five laughs a page, sixty to a hundred jokes in an episode. Thursday the unit is the numbered shot — on the order of 120-200 shots assigned across four cameras (estimate) — and a good day means every second of every scene is held by at least one camera in a usable size. Friday the unit is the scene pass, not the page: five to nine lettered scenes, each played whole, usually twice.

A good tape night is six scenes in four hours, no scene past three passes, fewer than a dozen alts thrown, the house still above three-quarters full at the last scene, and the show timing out under 23:00 before the trim. Nothing is ever measured in pages per day, because the entire script is shot in one night. The scarce resource is not daylight and not setups — it is audience. Roughly 240 minutes of usable room temperature, spent by every reset, reload, costume change and repeat.

Across the season the unit of progress is episodes in the can against episodes broken: the room must stay six to eight scripts ahead of the stage, and the loss condition is arriving at Monday with nothing shootable.

### What is absent

- Locations entirely — no scouting, no tech scout, no location manager or scouts, no permits, no location fees, no neighbourhood relations, no weather cover, no golden hour, no day-for-night. The show never leaves one stage for nine months.
- Company moves, unit moves, base camp, circus, honeywagons, a transportation department, drivers, picture cars, a picture-car coordinator.
- The stripboard. There is no Movie Magic board built from a script breakdown, no one-liner, no day-out-of-days driven by location and cast availability. The 'schedule' is a fixed weekly template identical for all twenty-two episodes, and the breakdown is trivial because every scene is on one stage with the same cast.
- Coverage as a sequence. There is no master, then singles, then inserts, then 'we'll get it in the coverage.' All sizes exist simultaneously or they do not exist at all.
- Per-setup relighting. The grid is hung once a season and the sets are lit once a week; there is no lighting unit moving through a day.
- Second unit, splinter unit, aerial unit, marine unit, insert unit.
- Storyboards and previs for anything but a rare effects gag.
- Steadicam, cranes, technocranes, dolly track, drones. Cameras live on pedestals or small dollies in a thirty-foot lane and never leave it.
- On-set VFX supervision, plates, greenscreen, a vendor pipeline, a VFX producer. Most episodes have zero effects shots.
- A DIT with a look pipeline, and meaningful colour grading. The show is flat, bright and high-key by design and the grade is a pass, not a process.
- ADR as routine practice. It is actively avoided because replacing a line desynchronises it from the laugh recorded on top of it.
- A score. There is a main title, an end title and three or four stingers of two to five seconds. No composer writing to picture across an episode, no music supervisor, no needle-drop budget.
- Foley and sound design at feature scale. The dominant sound element is a crowd that was in the room.
- Test screenings, preview cards and recruited audiences. The audience test already happened, during principal photography, at full volume.
- A long director's prep. There are no weeks of scouting, department head meetings and shot listing; the multicam director's prep is the rehearsal week itself, and many multicams use one or two directors for most of a season rather than a rotating roster.
- A locked script. There is no such document before Friday evening, and frequently not then — lines change between takes with 250 people watching.
- A standing stunt department, an armourer, animal wranglers, or a special-effects unit on the weekly pattern.
- Night shoots, split days, turnaround violations from a location wrap, the sixth and seventh day of a week.
- Dailies as the director's feedback loop. Feedback arrived live, from a crowd, before the take was over.
- The 1st AD as the engine of the shooting day. Those duties are split between the Stage Manager on the floor and the Associate Director in the booth, and the day they run is four hours long, not fourteen.

### What is reordered

- Rewriting is not upstream of production — it runs through and inside it. The script is rewritten after the table read, after the producers' run-through, after the network run-through, after the dress rehearsal, and between takes with the audience seated. Development never ends; it ends at wrap.
- Rehearsal precedes camera prep instead of being a luxury bought before the shoot. Three of five days are rehearsal, and camera work is designed onto blocking the cast already owns, rather than blocking being invented at the camera.
- Studio and network notes land mid-production, twice a week, on a show already on its feet — not only at outline and draft stage. The note-giving apparatus is a weekly production department, not a development phase.
- Casting for everyone but the regulars happens the week of shooting. A guest can be cast Monday and taped Friday, off-book, playing to a live crowd.
- Editorial decisions are made before the shoot, not after it. Camera blocking is the edit: what is not assigned to a camera on Thursday does not exist on Monday, and the line cut is an assembly made live.
- The audience test — which in features is a preview screening months after the shoot, and in other TV is never — happens during principal photography, and its result is fed back into the script within ninety seconds.
- The largest element of the sound mix is captured in production, not created in post. The audience is a production department, not a post one.
- Design and construction of a swing set happen after the script is written and four days before it is shot, instead of months earlier in prep. The biggest construction bill of all — the standing sets — is paid before episode one rather than spread across the schedule.
- Performance peaks are deliberately scheduled rather than maximised. Some scenes are under-rehearsed on purpose so the cast is fresh for the audience, inverting the normal rehearse-until-perfect order.
- 'Fix it in post' is often 'shoot it again Tuesday.' Reshoots are near, cheap and routine because the sets stand and the cast is on payroll, so the reshoot decision sits inside the production week rather than at the end of the schedule.
- Marketing assets are cut by the network out of a finished episode weeks before air, while the production is still shooting episodes twelve weeks later in story — promo and production run concurrently for the whole season rather than sequentially.

### How the crew differs

- PRESENT HERE, UNKNOWN ON A FILM SET — Technical Director: switches the live line cut on a vision mixer from the booth on tape-model shows. There is no equivalent job in single-camera production.
- Associate Director (DGA): sits in the booth readying shots ('ready three, take three'), keeps the shot count against the script supervisor's numbering, and runs the technical side of the take.
- Stage Manager (DGA): the director's representative on the floor. Cues cast and audience, calls the roll, and carries duties a live-action 1st AD would, but for a four-hour show rather than a fourteen-hour day.
- Video Controller / Shader: matches exposure, gamma and colour across four cameras live from a CCU so the switched cut does not jump. On film-style multicam the DP absorbs this by lighting flat enough that four cameras match.
- Engineer in Charge and video engineers on tape shows — a broadcast-engineering department that simply does not exist in film production.
- Warm-up Comedian: a paid performer whose entire job is the emotional state of the audience. Works the house before the first scene and fills every reset, for four to six hours.
- Audience Coordinator and a third-party ticketing house (Audiences Unlimited / On Camera Audiences, 1iota): release free tickets, overbook against no-shows, queue, screen and seat 150-350 strangers a week.
- Eight to twelve writers physically standing on the stage on tape night, pitching new lines to actors between takes. On a film set the writer is a guest; here the room is a production department.
- Writers' Assistant and Script Coordinator running a versioned script at overnight cadence, burning five to eight revision colours a week.
- Four camera operators shooting the same moment at once, plus cable pullers and camera utility keeping four cables out of four frames.
- Lighting Director with a console operator holding recorded presets per set and per scene — a television role layered on top of, or in place of, a gaffer working setup by setup.
- Audience Reaction Editor / laugh sweetener in post: shapes, lifts, trims and supplements the captured audience track. A whole craft that exists only because of this format.
- Studio Teacher and welfare worker where minors are series regulars — a permanent fixture on Nickelodeon and Disney Channel multicams, and a hard constraint on the shooting clock.
- A second team of stand-ins used three days a week so the cast can be in the rewrite room while the sets are lit.
- Camera Coordinator on larger shows, maintaining one shot list across four camera cards through a week of rewrites.
- House PA operator and announcer feeding the risers, and an A2 running the overhead audience microphone array.
- ABSENT HERE, ASSUMED EVERYWHERE ELSE — Location Manager, assistant location managers, scouts, permit coordinators. There is no locations department because there are no locations.
- Transportation Captain, drivers, picture car coordinator, base camp. Nothing moves.
- Steadicam operator, crane and technocrane technicians, drone operator, and on tape shows even the dolly grip: the operator steers the pedestal himself.
- A grip department of any size. On a permanently rigged, flatly lit stage there is almost no daily grip work.
- DIT with a look pipeline, and a colourist doing meaningful grading.
- VFX Supervisor and VFX Producer. Most episodes have no effects shots at all.
- Stunt coordinator on the weekly pattern; armourer; animal wranglers.
- 2nd AD, 2nd 2nd AD and the paperwork apparatus around a large crew and background; background casting at scale.
- A composer writing to picture, a music editor working across a whole episode, a music supervisor.
- Foley artists and sound designers at feature scale. The dominant sound element was recorded live from a crowd.
- A unit publicist on set daily, and the marketing apparatus around a theatrical release.

### What goes wrong

- The audience does not laugh — and you find out in front of them, with a hundred crew on the clock and no way to hide it. No other production form can fail publicly, in real time, at the moment of capture.
- Laugh decay across takes. Take two of a joke never returns take one's laugh, so the best performance and the best laugh routinely live in different takes and have to be married in post.
- Audience attrition. A house told four hours stays five; the back rows empty; the last scene of the show, often the emotional payoff, gets the thinnest laughs of the night and needs the most sweetening.
- A bad house. A tour group, a block of non-native speakers, a giveaway crowd, or just a quiet Tuesday. The same script gets measurably different results on different nights, and the player cannot separate a bad script from a bad sample.
- The audience laughs at the wrong thing — a flub, a prop failing, an actor breaking. The take is unusable, and the real joke thirty seconds later now plays into a spent room.
- Over-punching. A warm, character-true joke is replaced at two in the morning by a cleverer one that the room laughed at and the audience will not.
- The room laughs and the audience does not. The room is not the audience, has heard everything four times, and is paid to laugh at each other.
- The show times out at 25:00 for a 21:00 slot because the laughs made it long — so jokes that worked have to be cut for being funny.
- A network note on Wednesday night guts the B story, and the room has fourteen hours to rebuild it before camera blocking locks the shot list.
- An overnight rewrite invents a location the shop cannot build in four days, with the bay already holding last week's strike.
- Camera sees camera on a cross, or a boom clean in three frames dips into the fourth. Nobody catches it live, and the angle is simply gone.
- A moment nobody assigned to a camera — a reaction, an entrance, the listener on the biggest joke in the scene — does not exist. There is no coverage to fall back on, because coverage was decided on Thursday.
- All four cameras run out of film or card at the same moment and the house sits in silence while four magazines are changed.
- A reset fails: no duplicate cake, a wardrobe change that takes twelve minutes, a practical that will not re-rig. The warm-up runs out of material and the room goes cold.
- An actor goes up repeatedly in front of 250 people. Confidence collapses, the audience starts pitying rather than laughing, and the room's temperature never comes back that night.
- A guest actor who was brilliant in the room cannot play to an audience. The part gets shot in pieces after the house leaves and then has no laughs at all.
- A table-read casualty: a performance that dies Monday morning forces a recast by Tuesday with three days of rehearsal left and the network's approval to obtain.
- Minors' permitted work hours expire mid-scene on a kids' multicam, with an audience already seated and the scene half shot.
- A medical event in the bleachers, or a fire-marshal capacity problem, halts a taping outright.
- Sweetening that reads as fake. Lifted, stretched and library laughs push an episode past the point where the home viewer hears the machine — a reputational failure the show cannot undo.
- ADR anywhere near a joke destroys the relationship between the line and the laugh recorded on top of it, so the routine post fix of every other format is unavailable.
- The showrunner cannot be in the room and on the stage at once. Every hour on the floor Thursday is an hour the next script does not get, and the deficit compounds across twenty-two weeks.
- Room burnout. Twenty-two weeks of two a.m. finishes degrades output quality invisibly, and the first symptom is an episode that reads flat at the table with nobody able to say why.
- A hit's cast renegotiates mid-run, and a budget built entirely on a stable repeating pattern is destroyed by the one line item that can double.
- An episode dies on the night and the choice is to reshoot it next week — at the cost of next week's tape night — or to air it as it is.
- Stale runners. A catchphrase that carried two seasons stops getting a laugh, and the show discovers it live, in front of a crowd, with the line already shot.
- The pre-shoot trap: every scene moved off the audience's night is a scene whose laughs must be invented, and the more you protect yourself the more manufactured the episode sounds.
- Cued applause on an entrance reads as canned at home, so the stage manager's safety net becomes the thing critics point at.
- Studio and network give directly contradictory notes on the same run-through, with the rewrite due that night and both parties expecting compliance by Thursday.
- Page version drift: a camera card, a prop, or a costume built off a page that was superseded at one in the morning, discovered when the audience is already in the seats.

### The shape of the money

Multicam money is front-loaded, fixed and cast-dominated, and the pattern almost never moves once set.

CAPITAL FIRST. The season's largest construction bill is paid before episode one: four to eight standing sets plus a pre-rigged lighting grid on a leased stage, commonly $1-2M as one-time capital (estimate), amortised across twenty-two episodes. After that the weekly pattern is nearly flat — same stage, same crew, same grid, no locations, no company moves, no weather. The marginal cost of episode 22 is materially lower than episode 1, which is the entire economic argument for the form.

LEVEL. A first-season network multicam half-hour lands roughly $1.3-1.8M per episode (estimate), against a single-camera comedy at $2-3.5M and an hour drama at $4-9M. The gap is almost entirely locations, transport, extra camera/grip/electric days and a post schedule three times as long.

PROPORTIONS on a mid-run network multicam at around $2M an episode (estimate): cast 30-45%; writing and producing fees 12-20%; director 1-2%; the stage crew below the line — camera, electric, sound, props, set dressing, wardrobe, hair and makeup, stage crew — 20-28%; swing sets and construction 4-8%; stage and facility 3-5%; post, including editorial, mix, online and music, 5-8%. Audience, warm-up and seating together are well under 1% of the budget and are the single biggest determinant of whether the episode works.

TWO EXTREME SKEWS. First, cast. Multicam hits produce the largest per-episode actor deals in television: Friends' six leads reached $1M each per episode in seasons nine and ten; The Big Bang Theory's three original leads reached $1M each from season eight. Cast can pass 60% of the budget and reduce the whole below-the-line pattern to a rounding correction. Second, the things that dominate other formats are nearly nothing here: an episode carries a main title, an end title and three or four two-to-five-second stingers, so original score runs under two minutes, and visual effects are usually zero.

POST IS CHEAP because the show was cut while it was shot. Flat high-key lighting needs almost no grading, ADR is avoided on principle, the mix is a day, and the reference cut already exists as the line cut. Where a single-camera show spends on setups, moves and post, a multicam spends on people who are on camera and people who write jokes.

THE BUSINESS SITS OUTSIDE THE EPISODE. Studios deficit-finance against the network licence fee and monetise the library; the number that matters is eighty-eight to a hundred episodes, enough to strip five days a week in syndication. Multicam exists because the per-episode cost of reaching a hundred episodes is the lowest in scripted television — and because the live audience gives you, for free, the one thing money cannot buy: an honest answer about whether it is funny.

### Processes that exist only here

#### Pattern Budget and Season Order

Builds one repeating weekly cost pattern for all 22 episodes plus the one-time capital: standing sets, stage lease, pre-rigged grid. The season's money is largely spent before episode one airs a frame.

- **Who.** Line Producer / Unit Production Manager
- **Decides.** Which stage and lot, how many standing sets to build, the swing allowance per episode, crew size and rates, how many dark weeks, and the per-episode cushion for when the room overwrites.
- **Engine.** Two-tier cost model: amortised capital (sets, grid, camera package, stage lease) against per-episode variable (swing set, guest cast, overtime, resets). Deficit accrues against a fixed licence fee; episode 22 must cost less than episode 1.
- **Takes.** Four to eight weeks before the first table read; revised every episode

#### Staffing the Room

Hires eight to twelve writers across the WGA ladder, Co-EP down to Staff Writer, plus Writers' Assistant and Script Coordinator, for a room that must hand the stage a shootable script every week for nine months.

- **Who.** Showrunner / Executive Producer
- **Decides.** Ladder shape, ratio of joke writers to story writers, who can be trusted to run a punch-up unsupervised, and who goes to the stage on tape night versus who stays in the room.
- **Engine.** Writers as agents with typed stats: joke velocity, joke-type specialty, structure, character voice, stamina, and a burnout clock that degrades output after consecutive all-nighters. Room throughput is a function of composition, not headcount.
- **Takes.** Four to six weeks during staffing season, typically May to June for a fall start

#### Season Arc and Story Area

The whiteboard of the season's engine: relationship moves, runners, guest arcs, sweeps episodes, the finale. Multicam arcs run shallow because a fixed set of rooms and a fixed ensemble must return to rest each week.

- **Who.** Showrunner with the room
- **Decides.** Which relationships move and when, how many episodes a move consumes, which runners recur, what is held for sweeps, and which weeks are cheap bottle episodes on standing sets only.
- **Engine.** Series-state vector of relationship states, running gags with freshness decay, and character knowledge. Each episode consumes and returns state. Callbacks lose laugh value with repetition and recover with rest on an explicit fatigue curve.
- **Takes.** One to two weeks before the room opens; revisited continuously

#### Breaking the Story

Two to four days at the board turning a pitch into a beat sheet: cold open, Act One out, Act Two, tag, built so every scene lands in an existing set and the ensemble splits into pairings that generate jokes.

- **Who.** The room, led by the Showrunner
- **Decides.** The A/B/C story split, which characters pair off, which set each scene plays in, where the act break lands, and whether the episode needs a swing set at all.
- **Engine.** Beat graph under hard constraints: set availability, a cast-pairing chemistry matrix, five to nine scenes, an act-break requirement, and a per-scene joke-density expectation the eventual draft is scored against.
- **Takes.** Two to four days per episode, running six to eight episodes ahead of the stage

#### Outline and Outline Notes

A five to ten page beat outline goes to the studio, then the network. Two sets of notes arrive from parties with different interests and no obligation to agree; the showrunner reconciles them.

- **Who.** Assigned writer; studio and network current executives
- **Decides.** Which notes to take, which to absorb cosmetically, which to fight, and whether to take a note now in order to spend the goodwill later on something that matters more.
- **Engine.** Two note-giving agents with separate objective functions, memory of past compliance, and a relationship meter. Notes carry an implementation cost in room-hours and a political cost to refuse; refusal is gated on the meter.
- **Takes.** Two to three days to write, twenty-four to forty-eight hours per notes pass

#### First Draft and the Showrunner's Pass

The assigned writer delivers; the showrunner rewrites, often heavily, into the version the company will read. WGA credit stays with the assigned writer unless the room's work crosses the arbitration threshold.

- **Who.** Credited writer, then Showrunner
- **Decides.** How much to rewrite, whether to send the writer back for another pass, whether to throw it out and put the room on it, and how much of the writer's voice to protect.
- **Engine.** Draft object with per-scene quality, per-joke authorship tags, and divergence from the outline. Credit arbitration on cumulative authorship share. A writer whose drafts are always rewritten loses standing and eventually the job.
- **Takes.** Five to ten days for the writer, one to three days for the showrunner, usually overnight

#### The Table Draft

The whole room rewrites to table-read shape: a page-one punch, an act-out button, every scene checked for laughs per page, and a timed length that comes in near target before any real laughs are added on top.

- **Who.** The room
- **Decides.** Which scenes survive, which jokes go in, the target page count, and whether to protect a fragile story beat with a joke or cut the beat entirely.
- **Engine.** Every line carries spoken seconds; every joke carries an expected hold in seconds. Timed length is dialogue plus action plus holds, so a funnier draft is a longer draft. Room estimates of laugh size are noisy against true audience response.
- **Takes.** One to three days; the draft goes out the night before the read

#### Script Coordination and Colour Revisions

Issues, proofs, formats and distributes every revision in multicam format, lettered scenes and double-spaced dialogue, in standard revision colours. A single week can burn five to eight colours.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator
- **Decides.** Full reissue versus page revisions, when to issue, who gets pages when, and whether the stage can absorb another change before camera blocking locks the shot list.
- **Engine.** Versioned script with per-page colour history and a distribution graph: every department holds a version, and a department working off a stale page produces a downstream failure, wrong prop, wrong set piece, wrong line on a camera card.
- **Takes.** Continuous; a full overnight reissue takes two to four hours

#### Episode Slotting and Dark Weeks

Lays twenty-two episodes across roughly thirty calendar weeks: blocks of three or four production weeks, a dark week to let the room catch up, a two-week winter hiatus, and held dates for reshoots.

- **Who.** Line Producer with the Showrunner
- **Decides.** When to go dark, which script is ready enough to be next, whether to reorder to buy the room another week, and where in the run to put the expensive episode.
- **Engine.** A queue of scripts at readiness states feeding a fixed stage calendar. Room throughput against stage consumption. Going dark costs money and buys script quality; running out of shootable script is a loss condition.
- **Takes.** Set before the season, renegotiated every few weeks

#### Standing Set Build and Bay Layout

Four to eight permanent three-walled sets in a row along one stage facing the audience risers, with wild walls, a camera lane in front, and one or two bays left empty for swing sets.

- **Who.** Production Designer, Art Director, Construction Coordinator
- **Decides.** Set order along the stage, sightline angles, which walls fly out, how wide the camera lane runs, where entrances sit, and how many swing bays to keep free all season.
- **Engine.** Persistent 2D stage plan with per-set footprint, wall states fixed or wild, entrances, and sightlines to the risers. This layout permanently constrains camera positions, lighting and what the audience can see, for the whole series.
- **Takes.** Six to ten weeks before the first episode; struck only at series end

#### Swing Set Design and Build

A set built into an empty bay for one episode, a restaurant or a hospital room or an office, because a script finished four days ago said so. It is struck the morning after the tape.

- **Who.** Production Designer, Construction Coordinator
- **Decides.** Build versus redress an existing set versus rewrite the scene onto a standing set, and how much of the week's swing allowance to spend on it.
- **Engine.** Lead-time model: construction needs days the schedule may not have. A revision that invents a location after the build lock forces a rewrite, a redress, or an overtime build. Bay capacity is finite and shared with the previous episode's strike.
- **Takes.** Three to five days to build, struck overnight after tape

#### Weekly Guest Casting

Guest and day-player roles cast the week of, sometimes the day of, the table read, with approvals from both studio and network, for actors who must be off-book and audience-ready in three days.

- **Who.** Casting Director, Showrunner, studio and network casting
- **Decides.** Read for the network or offer only, theatre-trained versus film actor, and whether to write the part smaller because nobody good is available on four days' notice.
- **Engine.** Availability and approval gates plus a hidden stat: live-audience capability. Actors who read brilliantly in a room can freeze in front of 250 people, and the player only learns this at the run-through or on the night.
- **Takes.** Two to five days, concurrent with prep

#### The Table Read

Monday morning, everyone at a long table, the script read aloud once. The first and cleanest test of the material, and the only one that happens before any money has been spent shooting it.

- **Who.** Whole company: cast, room, director, department heads, studio and network executives
- **Decides.** Who attends and where they sit, whether to warm the room first, whether to have the showrunner read stage directions, and whether to read the fragile scene at all.
- **Engine.** A read-through laugh pass with its own response curve: forty to sixty industry listeners, polite-laugh inflation, executive silence, an attendance effect. Correlated with true audience response but well below one.
- **Takes.** Forty-five to seventy minutes

#### Timing the Read

A stopwatch on the read, act by act, producing a timed length. A read at 26:00 against a 21:00 slot means five minutes leave the script this week, and the laughs added later will put more back.

- **Who.** Script Supervisor
- **Decides.** Nothing directly. It produces the single number every other decision this week is measured against.
- **Engine.** Timed length with a per-act breakdown, a laugh-inflation factor converting read time into estimated tape time, and a hard slot target. Length is a constraint the entire week is scored against, not a note.
- **Takes.** Delivered within fifteen minutes of the read

#### The Notes Session

Immediately after the read, in a room, everyone with an opinion gives it. The showrunner takes notes from two masters and decides in front of both what will have changed by tomorrow morning.

- **Who.** Showrunner, room, studio executives, network executives
- **Decides.** Which notes to take, whether a story is broken enough to rebreak, and what to promise in the room versus what to actually do overnight.
- **Engine.** Notes as typed objects, story, character, joke, length, standards, brand, each with a source, a cost in room-hours, and a compliance check at the next run-through. Unaddressed notes escalate and escalation spends relationship capital.
- **Takes.** Thirty to ninety minutes

#### The Overnight Rewrite

The room eats dinner and rewrites, often to two or four in the morning: cut to length, rebreak scenes, and replace every joke that did not land at the read.

- **Who.** The room
- **Decides.** Cut for length or punch for laughs, patch a story or rebreak it, who goes home, when to call it, and whether to burn the room's stamina tonight or save it for Wednesday.
- **Engine.** Pitch generation: composition and stamina yield K pitches per open slot per hour; the showrunner selects on a noisy estimate of true strength. Stamina depletes and degrades both pitch volume and the accuracy of the showrunner's judgement.
- **Takes.** Four to twelve hours, ending when pages must be typed and copied

#### Blocking Rehearsal

The director walks the cast through the episode in the real sets, deciding entrances, business, moves and marks, while a writer sits at the back noting every joke that stopped working on its feet.

- **Who.** Director with the cast on the floor
- **Decides.** Where actors stand and move, who sits and who stands, which business carries a joke, how hard to cheat an actor out to the fourth wall, and how many reps a scene gets.
- **Engine.** Blocking as timed position tracks per actor on the stage plan. Positions determine later camera coverage, sightlines, and whether the audience can see the face the joke is on. Reps raise performance to a peak and then stale it.
- **Takes.** Three to five hours a day on days two and three

#### Producers' Run-Through

The show played straight through on its feet for the producers, with the room standing along the wall. Jokes are judged by whether twenty people who have read the script four times still laugh at them.

- **Who.** Director, cast, showrunner, room, department heads
- **Decides.** What is cut, rewritten or reblocked, whether an actor is wrong for a line, and whether a scene has to move to a different set.
- **Engine.** A run-through laugh pass from a jaded sample: jokes the room has already heard carry heavy decay, so silence here is ambiguous between stale and bad. That ambiguity must stay unresolvable until an audience sees it.
- **Takes.** Thirty to forty-five minutes, plus an hour of notes

#### Network and Studio Run-Through

The same show played again later in the week for the people who pay for it. They see one performance and give notes on it, and the cast, knowing they are being evaluated, plays it bigger than they will on the night.

- **Who.** Network and studio executives, showrunner, director, cast
- **Decides.** Which note to fight, whether to shield a cast member from an executive note, and whether to stage the run-through to hide the weak scene or expose it and ask for help.
- **Engine.** Executive agents with persistent preferences, memory of prior notes, and a relationship meter gating refusal. Cast performance inflates under executive gaze, which corrupts the signal the player is trying to read.
- **Takes.** Thirty to forty-five minutes, plus a notes call of thirty to sixty minutes

#### Punch-Up
