The room goes through the script line by line replacing weak jokes with better ones. Pitches fly, the showrunner picks, the Writers' Assistant types. The best joke in the episode is often pitched here at one in the morning.

- **Who.** The room around a table, page by page
- **Decides.** Which lines are open, how long to stay on a slot before moving on, whose pitch goes in, and whether to protect the original writer's joke on principle.
- **Engine.** Slot-by-slot pitch auction with a time cost per slot and decaying marginal return: the tenth pitch is rarely better than the third. Over-punching swaps a warm joke for a cleverer, colder one, a real and punishable failure.
- **Takes.** Two to six hours, most nights of the week

#### Second Team and Lighting the Sets

Stand-ins walk the blocking so the sets can be lit for four cameras at once: flat, soft, high-key, no camera casting a shadow into another's frame, and every mark usable from every angle.

- **Who.** Director of Photography, Gaffer or Lighting Director, stand-ins
- **Decides.** Key direction and level per set, how much modelling to risk, practical placement, whether to relight for one scene or accept the compromise, and where the cheats live.
- **Engine.** Lighting as a per-set state that must satisfy four frustums simultaneously. Any directional choice flattering one camera degrades another. Relighting costs stage time; relighting during the taping costs audience.
- **Takes.** Two to four hours on days three and four; the grid itself is rigged once a season

#### Wardrobe, Hair and the Change Plan

Fittings early in a week whose script keeps changing, plus a plan for scenes that will be played twice in order, with quick changes made behind the set while an audience sits waiting.

- **Who.** Costume Designer, Costume Supervisor, Key Hair and Makeup
- **Decides.** How many changes, which happen on the floor and which in the room, how many duplicates of a distressed costume to build, and what can be pre-rigged under the previous costume.
- **Engine.** Each change has a duration that becomes dead air on tape night. Duplicates cost money and buy takes. Continuity state persists per take so the editor cannot marry a take with a stain to one without.
- **Takes.** Fittings days one and two; changes rehearsed at camera blocking

#### Props, Practicals and the Reset

Every practical the script asks for must work and must reset in minutes, because the scene will be played at least twice while an audience watches the reset happen.

- **Who.** Property Master, Set Dressing, Special Effects
- **Decides.** Practical or cheat, how many duplicates to build, whether a gag is worth the reset it costs, and whether to move it into a pre-shoot instead of risking it live.
- **Engine.** Every prop gag carries a reset time and a duplicate count. Reset time is subtracted from audience patience; exhausting duplicates ends the scene's takes. A gag that fails takes a take with it.
- **Takes.** Prep across the week; reset rehearsed at camera blocking

#### Camera Blocking

A full day walking the show shot by shot with the cameras. Every shot assigned, numbered, sized and rehearsed, with four operators working a lane thirty feet wide without shooting each other.

- **Who.** Director, four Camera Operators, DP, Script Supervisor, Camera Coordinator
- **Decides.** Which camera takes which shot, the sizes, who holds the master, when a camera moves and when it stays, and where one camera gives up a shot so another can have it clean.
- **Engine.** The coverage matrix: for every second of every scene, which camera holds which subject, at what size, clean or dirty, usable or not. Post can only ever use what this matrix contains. It is the artefact linking shoot to edit.
- **Takes.** Six to ten hours, the day before tape

#### Cross-Shooting and Camera Clearance

The outer cameras shoot across each other for singles while the inner cameras hold the masters. Every cross risks a camera, a boom or an operator appearing in another camera's frame, and the geometry changes with every reblock.

- **Who.** Camera Operators, DP, Director
- **Decides.** Pedestal positions and heights, lens choice, how far to cross, whether to accept a dirty foreground, and which shot to sacrifice to clear another.
- **Engine.** Real frustum geometry on the stage plan, with camera bodies, operators, pedestals, booms and set walls as occluders. Clearance is computed, not asserted, so a late blocking change can silently ruin a shot nobody checks until dailies.
- **Takes.** Continuous through camera blocking, re-checked after every page change

#### Shot Numbering and Camera Cards

Every shot gets a number. Each operator's card, taped to the camera, lists their shots in order with size, subject and move. On a tape show the Associate Director readies from the same list.

- **Who.** Script Supervisor and Camera Coordinator
- **Decides.** Shot granularity, how much an operator holds versus follows, and how many shot changes an operator can execute in one continuous scene before something gets missed.
- **Engine.** Per-operator shot queue with execution difficulty and a realistic miss rate under time pressure. A late page change must propagate to every card or an operator executes a stale shot. Card version mismatch is a modelled failure state.
- **Takes.** Built during camera blocking, reissued after every rewrite

#### The Boom Plot

Two booms on platforms must cover a whole scene played continuously, from above four simultaneous frames, without dipping into any of them or throwing a shadow across a flatly lit set.

- **Who.** Production Sound Mixer, two Boom Operators, Utility
- **Decides.** Boom positions and arcs, which lines each boom takes, where to plant a mic instead, whether to wire an actor, and which frame takes the compromise.
- **Engine.** Boom as a swept volume tested per second against all four frustums and the light plot. Uncovered lines read thin in the edit; a dip loses a take in exactly one angle, which the editor must then work around.
- **Takes.** Planned at camera blocking, adjusted at dress

#### Audience Mic Array and Laugh Capture

Six to twelve microphones hung over the risers record the audience on isolated tracks. This is the raw material of the laugh track and the only objective record of whether the show actually worked.

- **Who.** Production Sound Mixer, A2
- **Decides.** Array pattern and height, how much house PA bleed to accept, gain structure so a big laugh does not clip, and whether to record the warm-up for later use.
- **Engine.** Audience response captured as a separate multitrack stem with per-joke amplitude and decay length. Post can only shape what was captured; a clipped or thin capture cannot be repaired without going to library laughs.
- **Takes.** Rigged once, checked every tape night

#### Pre-Shoots

Anything the audience cannot usefully watch, swing sets far from the risers, stunts, effects, children past their hours, scenes needing a lighting change, shot in the afternoon before the house arrives.

- **Who.** Full company, no audience
- **Decides.** What to pre-shoot and therefore what forfeits a live laugh, how much of the day to spend, and whether to protect a difficult scene from the audience or risk it live.
- **Engine.** Pre-shot material has no live audience track, so its laughs must be manufactured later at an authenticity cost, and it cannot be rewritten in response to an audience. Every pre-shoot trades risk for a laugh you will never get.
- **Takes.** Two to five hours on day four and the afternoon of tape day

#### Dress Rehearsal

A full camera run in costume without the audience, hours before the taping. The last chance to find a missing shot, a blown reset or a costume change that cannot physically be made in time.

- **Who.** Full company
- **Decides.** Fix or accept each defect, whether to cut a shot, whether to hand the cast new pages this late, and what to warn the warm-up comic about.
- **Engine.** Dress produces a defect list with per-defect fix costs in minutes against a hard house call time. Pages issued after dress carry a cold-performance penalty and a flub probability that persists into the taping.
- **Takes.** Sixty to ninety minutes plus notes, ending an hour before the house opens

#### Audience Booking and Seating

Free tickets released and overbooked against no-shows to fill 150 to 350 raked seats. The house is queued, screened, seated and told the rules. Who is in those seats is a variable the show does not control.

- **Who.** Audience Coordinator and a ticketing house
- **Decides.** Overbooking ratio, whether to paper the house with paid fillers, demographic targeting, how tightly to seat, and how long to hold the queue outside in the cold.
- **Engine.** Audience as a sampled population with per-person comedy literacy, show fit, fatigue rate and contagion susceptibility. Seat density raises contagion; a half-empty house laughs measurably smaller than a packed one.
- **Takes.** Tickets released weeks ahead; the house is seated in thirty to forty-five minutes

#### The Warm-Up

A comic works the house before and throughout the taping: explains the process, raises the room's temperature, fills every reset, gives away merchandise, and keeps 250 strangers alive for four to six hours.

- **Who.** Warm-up Comedian
- **Decides.** How hard to work the room before scene one, when to spend material and when to save it, whether to bring the cast out, and whether to let a stalling reset show or cover it.
- **Engine.** Room temperature as a decaying state raised by warm-up quality and by laughter itself, lowered by waiting, resets and repeats. Every laugh in the show is scaled by it, and the comic's stock of material depletes across the night.
- **Takes.** Twenty to thirty minutes before scene one, then continuously

#### Audience Cueing

Cues audience applause on entrances and at scene ends, cues the cast, calls the roll, and manages where the room is looking, the sets or the overhead monitors, during pre-shot playback.

- **Who.** Stage Manager
- **Decides.** When to cue applause and when to let the room find it, whether to explain a scene before playing it, and how much of the machinery to show the audience.
- **Engine.** Cued and organic applause are distinguishable in the captured track and in the edit. Explaining a scene raises comprehension and lowers surprise, a real trade between a laugh that lands and a laugh that lands bigger.
- **Takes.** Continuous through the taping

#### The Scene Pass

The scene played straight through for the audience, usually twice, all four cameras rolling. On a tape show the director calls a live line cut; on a film-style show every camera simply runs and nothing is switched.

- **Who.** Director, Stage Manager, Technical Director or four operators, whole company
- **Decides.** How many passes, whether to stop mid-scene or run through a mistake, whether to reset for a better take, and when a scene is good enough to move on from.
- **Engine.** Per take: performance quality per actor, audience response per joke, technical validity per camera. Take two of a joke returns a fraction of take one on a familiarity decay curve. That curve is the central tension of the night.
- **Takes.** Fifteen to forty minutes per scene including resets

#### Alts from the Floor

After a joke dies the showrunner walks to the actor and gives a new line invented ninety seconds ago, to be played immediately to the same audience. Nothing else in television works this way.

- **Who.** Showrunner and writers standing at the edge of the set
- **Decides.** Throw an alt or move on, which pitch to use, how many alts to burn on one line, and whether this actor can carry a cold line right now in front of a cooling room.
- **Engine.** An alt is a fresh joke with an unrehearsed performance penalty and full audience novelty. The audience's memory of the original setup persists, so a third or fourth alt degrades the whole beat and costs minutes of room temperature.
- **Takes.** Ninety seconds to pitch, two to six minutes to play

#### Reset Management

Between takes the set is put back: food replaced, drinks refilled, furniture marked in, costumes changed, faces touched. The audience watches every second of it and cools while it happens.

- **Who.** Property Master, Set Dressing, Wardrobe, Hair and Makeup, Stage Crew
- **Decides.** How much to reset and what to let go, whether to cheat continuity, whether to touch up at all, and whether to hold the house or release it for a break.
- **Engine.** Every reset consumes minutes and room temperature. Continuity state per take determines which takes the editor can later combine, so a fast sloppy reset buys laughs and costs the cut.
- **Takes.** Two to twelve minutes per take

#### Holding for the Laugh

The cast must wait out a laugh without dying in it, and come back in before it dies on its own. Too early and the line is buried, too late and the scene sags. This is the craft that separates multicam actors from everyone else.

- **Who.** Actors, Director
- **Decides.** How long to hold each laugh, whether to play through a small one, and whether to milk a physical moment for a second wave.
- **Engine.** Every laugh has amplitude and decay; the actor's re-entry point against that decay changes both the captured audio and the runtime. A bad hold is only recoverable by trimming the laugh, which reads as fake.
- **Takes.** Live, on every joke

#### Pickups and Wild Lines

After the house is released, the company shoots what it could not get in front of them: missed angles, a blown gag, inserts, and lines recorded wild and clean. There is no audience left, so there is no laugh.

- **Who.** Director, Script Supervisor, Production Sound Mixer
- **Decides.** What to fix and what to accept, whether to reshoot a whole scene cold, and what to record wild against a possible fix in post.
- **Engine.** Pickups carry the same no-live-laugh penalty as a pre-shoot, plus continuity and energy mismatch against the audience takes. Crew overtime runs and every pickup is charged to the episode's variable budget.
- **Takes.** Thirty minutes to two hours after the house clears

#### The Line Cut and the Quad Split

All four angles record isolated while a live switched cut is made alongside them, for the audience monitors, for the producers, and as the editor's reference to what the director intended.

- **Who.** Technical Director with Associate Director, or the video playback operator
- **Decides.** Whether to cut live at all, who calls it, how tight to punch, and whether the audience watches the cut or watches the stage.
- **Engine.** The line cut stored as an edit decision list over the coverage matrix, not as separate media. It biases but does not bind the editor, and because the audience watched it, it shaped the laughs that were recorded.
- **Takes.** Live, throughout the taping

#### Reload and Offload

Four cameras run out at the same time. On film, four thousand-foot magazines give roughly eleven minutes each; on digital, four cards must be offloaded and verified. Everything stops for all four at once.

- **Who.** First Assistant Cameras, Loader, DIT or Tape Operator
- **Decides.** Reload between scenes rather than mid-scene, whether to run short ends, how many magazines or cards to have ready, and whether to hold the audience through a reload or break them.
- **Engine.** Per-camera media clock with a hard stop and a reload duration. A reload forced mid-scene destroys a take and costs room temperature. Staggering media across four cameras is a genuine tactical choice with a real cost.
- **Takes.** Two to five minutes per reload, shared across four cameras

#### The Group Edit

Angles synced by timecode into grouped clips; the editor cuts by punching between four simultaneous views of one continuous performance, and mixes takes that were each played whole.

- **Who.** Editor and Assistant Editor
- **Decides.** Which angle on every line, which take under every laugh, where to cut away to bury a mistake, and how to assemble one scene out of two complete performances.
- **Engine.** The coverage matrix plus a take library with continuity, performance and technical-validity flags per angle per take. A moment with no usable angle simply cannot be cut, and the engine must permit and surface that failure.
- **Takes.** Two to four days to the editor's cut

#### Building the Laugh Track

The show's own audience tracks are trimmed, levelled, moved between takes, and where the room was thin, supplemented from library. This is sweetening, and the same tools will build a track that never existed.

- **Who.** Editor, Audience Reaction Editor, Sound Supervisor
- **Decides.** Which take's laugh marries which take's picture, how far to trim each laugh, where to lift and where to add, and where to leave a silence honest.
- **Engine.** Laugh as an editable audio object separable from picture, with an authenticity score that falls as laughs are lifted, stretched or synthesised. Aggregate fakeness becomes a visible property of the finished episode.
- **Takes.** One to two days, overlapping picture editorial

#### Cutting to Time

The show comes in long because the laughs made it long, and two to five minutes must leave the cut. Jokes that worked in front of an audience get cut because the laugh they earned costs seconds the network will not give.

- **Who.** Showrunner and Editor
- **Decides.** Which jokes to cut, which laughs to trim, whether to lose a whole scene, and whether to buy seconds by shortening the main title or the tag.
- **Engine.** Runtime equals dialogue plus action plus retained laugh duration, against a hard slot length. Every trim has a comedic cost, trimming a laugh short lowers perceived response, and cutting a setup breaks every payoff downstream.
- **Takes.** One to two days

#### Producer's Cut, Network Cut, Lock

The cut goes to the studio, then the network, each with notes, each having already given notes on the outline, the draft and two run-throughs. The show locks a week or two after it was taped.

- **Who.** Showrunner, studio and network post executives
- **Decides.** Which late notes to take, whether to defend a joke Standards flagged, and whether to spend a reshoot rather than lose a scene.
- **Engine.** The same two note-giving agents with memory, now scoring compliance against notes given three weeks earlier. Late notes can force pickups or reshoots at real cost, because the sets still stand and the cast is still employed.
- **Takes.** Three to seven days from editor's cut to lock

#### Mix and Delivery

Dialogue, audience, a few effects and a handful of music cues mixed in about a day against the network's loudness spec. There is almost no score and almost no ADR, because ADR desynchronises the line from its laugh.

- **Who.** Re-recording Mixer, Sound Supervisor
- **Decides.** How loud the audience sits under the dialogue, whether a thin laugh gets help, whether to ADR a line at the cost of its laugh, and where the stingers land.
- **Engine.** The audience as its own stem with a player-set level that directly drives how funny the home viewer perceives the show to be, independent of the jokes. ADR replaces a line and voids the live laugh recorded on top of it.
- **Takes.** One day to mix, one day to deliver

#### Main Title and Bumpers

A multicam episode needs a main title, an end title, and three or four scene-transition stingers of two to five seconds. Total original music per episode is often under two minutes.

- **Who.** Composer, Music Editor
- **Decides.** Stinger character and length, whether a transition needs music at all, and whether to license a song for a rare montage.
- **Engine.** Music as a small fixed cost and a small fixed runtime contribution, unlike every other format. A stinger's length is runtime the show must pay for, so music competes directly with jokes for seconds.
- **Takes.** One to two days, mostly reuse of existing cues

#### The Reshoot Decision

A scene that died can be shot again next week, in front of the next audience, on sets that still stand with a cast still on payroll. No other format can re-run its own failure this cheaply.

- **Who.** Showrunner and Line Producer
- **Decides.** Whether to reshoot, in front of an audience or cold, whether to rewrite first, and whether the episode is worth the hours it will take out of next week.
- **Engine.** Reshoot cost drawn against the episode's variable budget and next week's stage time. A reshot scene inherits the new night's room temperature, and matching it to the original night's laughs becomes an edit problem the engine must surface.
- **Takes.** Two to four hours inserted into the following week

#### Deficit and the Syndication Threshold

The studio spends more per episode than the network's licence fee and is betting on the library. The number that matters is eighty-eight to a hundred episodes, enough to strip five days a week.

- **Who.** Studio business affairs with the Line Producer
- **Decides.** Whether to absorb a cast raise, whether to cut the pattern budget mid-season, and whether to trade cash now for episode count later.
- **Engine.** Cumulative deficit against episode count with a step change in asset value at the strip threshold. Cast leverage rises with ratings, so one renegotiation can move cast from a third of the budget to more than half.
- **Takes.** Measured every episode, resolved across years

---

## 7. Live and broadcast television

*news and rolling news, sport and outside broadcast, live events and ceremonies, awards shows, entertainment strips, and live drama. Anything cut in real time and transmitted as it is made: no post, no second take, and a clock that does not stop.*

### The unit of work

The unit of work is the SECOND OF TRANSMISSION; the unit of progress is the RUNDOWN ITEM CLEARED. A live-action day burns pages. A live day burns clock — you cannot get more of it by working harder and you cannot get any of it back.

The atomic object is the ITEM: a news story, a sport segment between breaks, an award, a performance, a live-drama act. Each carries a slug, a planned duration, an actual duration, a format (VO, VO/SOT, package, live two-way, OOV, donut, presentation, VT, live), the sources it needs, and a status that runs not written → written → approved → ready → cued → on air → aired → dropped. The health of the show at any instant is one signed number: seconds HEAVY or seconds LIGHT against the hard out, recomputed every frame.

A GOOD DAY: out of titles on the second; no black; no wrong source; no hot mic; no misspelled name on air; every VT played to its out-cue; every break hit; the presenter never asked to stretch more than about twenty seconds; the hard out hit within a second or two of the junction. In sport add: nothing missed because you were in a replay, and a replay on air inside roughly ten seconds of anything that mattered. In news add: you were first and you were right, and nothing said on air had to be corrected afterwards.

Secondary counters crews actually keep: cuts per minute (2-20 in live sport, 1-4 in live drama); replays per period; time-to-air on a breaking story (a staffed news operation targets under 60 seconds from a standing start); drift in seconds per item; faults per transmission, where the target is literally zero; and for a touring OB, venues turned around per week.

Note the scale inversion: a drama unit might deliver 3-5 minutes of finished screen time a day at very high cost per minute. A gallery delivers 22 minutes in 22 minutes, an Olympic host operation delivers 11,000+ hours in seventeen days, and a rolling news channel delivers 8,760 hours a year. Live television is the only form where finished output equals elapsed time exactly, by definition.

### What is absent

- PICTURE EDITORIAL. There is no offline, no online, no assembly, no fine cut, no picture lock. The cut is made on a switcher at the instant of acquisition and is irrevocable the moment it is taken. The editor's job is done in real time by the vision mixer and the director, and there is no version two.
- THE SECOND TAKE. Not 'expensive' — nonexistent. The entire scheduling, insurance and contingency logic of film production assumes you can go again. Nothing here does.
- COVERAGE. You never shoot a moment from several angles for later selection. All angles exist simultaneously and you select one NOW, live, while the others go unseen (or to iso record, which is a replay resource, not a cutting resource).
- DAILIES / RUSHES. Nobody watches yesterday's material to decide anything. The show that aired is the only version of itself, and the review is a fault log, not a viewing.
- THE DI AND THE GRADE. No colourist, no grading suite, no LUT pipeline in finishing. Matching is done live on CCU panels during rehearsal and ridden continuously through transmission.
- POST SOUND ENTIRELY. No sound editorial, no ADR, no Foley, no dialogue premix, no dub stage, no re-recording mixer, no final mix. The live mix IS the deliverable, made once, by one person, in a moving truck.
- THE VFX PIPELINE. No plates, no bids, no turnover, no vendor management, no versioning, no final approval. Graphics and virtual elements render at frame rate live or they do not exist.
- SCRIPT SUPERVISION / CONTINUITY MATCHING. There is nothing to match because there is only one pass. (The job title 'Production Assistant' in US sport/news means something completely different: timings.)
- THE STRIPBOARD. No shooting schedule, no day-out-of-days, no cross-boarding, no scheduling out of continuity. Order is broadcast order, always, without exception.
- THE SLATE AND SYNC. No clapper, no sync point, no sound roll. Everything is genlocked (black-and-burst/tri-level) or PTP-locked (SMPTE ST 2059) to one reference from power-up; sync is an engineering state, not a post task.
- PICKUPS, RESHOOTS, ADDITIONAL PHOTOGRAPHY. None of these concepts apply.
- TEST SCREENINGS AND RECUTS. No preview audience, no alternate ending, no director's cut, no recut after bad numbers. Your feedback arrives while you are still on air, via ratings minute-by-minute and social media.
- DELIVERY AND MASTERING as a finishing stage. The deliverable left the building as it was made. Post-transmission versioning (highlights, catch-up compliance edits, subtitle conform) makes COPIES of a finished thing.
- THE COMPLETION BOND. There is nothing to complete. The insurance product is event cancellation, abandonment and non-appearance cover instead.
- CASTING, for most of it. News and sport talent is on annual staff or talent contract, negotiated by a talent agent and an HR department, not cast per programme. In sport you cannot recast the event; whoever turns up is the cast.
- DEVELOPMENT AND GREENLIGHT, for news and sport. There is no script to option, no package to assemble, no financier to persuade. The commissioning decision is a multi-year rights contract or a permanent editorial remit.
- LOCATION SCOUTING as an aesthetic act. Venues are dictated by the rights contract or the event. The recce is engineering — power, cable runs, sightlines, fibre demarc, RF spectrum — not a search for a look.
- THE UNIT MOVE inside a shooting day. You do not move. The truck parks once and stays until de-rig.

### What is reordered

- EDITING MOVES TO ZERO. In film, edit follows shoot by months. Here the cut happens in the same frame as the capture, by a different person (vision mixer) taking a verbal order from a third (director), with the consequence irreversible before anyone can evaluate it.
- GRADING MOVES BEFORE AND DURING CAPTURE. Colour and exposure matching happens on CCU panels at rig and is ridden live by the racks/vision engineer as the sun moves, floodlights come up and the pitch goes into half-shadow.
- THE MIX MOVES TO ZERO — and the mixer becomes a hazard-mitigation role. The A1 is not finishing a mix, they are preventing an open mic from becoming a regulatory incident while riding crowd against commentary against effects.
- THE SCRIPT IS WRITTEN DURING THE SHOOT. A news script is being rewritten while the programme is on air; the writer is typing into a row that plays in four minutes. In film the script precedes everything; here it trails the event.
- REHEARSAL BECOMES THE POST STAGE. Every creative decision that scripted production makes in the edit is made here at camera rehearsal and dress, days early, and then executed blind. Rehearsal is where risk is bought down and it is finite and contested.
- LEGAL AND COMPLIANCE MOVE INTO THE TRANSMISSION ITSELF. There is no clearance pass. A lawyer or S&P executive rules in two to five seconds, with a five-to-ten-second buffer as the only safety margin.
- VFX/GRAPHICS DELIVERY DEADLINE IS REHEARSAL, NOT PICTURE LOCK. Templates must be built, bound to live data and proven before dress; after that nothing can be fixed except by typing into a field under pressure.
- PROMOTION RUNS BEFORE AND INSIDE THE PROGRAMME. Trails, idents, continuity announcements and junction promos are scheduled around a programme that has not happened yet, by people who will have to react if it over-runs.
- THE BUDGET IS COMMITTED BEFORE THE CONTENT EXISTS. You book the truck, the crew, the EVS channels and the satellite window months before you know what the show will be. In film the budget follows the script; here it precedes the event.
- 'POST' EXISTS, BUT AFTER AND DOWNSTREAM. Highlights cuts, clips, social edits, catch-up compliance edits, subtitle conform, archive — all derivative of a finished broadcast, produced by a separate team on a separate clock.
- MARKETING AUDIENCE DATA ARRIVES DURING THE PRODUCT. Minute-by-minute overnights mean you learn whether a segment worked while later segments are still unmade, and can change them.
- THE DEBRIEF IS THE ONLY FEEDBACK LOOP, AND IT FEEDS THE NEXT PRODUCTION, NOT THIS ONE. Where film iterates inside a project, live television iterates across projects — the fault log is the campaign-level memory.

### How the crew differs

- PRESENT HERE, ABSENT IN LIVE ACTION — Vision Mixer (UK) / Technical Director (US): operates the production switcher and physically executes the cut. This is the editor's job, performed in real time on someone else's verbal order.
- Engineer in Charge (EIC): owns the technical facility for the day — reference, routing, failover, fault calls. There is no film equivalent; the nearest is a DIT crossed with a post supervisor crossed with a fire marshal.
- Vision Engineer / Racks (UK), Video Operator / Shader (US): rides iris and paint on CCU panels so N cameras match. The colourist, working live, forever, with no undo.
- EVS / Replay Operator (LSM operator) and Senior Replay Producer: build and play slow-motion replays within seconds of an event. Wholly unique to live; nothing in scripted production resembles it.
- Broadcast Associate (US) / Production Assistant (UK): does the timings — forward times, back-times, counts into and out of every break and VT, and calls shot numbers. Nothing to do with the film-world PA.
- Prompter / Autocue Operator: scrolls the script at the presenter's pace, live, and rescues them when the script and the rundown disagree.
- Sound Supervisor (A1) and A2: A1 mixes the entire programme live in the truck; A2 handles floor audio, RF and talent earpieces. The A1 is the production mixer and the re-recording mixer simultaneously.
- Comms Engineer: designs and maintains the intercom matrix. In a live production, comms IS the command structure; if it fails, the organisation ceases to exist.
- Graphics Operator (Vizrt / Chyron / Ross XPression), Font Op, Score Op, Data Operator: build and fire graphics live and type names into supers while the show runs. Graphics are the live equivalent of both titles and VFX.
- Transmission Controller, Master Control Operator, Network Operations: run the channel around the programme, and are the last people who can stop something reaching air.
- Continuity Announcer: speaks live over junctions and idents. Has no film-world counterpart at all.
- Commentator, Co-commentator / Analyst, Commentary Producer (Guarantor), Touchline / Sideline Reporter: the voices of the programme, none of whom are actors and none of whom are scripted.
- Uplink / Satellite Truck Engineer, Links Engineer, Bonded-Cellular Operator: get the signal off the site. Distribution as a live production role.
- Delay Operator and Standards and Practices / Compliance Producer in the gallery: hold the dump button and rule on legality in seconds.
- Live Captioner (Stenocaptioner / Respeaker) and Audio Describer: produce access services in real time as the show happens.
- Rigger-Driver: drives the truck, rigs the venue, de-rigs it, drives to the next one. One person, three jobs, legally bounded hours.
- Spidercam / Skycam Pilot, Rail-cam Operator, RF Camera Operator, Robotic Camera Operator running several heads from one seat, Ultra-motion Operator.
- VAR / Hawk-Eye Operator and Replay Official Liaison: broadcast staff serving the officiating process.
- Audience Wrangler and Warm-up Artist: manufacture and maintain the room's energy for hours.
- Host Broadcaster Liaison / Feeds Coordinator / Unilateral Producer: manages what other broadcasters are buying from you while you are still making it.
- Floor Manager (UK) / Stage Manager (US): the director's proxy on the floor, cueing talent by hand, holding the audience, and the only person who can physically stop the show. Overlaps a 1st AD but exists entirely during transmission, not before it.
- ABSENT HERE, CENTRAL IN LIVE ACTION — Editor and Assistant Editor. No cut exists to make.
- Post Production Supervisor, Post Coordinator, Conform/Online Editor, Colourist, DI Producer. The entire post organisation is missing.
- Supervising Sound Editor, Sound Designer, Dialogue Editor, Foley Artist, ADR Supervisor, Re-recording Mixer. There is no dub stage.
- VFX Supervisor, VFX Producer, Compositor, Matchmove, Rotoscope artist. Replaced by a graphics operator who must be finished before the show begins.
- Script Supervisor (continuity). Nothing to match; there is one pass.
- 1st, 2nd and 3rd Assistant Director. The scheduling and floor authority is split between Director, Floor Manager and the timing PA.
- Gaffer and Best Boy Electric in the film sense. Replaced by a Lighting Director working to a plot with a Lighting Console Operator riding cues live on a desk.
- Key Grip and the grip department. Replaced by pedestals, jibs, robotic heads, rail systems and cable-cams, all rigged once and operated, not repositioned shot to shot.
- DIT, Data Wrangler, Loader. The nearest analogue — the Ingest/Media Manager — solves a different problem: what to record now, not what to back up later.
- Casting Director, Screenwriter (in news and sport), Storyboard Artist. The camera plan and camera script replace the storyboard.
- Location Manager as an aesthetic role, Production Designer on news and sport (the studio set is designed once and amortised over years), Stunt Coordinator, Armourer, Animal Wrangler.
- Completion Guarantor. Nothing to guarantee the completion of.

### What goes wrong

- DEAD AIR / GOING TO BLACK. The defining failure. Irrecoverable, immediately visible to every viewer and to the regulator, and there is no version of the programme in which it did not happen.
- CUTTING TO THE WRONG SOURCE. A mislabelled router destination, a wrong button, a stale macro — and twenty million people see the thing you were not supposed to show, or a camera operator scratching their nose.
- THE HOT MIC. A microphone left open over a channel it should not be on. Careers have ended in four seconds. Unique to live: in film the take is simply not used.
- THE DUMP FIRING LATE, OR NOT AT ALL. The delay buffer is five to ten seconds and a human has to recognise the problem and hit it. Firing it also spends the buffer, leaving you unprotected while it rebuilds.
- UNDER-RUN. The event finishes early, the guest cancels, the ceremony is shorter than anyone predicted — and now you must fill live, on air, with nothing prepared, in front of everyone.
- OVER-RUN AND CRASHING THE HARD OUT. The event runs long and you lose the ending, or you blow through the network junction and take out the schedule behind you.
- THE BREAK EATS THE MOMENT. You take the commercial break with ninety seconds left and the winning score happens inside it. Purely a live failure mode.
- THE HEIDI PROBLEM. Leaving or joining an event at the wrong instant — cutting away from a finish, or arriving after it.
- THE WRONG WINNER. An envelope error, a results graphic fired early, a scorebug that reveals an embargoed outcome before the announcement.
- LOSS OF THE TRANSMISSION PATH. A digger through the fibre, satellite rain fade, the uplink losing lock, or 80,000 phones killing your bonded cellular at exactly the moment the crowd reacts.
- LOSS OF REFERENCE. Genlock or PTP failure takes every unlocked source in the facility out at once — pictures roll, frame syncs error, and the whole plant glitches simultaneously.
- POWER. A generator drop, a lost phase, a breaker on the wrong circuit, UPS runtime exhausted before anyone finds the fault.
- COMMS FAILURE. The director cannot reach a camera operator, or the producer cannot reach the presenter. The organisation stops existing while the programme continues.
- MIX-MINUS ERROR. The presenter or reporter hears their own voice returned at path delay and stops talking mid-sentence, live, and cannot explain why.
- THE SATELLITE COLLISION. At 250 ms each way, or two seconds on bonded cellular, presenter and reporter talk over each other for the entire interview.
- PROMPTER FAILURE. A crash, a stuck scroll, or a duplicated paragraph, and the presenter reads the same sentence twice or simply stops.
- THE PROMPTER AND THE RUNDOWN DISAGREE. Someone edited a row after prompter load; the presenter reads a story that no longer exists while a different VT rolls.
- PLACEHOLDER GRAPHICS. A broken data binding puts TEAM NAME or LOREM IPSUM on air, full screen, for as long as it takes someone to notice.
- THE MISSPELLED NAME. Typed under time pressure into a super and transmitted to the entire country.
- THE VT THAT IS NOT THERE. The package is still ingesting when its cue arrives, or was published to the wrong server, or is a frame short and ends on black.
- AUTOMATION FIRING THE WRONG ROW. An APC system executing a stale macro after a late rundown edit — several devices doing the wrong thing at once, faster than a human can stop it.
- THE NRCS FALLING OVER MID-BULLETIN. The rundown disappears from every screen and the show has to be run off printed paper and shouting.
- RF FAILURE AND INTERMODULATION. A radio mic hit at the worst moment, a spectrum coordination error producing interference on a third channel, a stadium full of unlicensed transmitters.
- LOUDNESS AND CHANNEL-MAP ERRORS. The international sound pair goes to air instead of the domestic, or the programme sits ten LU above the standard and the regulator hears about it.
- WEATHER STOPPING THE EVENT. Rain delay, lightning protocol, wind outside the cable-cam limit, a cancelled parade — and hours of airtime to fill.
- AN INJURY OR DEATH ON AIR. Tommy Cooper collapsed and died during a live variety broadcast in 1984 while the audience laughed. There is no cut away from this that has been planned in advance.
- THE STREAKER, THE PITCH INVASION, THE PROTEST. A policy decision about where to point the camera, made in under two seconds, with a broadcaster policy and a moral argument on both sides.
- SOMEONE SHOUTING AN OBSCENITY BEHIND A LIVE REPORTER. Unstoppable, unpreventable, and the delay is the only defence.
- LEGAL CATASTROPHE IN FLIGHT. Contempt, defamation, naming a minor, broadcasting an unproven allegation, breaching reporting restrictions, breaking an election silence period or an exit-poll embargo.
- MUSIC RIGHTS BLEEDING INTO THE MIX. The arena plays an uncleared track, it goes into the international sound, and every broadcaster in every territory inherits the problem.
- CALLING A RACE WRONG ON ELECTION NIGHT. Broadcast, believed, and only retractable on air, slowly, in public.
- THE DEATH OF A HEAD OF STATE MID-TRANSMISSION. The obituary procedure voids the schedule, changes the music policy across the network, and abandons whatever you were making.
- EVACUATION WITH FORTY CAMERAS RIGGED. Bomb threat, fire alarm, structural failure — and a truck park you cannot reach.
- TALENT DRYING, CORPSING, OR NOT BEING THERE. In live drama an actor who forgets has no prompt, no reset and no second take. Fox's Rent: Live in 2019 aired largely from the dress-rehearsal recording because a lead broke his foot at dress — the live show's insurance policy WAS the rehearsal tape.
- PHYSICAL COLLISION ON THE FLOOR. A camera cable walked into a robotic track, a jib into a lighting bar, a Steadicam operator into a set piece that was supposed to have moved.
- TIME-ZONE AND SIMULCAST ERROR. The East feed spoils the West feed; a promo reveals a result to a region still watching it live.
- THE DATA FEED BEING WRONG BEFORE IT IS RIGHT. A provisional official result graphic goes to air, is corrected thirty seconds later, and the first version is already on social media with your logo on it.
- CREW FATIGUE FROM A DOUBLE TURNAROUND. De-rig overnight, drive, rig at dawn, transmit — and the error rate on the second show is measurably worse.
- ACCUMULATED REPUTATION DAMAGE. Unlike a film, every fault is witnessed live by the whole audience and by the regulator, and the record of it persists into your next rights negotiation.

### The shape of the money

Live television inverts the film budget. Development, script, post and VFX — three of the largest cost groups in scripted drama — do not exist as line items at all. What replaces them is RIGHTS, FACILITIES and PEOPLE-FOR-A-DAY, all committed before anyone knows what the programme will contain.

SPORT. The rights fee dwarfs the production. For a major league property the fee routinely runs ten to a hundred times the cost of covering it (estimate); the coverage is a rounding error on the contract, which is exactly why the contract usually mandates minimum production standards. Inside the production budget of a single match, a rough split (estimate): facilities 30-40% (truck and B-unit, cameras, box lenses, replay servers, RF, ultra-motion, cable-cam); crew 30-45% (day rates, travel days, hotels, per diems, overtime); connectivity and transmission 8-15%; venue access, power and rigging 5-10%; graphics and data licences 3-8%; logistics and catering 3-5%.

NEWS. The money is capital, not project. A studio, a gallery, a playout chain, a newsroom computer system and a graphics system are bought once and amortised over five to ten years; the marginal cost of tonight's bulletin is crew hours plus whatever field circuits the stories need. A news operation costs almost the same on a dead Tuesday as on the biggest day of the decade — which is why cost control here is headcount and bureau count, never per-programme savings.

THE SHAPE DIFFERENCES THAT ACTUALLY MATTER. (1) There is no above-the-line / below-the-line split: presenters, commentators and correspondents are on annual talent contracts, not engaged per production. (2) Contingency is bought as HARDWARE REDUNDANCY at booking time, not held as a percentage reserve — you pay for the second satellite path whether or not the fibre gets cut. (3) Overtime dominates budget variance because you do not control the event's length: extra time, a rain delay, a filibuster, a five-hour awards show. (4) Cost per finished hour is one to two orders of magnitude below scripted drama, but cost per hour of STANDING CAPACITY is high and fixed. (5) The insurance product is event cancellation, abandonment and non-appearance cover, not a completion bond. (6) A large share of spend is subcontracted to facilities houses at a markup rather than employed, so the marginal decision is always "book it or don't", never "we already own it".

ORDER-OF-MAGNITUDE FIGURES, ALL ESTIMATES. A large triple-expando OB truck with a B-unit: roughly US $8k-$30k/day, or UK £6k-£20k/day, crew on top. Ultra-motion, cable-cam and each additional replay operator price as separate line items. Ku-band occasional-use satellite historically ran hundreds to low thousands of dollars per hour and has now been largely displaced by fibre contribution circuits and bonded cellular, which has collapsed the cost of getting a reporter live from a field. Camera counts scale the same way costs do: a routine league match 12-30 cameras, a European final 35-50, a Super Bowl well over a hundred in recent years (estimate). Verified at the top of the scale: OBS produced over 11,000 hours of coverage at Paris 2024 including about 3,500 hours of live sport, with more than 110 cameras plus three helicopters and eight drones on the opening ceremony alone; the London 2012 International Broadcast Centre housed nearly 5,900 OBS personnel. A Summer Games host-broadcast operation therefore runs into the hundreds of millions of dollars (estimate) — and still spends none of it on post.

### Processes that exist only here

#### Rights acquisition and the broadcast window

Bid for the event years ahead: how many fixtures, which windows, exclusivity, territory, clip and near-live rights, highlights embargo hour, and the minimum production standards clause you are agreeing to meet.

- **Who.** Director of Sport / Head of Acquisitions
- **Decides.** Which properties to bid for and at what fee; exclusive live versus shared or highlights-only; contract length; whether to buy clip and archive rights separately; which windows you are obliged to fill.
- **Engine.** Rights as multi-year contract objects: fee schedule, window count, exclusivity flags, embargo timers, production-standard obligations (minimum camera count, minimum replay channels). Audience model prices each window; breaching a standards clause triggers a named penalty.
- **Takes.** 6-24 months of negotiation; contracts typically run 3-6 years

#### Host broadcaster versus unilateral decision

At an event with a host broadcaster (OBS at the Olympics, HBS at the World Cup, the EBU at Eurovision), decide how much of your own coverage to make on top of the world feed you are already paying for.

- **Who.** Executive Producer, Head of Production
- **Decides.** World feed only; world feed plus your own commentary; world feed plus unilateral cameras and a presentation platform; or a full parallel production with your own gallery. How many unilateral positions to book and where.
- **Engine.** The venue carries a booked-position inventory — commentary boxes, unilateral camera platforms, presentation sets, mixed-zone slots — sold on a published rate card and finite. The world feed is a fixed cut you cannot alter; every unilateral adds a switchable input and a cost.
- **Takes.** Booked 6-12 months ahead; positions allocated by the host long before

#### Technical recce / site survey

Walk the venue. Measure every cable run, find the truck park and the power, check sightlines from every proposed position, locate the fibre demarcation point, scan the RF spectrum, and work out where the sun will be at kick-off.

- **Who.** Engineering Manager, Technical Producer, Rigging Supervisor
- **Decides.** Truck park; cable routes and lengths; venue power versus generator; which camera positions are actually viable; where RF receive antennas go; what needs scaffolding or a tower; where the commentary positions sit.
- **Engine.** Venue as a geometric model: positions with sightline cones, run distances against cable limits (triax and SMPTE 311M hybrid fibre both have length ceilings), power drops rated in amps, RF obstruction, and sun azimuth by date and kick-off time.
- **Takes.** 1 day per venue, weeks to months ahead; repeated for every new venue

#### Camera plan

Draw the plan: how many cameras, numbered positions, lens on each, height, which are handheld, which are robotic, what sits on a jib, Steadicam, rail, buggy or cable-cam, and where the beauty shot lives.

- **Who.** Director, Technical Producer
- **Decides.** Camera count and position per venue; lens per position (a 100x+ box lens on the main, wide on the goal, long for reaction); which cameras are iso-recorded; which are wired and which are RF; which are unmanned.
- **Engine.** Each camera is an object with position, height, lens range, mount type, operator skill, cable type, latency and a coverage cone. The engine must be able to answer, at any instant, which cameras can see the thing that is happening.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days of design per venue; standard plans reused across a competition

#### Facilities booking and truck specification

Book the trucks and everything in them: switcher size, EVS channel count, audio desk, router, comms panels, RF links, specialty rigs, tenders, generators. Every line has a day rate, a travel day and a de-rig day.

- **Who.** Head of Outside Broadcast, Production Manager
- **Decides.** Truck tier (single-expando up to a triple-expando A-unit plus B-unit); switcher M/E count; EVS channels and operators; number of RF and ultra-motion cameras; whether to carry a backup truck at all.
- **Engine.** A facilities market with genuinely finite supply — there are only so many large trucks in a country and they clash on big weekends. Inventory carries switcher/EVS/desk/router capacity; scarcity drives price and forces downgrades.
- **Takes.** Booked 2-12 months ahead; major events lock trucks a year out

#### Rig and cable pull

Park, power up, run cable to every position, build camera platforms, rig RF antennas, dress the commentary boxes, run comms, pull the effects mic lines, and prove every path back to the truck before anyone shades a camera.

- **Who.** Riggers, Rigger-Drivers, Engineer in Charge
- **Decides.** Rig order and crew split; which runs go first; overnight rig versus same-day; how many rig days to buy; whether to pre-rig the venue permanently for a season.
- **Engine.** A work queue over positions with durations, hard dependencies (power before cameras, cameras before shading, shading before rehearsal) and a labour pool with hours and fatigue. Overrunning the rig eats rehearsal, never transmission — the show still starts on time.
- **Takes.** 4 hours for a small OB; 1-3 days for a major match; 4-8 weeks for a Games venue or a stadium show

#### Reference, timing and signal-path build

Lock every device to one reference — black-and-burst or tri-level sync in an SDI plant, PTP under SMPTE ST 2059 in an ST 2110 IP plant. Build the router crosspoints, lay out the multiviewers, label every source correctly.

- **Who.** Engineer in Charge, Broadcast Engineer
- **Decides.** Reference topology and grandmaster clock; router source and destination map; multiviewer layouts for each gallery seat; where to insert frame syncs for out-of-phase remote sources; salvo presets for fallback.
- **Engine.** A global frame clock (25 / 29.97 / 50 / 59.94) with every source carrying a phase offset in frames. Loss of reference rolls or glitches every unlocked source at once. The router is a crosspoint matrix with salvos; a mislabelled destination is how the wrong source reaches air.
- **Takes.** 2-6 hours on rig day; permanent in a studio plant but revalidated after every change

#### Comms and talkback design

Design the intercom: matrix panels in the gallery, wired and wireless belt packs on the floor, party-line rings, four-wire circuits to remote sites, and switched talkback or IFB into talent earpieces. Decide who hears whom.

- **Who.** Comms Engineer, Sound Supervisor
- **Decides.** The talkback map — which panel key reaches which group, who is on open mic to the director, who hears programme versus interrupt, who is allowed to interrupt talent, who can hear the producer's ear line.
- **Engine.** Comms is a directed graph of ports with talk/listen permissions, groups and interrupt priority. Every instruction in the game travels this graph. If the edge does not exist or the pack has failed, the order simply does not arrive and the shot does not happen.
- **Takes.** 1-2 hours to configure; designed once per show format, rebuilt at every venue

#### IFB and talent earpiece

Fit and prove the presenter's earpiece: programme audio with the producer and director able to cut in over the top. Set mix-minus so the presenter never hears their own voice returned down a delayed path.

- **Who.** A2 (floor audio), Sound Supervisor
- **Decides.** Who is on that ear (director only, producer only, both); whether the presenter gets the count; whether they hear the remote guest clean or the full programme; how loud, and what they hear during a VT.
- **Engine.** A per-talent audio bus with mix-minus routing that is latency-aware. Get mix-minus wrong and the talent hears themselves at path delay and stops mid-sentence — a real modelled failure with a visible on-air consequence, not a cosmetic warning.
- **Takes.** 10 minutes per person, re-checked at every rehearsal and again immediately before air

#### Transmission path and backup

Get the signal home. Primary is normally a fibre contribution circuit; backups are Ku-band satellite from a DSNG truck, bonded cellular (LiveU, TVU), or a second diverse fibre. Order the circuits, prove them, keep them hot.

- **Who.** Transmission Manager, Uplink Engineer, Links Engineer
- **Decides.** Primary and backup media; codec and bitrate (JPEG XS or ST 2110 for sub-frame latency, JPEG 2000 or long-GOP for economy); whether backup runs hot or cold; who is authorised to call the switch.
- **Engine.** Each path carries bandwidth, latency and a live hazard. Geostationary satellite adds roughly 240-280 ms round trip; bonded cellular 0.5-3 s; JPEG XS sub-frame. Hazard is driven by rain fade, digging, and RF congestion in a stadium full of phones. Failover has a switch time.
- **Takes.** Circuits ordered days to weeks ahead; path proving 2-4 hours before air; monitored throughout

#### Line-up and path proving

Send bars and tone, then a line-up caption naming the programme and the start time. Master control confirms levels, colour legality, audio phase, channel mapping and loudness, and proves the reverse vision path back to the truck.

- **Who.** Master Control Operator, Network Operations, Engineer in Charge
- **Decides.** Loudness target and standard (EBU R128 at -23 LUFS in Europe, ATSC A/85 at -24 LKFS in the US under the CALM Act); aspect and format flags; audio channel map — stereo pair, 5.1, international sound on separate pairs.
- **Engine.** Signals carry measurable metadata: integrated loudness, true peak, colour legality, channel assignment. Master control can and does reject a feed. Getting this wrong is an audible or visible on-air defect — wrong language pair, out-of-phase audio, illegal blacks.
- **Takes.** 30-60 minutes before transmission; re-checked hourly on long events

#### Camera shading and matching

On CCU panels, set iris, black level, gamma, knee, detail, matrix and white balance so twenty cameras look like one camera, then ride the iris live as cloud crosses, floodlights come up and half the pitch falls into shadow.

- **Who.** Vision Engineer / Racks (UK), Video Operator (US)
- **Decides.** The base look; per-camera match against the reference camera; how aggressively to ride iris during transmission; when to switch scene files for day-to-night; how to handle mixed daylight and floodlight in the same frame.
- **Engine.** Each camera holds an exposure and colour state plus a difference metric against the reference. Cuts between mismatched cameras are visible and cost quality score. Venue light changes continuously across the event, so the state drifts if nobody rides it.
- **Takes.** 45-90 minutes at rig; continuous through transmission

#### Audio rig and microphone plot

Plot every microphone: effects mics round the pitch, stump and net mics, crowd mics in the stands, presenter lavs and sticks, commentary lip mics, radio mics on the floor. Coordinate the frequencies. Build the desk layers.

- **Who.** Sound Supervisor (A1), A2
- **Decides.** Mic type and placement per source; the RF frequency plan; desk layout and layer assignment; what belongs in the international sound; how much crowd; whether to gate the effects or ride them by hand.
- **Engine.** RF spectrum as a finite, contested resource with intermodulation — two badly chosen frequencies generate a third that lands on a fourth mic. Each mic has a pattern, a position and a signal-to-crowd ratio. The desk has finite faders and finite layers.
- **Takes.** 3-8 hours to rig a stadium; 30 minutes in a studio; frequency coordination days ahead at major events

#### International sound and the clean feed

Build two mixes simultaneously: the domestic mix with your commentary, and the international sound — effects and atmosphere with no voices — that every other broadcaster in the world takes and lays their own commentary over.

- **Who.** Sound Supervisor, Host Broadcaster Audio Supervisor
- **Decides.** What is in international sound; stereo, 5.1 or immersive bed; whether stadium PA announcements and arena music are carried (a rights problem for everyone downstream); what sits on each outgoing pair.
- **Engine.** Parallel bus structure where a source can exist in one mix and not the other. A rights-encumbered element carried into the international sound creates a clearance failure for every taker in every territory — one mistake, dozens of downstream consequences.
- **Takes.** Designed once per event format; mixed live throughout

#### Graphics build and data binding

Build the scorebug, name supers (astons or lower thirds), result cards, tables, clocks, transitions and stings. Bind them to the live data feed so they populate themselves, and build a manual fallback for when the feed dies.

- **Who.** Graphics Producer, Broadcast Designer, Graphics Operator (Vizrt, Chyron, Ross XPression)
- **Decides.** Template set and brand look; what is automatic and what is typed by hand; the correct spelling and title of every named person; which stats to carry; how long each graphic holds before clearing itself.
- **Engine.** Templates as parameterised objects bound to a live data stream. A broken binding puts placeholder text on air in front of millions. Hand-typed names carry a spelling-error probability that rises with time pressure and with the obscurity of the name.
- **Takes.** Brand design over weeks; per-event prep 2-6 hours; supers typed continuously during transmission

#### Live data and timing feed integration

Take the official timing and scoring feed and the third-party stats feed, validate them, and drive the clock, the scorebug and replay metadata from them rather than from a person watching and typing.

- **Who.** Data Operator, Stats Producer, Timing and Scoring Liaison
- **Decides.** Which feed is authoritative; what latency is acceptable; when to override the feed by hand; what to do when the official clock and the stadium clock disagree; whether to trust a provisional result.
- **Engine.** A data stream with its own latency and error rate, independent of video latency. Graphics can therefore fire early or late against picture. The engine must be able to deliver a wrong provisional result followed by a correction, and make you live with both.
- **Takes.** Integrated and proven at rehearsal; monitored throughout

#### Rundown / running order build

Build the rundown in the newsroom system — ENPS, Avid iNEWS, Octopus, Ross Inception — with every item carrying a slug, page number, format, source, duration and back-time, in the exact order it will be transmitted.

- **Who.** Producer, Line Producer, Broadcast Associate (US) / Production Assistant (UK)
- **Decides.** Story order and the lead; item durations; format per item (VO, VO/SOT, package, live two-way, OOV, donut); which items are floaters and which are protected; where the breaks fall; what the kicker is.
- **Engine.** The rundown is the game's central document: an ordered list of items with durations, required sources and a status machine — and it is edited BY SEVERAL PEOPLE AT ONCE WHILE IT IS PLAYING. Concurrent edit, and its failure modes, is the mechanic.
- **Takes.** 3-6 hours to build a bulletin; rewritten continuously up to and straight through transmission

#### Back-timing and the hard out

Compute forward times from the top and back-times from the fixed end. Know at every instant whether the show is heavy or light and by how many seconds, and tell the director before the director asks.

- **Who.** Broadcast Associate (US) / Production Assistant (UK), Line Producer
- **Decides.** Which item absorbs the over-run; what gets dropped and in what order; whether to stretch the chat; whether to take the break early; whether to float the kicker or kill it outright.
- **Engine.** A live running total against an immovable hard out. Every item's actual duration diverges from planned because talent speak at their own rate and guests ramble. The engine must expose a signed drift in seconds, updated every frame, propagated forward through the rundown.
- **Takes.** Continuous, all show, reported in seconds on every item

#### Scripting and prompter

Write the presenter's words to time (roughly three words per second read aloud), write in-words and out-words for every clip, and push it to prompter over MOS so the prompter follows the rundown automatically.

- **Who.** Writer, Script Editor, Prompter / Autocue Operator
- **Decides.** Wording and read rate; which camera each read is on; the out-cue of every package; the ad-lib fallback if a VT fails; where the presenter looks and when they turn.
- **Engine.** Script text has a duration derived from word count and that presenter's personal read rate. Editing the script changes item duration and therefore the back-time. Prompter and rundown can desynchronise if a row is edited after prompter load — a classic, modellable disaster.
- **Takes.** 10-25 minutes of writing per minute of finished script; rewritten in breaks and live on air

#### Camera scripting for scripted live

On live drama, awards and entertainment, number every shot against the script: shot number, camera, framing, movement and the cue that triggers it. The PA then calls shot numbers on talkback so the mixer and the operators stay ahead.

- **Who.** Director, Production Assistant
- **Decides.** Shot list and numbering; which camera takes which line; every move cue; when the script is locked so the numbers stop changing; what the recovery shot is for each sequence.
- **Engine.** A shot table keyed to positions in the script or rundown. Each shot demands a specific camera in a specific framing at a specific time; a camera cannot be in two shots at once or reposition faster than physics allows. Late script changes invalidate every downstream number.
- **Takes.** Days of prep on a live drama; hours on an awards show; locked at the final dress

#### The rehearsal ladder

Work the ladder: read-through, stagger-through, technical rehearsal, camera rehearsal, dress rehearsal, and for major events a full dress recorded as insurance and as a jury or edit reference.

- **Who.** Director, Floor Manager / Stage Manager, Producer
- **Decides.** How many passes to buy; what to rehearse properly and what to talk through; whether to record the dress as an insurance copy; what to fix and what to accept and live with.
- **Engine.** Rehearsal is the only instrument for buying down error probability. Each pass reduces the failure hazard of the items it covers and raises crew familiarity; unrehearsed items keep their full hazard. Rehearsal time is finite and competes directly with rigging.
- **Takes.** News: a 10-minute top-of-show run. Awards: 3-5 days on site. Eurovision: each delegation gets two closed rehearsals (30 min then 20 min) plus three dress rehearsals per show, across roughly two weeks on site, with venue build starting about six weeks out

#### Floor management and cueing

Be the director's body on the floor: count talent in and out by hand, hold the audience, reset furniture in breaks, relay hair and wardrobe notes, and be the only person in the building who can physically stop the show.

- **Who.** Floor Manager (UK) / Stage Manager (US)
- **Decides.** When to cue; how to signal wind-up or stretch; whether to interrupt the presenter; whether to walk into shot to fix something; whether to hold a start.
- **Engine.** Cueing is a physical act with a position and a sightline — the talent must be able to see the FM. Wind-up and stretch signals modulate the talent's read rate, which changes item duration, which changes the back-time. The chain must be continuous, not cosmetic.
- **Takes.** The entire transmission plus every break

#### VT and package build

Cut the pre-recorded inserts — news packages, sport features, nominee montages, title sequences — and deliver them to the playout server with frame-accurate in and out points, a known audio layout and a written out-cue.

- **Who.** VT Editor / Craft Editor, Server Operator
- **Decides.** Content and duration to the frame; the last words on the track; where it lives on the server; whether the reporter tracks it live in the booth or pre-records; whether it is finished enough to play.
- **Engine.** Packages are media objects with a duration and an ingest state: rendering → transferring → on server → verified. A package still ingesting when its cue arrives is a genuine on-air failure with a probability attached, not a dialog box you dismiss.
- **Takes.** 45 minutes to 3 hours per news package; weeks for an awards title sequence; frequently finished minutes before air

#### Ingest and record layout

Decide what is recorded and where: every camera iso, programme, clean feed, international sound, all onto server channels with a naming convention a person under pressure can still navigate.

- **Who.** Media Manager, EVS Operator, Ingest Operator
- **Decides.** How many record channels per server; which cameras get an iso and which do not; how much storage and how many days; what is protected from overwrite; what is nearlined for the highlights edit.
- **Engine.** Record channels and storage are finite and shared with playout — a channel used to record cannot play out. Any camera not iso-recorded is a replay that can never be built. This is the trade-off, and it must bite in the moment it matters.
- **Takes.** Configured at rig; runs continuously through the event

#### Replay build and the slow-motion cut

Mark the moment, trim the clip, choose the angle, set the speed, and have it cued before the director asks for it. Build multi-angle sequences for one incident and playlists for the highlights that air at the break.

- **Who.** EVS / Replay Operator (LSM), Senior Replay Producer
- **Decides.** Which angle tells the story; what speed; whether to run ultra-motion; how far back to start; when to offer it to the director; when to say not yet.
- **Engine.** Each operator holds a timeline of recorded in/out points with per-angle availability. Building a clip takes real seconds and the event does not pause. Being in a replay must cost live coverage — the engine has to be able to make you miss the next goal.
- **Takes.** Continuous. A usable replay is 4-15 seconds of work after the moment

#### Ultra-motion and specialty cameras

Operate the cameras that exist only in live coverage: 3x-8x ultra-motion, cable-suspended aerials flying over the field of play, RF handhelds on the touchline, rail cameras, buggies, pole cams, helicopters and drones.

- **Who.** Ultra-motion Operator, Steadicam Operator, Spidercam / Skycam Pilot, RF Camera Operator, Rail-cam Operator, Drone Pilot
- **Decides.** Where the specialty camera lives; when to use it; what it covers when nothing is happening; whether to risk a low pass; whether the wind is inside the cable-cam's limits.
- **Engine.** These cameras have different frame rates (a 6x camera's material exists only as slow motion), different latency, different reliability — RF dropout, drone battery, cable-cam wind limits — and separate cost, airspace and safety constraints that gate their use.
- **Takes.** Rigged over 4-24 hours (a cable-cam needs four corner winches and a flight test); operated throughout

#### Vision mixing

Execute the cut. Preset the next source on preview, take it on the director's word, arm and build keys and DVEs, set the next transition, and hold an M/E ready for the graphic that is coming in nine seconds.

- **Who.** Vision Mixer (UK) / Technical Director (US)
- **Decides.** Exactly when the cut lands relative to the spoken word 'take'; which M/E holds what; cut, mix or wipe; and how to rescue a call that is physically impossible on the panel as currently set.
- **Engine.** Model the panel: program and preview buses, N M/E stages, keyers, downstream keyers, aux buses, memories and macros. A source not preset cannot be taken instantly; a key not armed cannot be dissolved in. Reaction time plus panel state is the mechanic, not a dropdown.
- **Takes.** The whole transmission — a cut every 3-8 seconds in sport, far slower in drama

#### Directing the live cut

Watch forty monitors at once, keep the story in the right frame, call shots ahead of the action, brief operators between plays, call replays in and out, and hand back to the presenter without a gap.

- **Who.** Director
- **Decides.** Every cut; every camera's next framing; replay now or wait; go wide or stay on the face; when to take the break; what to do with the picture you are not allowed to show.
- **Engine.** The director's view is a multiviewer: many simultaneous live sources of which exactly one is on air. Decisions are made on incomplete information against a hard clock. The engine must simulate what the director CANNOT see as carefully as what they can.
- **Takes.** The whole transmission, on top of days of preparation and rehearsal

#### Producing in the ear

Own editorial in flight: what the show is about right now, what the presenter asks next, what to drop, when to stay with a picture, when to leave it, and what the legal position is on the thing that just happened.

- **Who.** Producer / Executive Producer
- **Decides.** Stay or leave; drop or keep; ask this next; get out of this; do not show that; come to me; run the obit; apologise now or at the end.
- **Engine.** Editorial state: what the audience has already been told, what is confirmed, what is only sourced, what is legally unsafe. Instructions arrive in the presenter's ear mid-sentence over IFB and must be executable by the talent model, imperfectly.
- **Takes.** The whole transmission

#### Live sound mixing

Ride the mix live: commentary over effects, crowd under, stings, VT audio, remote guests, PA spill — and hold it inside the loudness standard while a stadium goes from silence to 105 dB in one second.

- **Who.** Sound Supervisor (A1)
- **Decides.** Fader by fader balance; when mics open and close; how much crowd; when to duck effects under commentary; when to kill a mic that is about to become a problem.
- **Engine.** Continuous audio state: per-source level, crowd level driven by event drama, loudness integrated across the whole programme, and open-mic risk as a live hazard. An open mic that should be closed must be able to end someone's career.
- **Takes.** A 1-3 hour soundcheck, then the whole transmission

#### Commentary and the commentary position

Run the box: lip mics, headsets carrying programme and talkback, a monitor showing programme at its own latency, a stats runner, and a producer counting them into and out of every break.

- **Who.** Commentator, Co-commentator / Analyst, Commentary Producer (Guarantor)
- **Decides.** Who commentates; what they are briefed on; when they hand to the touchline reporter; when they shut up and let the pictures run; what they must not say about the incident everyone just saw.
- **Engine.** Commentary is an audio source with a latency relationship to picture — the commentator watches a monitor that may be behind the live action they can see with their own eyes. Their words become the programme's editorial and legal liability in real time.
- **Takes.** 2 hours of prep and briefing; live throughout

#### The delay and the dump

Run the profanity and incident delay: a buffer usually of five to ten seconds on television (radio's classic is seven; some systems hold thirty), with a dump button that discards the buffer and substitutes clean audio or video.

- **Who.** Delay Operator, Standards and Practices (US) / Compliance Producer (UK)
- **Decides.** How long the delay is; what triggers a dump; audio only or audio and video; and whether to accept the risk of dumping the wrong thing and losing content that was fine.
- **Engine.** A real ring buffer of N seconds. Firing the dump SPENDS the buffer; the delay then rebuilds by imperceptibly stretching pauses, and until it has, you are effectively live and unprotected. Operator reaction time against buffer length is the entire mechanic.
- **Takes.** Armed before air, live all show; each dump costs several seconds of protection

#### Compliance and legal in flight

Sit in or beside the gallery and rule in real time: can we name them, can we show the body, is that contempt, is that person a minor, is that allegation unproven, is that music cleared, are we in an election silence period.

- **Who.** Programme Lawyer, Compliance Producer, Standards and Practices Executive
- **Decides.** Show or do not show; name or do not name; apologise now or at the end; pull the picture; put up an advisory; cut back to the studio and move on.
- **Engine.** A rules model with jurisdiction and clock: the Ofcom watershed at 21:00 in the UK, FCC indecency restrictions between 06:00 and 22:00 in the US, reporting restrictions, embargoes, election silence. Consequences persist — fines, findings, licence conditions across a campaign.
- **Takes.** Real time. A ruling is wanted inside 2-5 seconds

#### Break and junction management

Hit the commercial breaks. Inventory is sold against break times; the show must reach each break within seconds and come back exactly when it ends, whatever the event happens to be doing at that moment.

- **Who.** Line Producer, Broadcast Associate, Master Control Operator, Traffic
- **Decides.** When to take the break; early or late; take a floating break or hold; drop a break entirely and eat the make-good; go to break with the score at 2-2 and ninety seconds left.
- **Engine.** Breaks are contracts with a scheduled time, a duration and attached revenue. Missing one costs money in make-goods; taking one at the wrong instant costs audience and reputation. Sport must generate natural break triggers — timeouts, innings, drinks, half-time.
- **Takes.** 2-6 minutes each, 8-20 per event, decided in the final 30 seconds before each one

#### Playout, master control and presentation

Run the channel around your programme: junctions, idents, promos, continuity voice, subtitles, audio description, regional opt-outs, and the schedule that your over-run is about to destroy.

- **Who.** Transmission Controller, Master Control Operator, Continuity Announcer
- **Decides.** Let the programme over-run and slide the schedule, or crash the next programme; drop a promo; shorten the junction; opt the regions out early; join the next event in progress.
- **Engine.** A channel schedule running as a second, independent timeline alongside the gallery. Over-running has modelled downstream cost: the next programme starts late, the late bulletin's junction collapses, a regional opt misses its window entirely.
- **Takes.** Continuous, 24 hours a day, independent of any single show

#### Feed distribution and unilateral service

Send out what other people are paying for: world feed with international sound, clean feed with no graphics, dirty feed with them, iso feeds, and unilateral return circuits into each rights holder's country.

- **Who.** Feeds Coordinator, Host Broadcaster Liaison, Transmission Manager
- **Decides.** How many outgoing feeds and what is on each; which graphics are burned in and which are supplied as a separate layer; what latency each taker will accept; who gets the tight iso and who does not.
- **Engine.** Outgoing feeds are distinct products carrying different graphic and audio layers on separate circuits. A domestic-only graphic appearing on the world feed is a contract breach with named consequences for dozens of downstream broadcasters at once.
- **Takes.** Configured at rig; policed throughout transmission

#### Redundancy and failover drill

Build a second of everything that matters — dual power feeds, UPS, a mirrored switcher or spare M/E, a hot spare replay server, a diverse transmission path — and rehearse the switch until the crew can do it in the dark.

- **Who.** Engineer in Charge, Technical Manager
- **Decides.** Which single points of failure to buy out and which to accept; hot standby or cold; who is authorised to declare a failover; what the fallback picture and the apology caption are.
- **Engine.** Every device carries a failure hazard per hour; every failover carries a switch time and an on-air visibility. Redundancy is paid for at booking, before you know whether you needed it. That is the bet, and the engine must let the player lose it.
- **Takes.** Designed at spec; drilled on rig day; used once in a career and that is the career

#### Contingency and standby material

Prepare for the thing you are covering simply stopping: rain, injury, lightning, evacuation, a filibuster, an empty red carpet, an unexplained twenty-minute hold. Build standby VTs, standby interviews, a standby studio and a standby script.

- **Who.** Executive Producer, Producer
- **Decides.** How much standby material to build; who the standby presenter is; the fill order; what triggers the switch to contingency; who is allowed to declare it.
- **Engine.** A stack of standby assets with durations and shelf lives, plus trigger conditions living in the event model. The engine must be able to have the event stop for an unbounded time while the programme is still obliged to exist and fill.
- **Takes.** Days of preparation for material usually never used; the decision itself takes ten seconds

#### Obituary / RIP procedure

The standing plan for the death of a monarch, head of state or major figure: prepared obituary packages maintained for years, a schedule collapse, black ties on the desk, music policy changed across every station, a defined authorisation chain.

- **Who.** Executive Producer, Head of News, Transmission Controller
- **Decides.** Whether and when to trigger; what level (full schedule collapse versus a special report); who confirms the death and on what sourcing; what is abandoned mid-transmission.
- **Engine.** A long-lived asset set refreshed over years plus a formal authorisation chain. Triggering has enormous, irreversible, channel-wide consequences — and triggering it wrongly, on a bad source, is the worst single outcome in the game.
- **Takes.** Obituaries maintained and updated for years; execution begins within 60 seconds of confirmation

#### Breaking news interrupt

Break into the schedule: get a presenter to a desk, get a camera hot, get a caption up, get a correspondent on a phone line if that is all there is, and hold air with almost nothing while the story is still being confirmed.

- **Who.** News Editor, Gallery Producer, Director
- **Decides.** Whether to break in at all and how fast; what you are prepared to say on one source; whether to stay on a live picture of nothing happening; when to hand back to the schedule.
